Within five years he repaid construction loans and added a four-bay, three-story rear courtyard: ground floor for wine-making and ham curing, upper floors for Fu-Lu-Shou-Xi luxury rooms with modern furniture for Shanghai and Shaoxing guests. In 1920 he bought land across from Jingtang, building a six-bay two-story building (ground floor rented as a grocery, upper floor guest rooms). In the early 1930s he added a three-bay, four-story exquisitely carved Western-style building north of the old inn. Cheng Renchang ultimately had 70+ rooms and 250+ beds—the street's largest inn. In peak season, two per bed plus floor mats in halls and courtyards accommodated 700+ guests. Its most famous guest was Chiang Kai-shek's first wife Mao Fumei, who arrived in two new cars on the 20th day of the 2nd lunar month 1937 to fulfill a vow at Fangyan. In the mid-1930s, Zhaoye's sons split the inn: eldest Cheng Xuanhu inherited the new four-story building and Fu-Lu-Shou-Xi courtyard as Xin Renchang; second son Cheng Xuanjie managed the old inn and annexes as Cheng Renchang Longji (Lao Renchang).

Meanwhile, Cheng Lelai's sons opened inns. In 1864, third son Cheng Zhaofu (1857-1939) started Cheng Zhenxing Restaurant with 500 copper coins from his father. Sixteen years later in 1880, he built a 16-bay two-story siheyuan outside the village gate at Lion Mountain's east foot, switching to inn business. Over 60+ years, Cheng Zhenxing expanded four times: in 1909 an identical mansion north of the original; in 1924 an ancestral hall west of the street between the two mansions and Cheng Renchang; then a two-story six-bay Western-style building south of the hall; in 1942 (three years after Zhaofu's death), his sons Xuanmei and Xuanji commissioned a Hangzhou construction firm to build a seven-bay three-story Western-style building south of the existing one. Cheng Zhenxing became a super-large luxury inn with three courtyard complexes, two independent buildings, nearly 130 rooms, and 350+ beds. Second brother Cheng Zhaofeng (1855-1928) built a similar courtyard north of the second mansion as Cheng Chengchang. Sixth brother Cheng Zhaogong ran Cheng Longchang Inn in Chenglu Village south of Songmen Bridge. In 1943 Xuanmei died; two years later his son Cheng Chongxue split from uncle Xuanji: Chongxue got the second mansion and Western building as Xin Zhenxing; Xuanji kept the first mansion and ancestral hall as Lao Zhenxing.

Besides these two families, scattered inns appeared in the late 19th and early 20th century: from south to north—Ma Dahe at the street corner north of Huilong Bridge; Cheng Meiyi northwest of Degeng Residence; Cheng Yifeng Yaji and Cheng Yifeng Laodian between the street and Fangyan Stream at Centipede Mountain's west foot; Cheng Dachang at Sanjiantou gully mouth; and Cheng Baochang, Cheng Qianchang, Cheng Hongchang near Songmen Bridge. On Fangyan Mountain's Heavenly Street, small inns opened—owned by Wenlou Village Cheng descendants. At peak there were 10 small inns (Guangxu era onward), each with only 3-4 rooms. Unlike street inns, their names avoided Cheng and words like Zhenxing or Longchang, instead using Daoist allusions: Sanxing Lou, Yuhua Lou, Tianmen Dian, Hulu Zhaopai.

Competition Culture: Yu Dafu (1895-1945) described in his 1933 Fangyan Jijing how scholars in long gowns would accost travelers in Jinhua hotels, asking if they were going to Fangyan, how many times, and where they stayed before—then presenting a business card for first-timers. These were fangtou (room-heads), inn agents recruiting customers. Though not the 3,000-5,000 Yu mentioned, 300-500 likely existed. Cheng Longchang's grandson-in-law Ying Yexiang recalled Cheng Zhenxing sending 200-300 agents before each pilgrimage season; the gazetteer records Cheng Renchang having 70-80 agents. Agents traveled over a month early to Shanghai, Hangzhou, Ningbo, Wenzhou, Tiantai, Jinhua, Dongyang, and Yiwu, needing local dialect skills. Top agents like Cheng Shuijing (Zhaofu's sixth brother's son, multilingual) earned great respect and even influenced management. Corresponding xiangtou (incense-heads) organized pilgrims locally, writing inns 1-2 months ahead with dates and numbers, often noting rain-or-shine to show piety. Agents brought gifts and distributed red cloth strips with inn names tied to luggage.

Before engines, pilgrims walked or took boats to Yongkang/Dongyang/Yiwu then walked. Sedan-chair businesses sprang up in Yongkang, Dongyang, Yiwu, and Zhuji—mentioned in the late Qing novel Kuocang Shan Enchou Ji. In January 1934 (Republic 23), the Hangjiang Railway opened with stations at Jinhua, Yiwu, and Zhuji; the same year Fangyan Highway reached the Cheng Ancestral Hall north of Chenglu. Inns coordinated with sedan-chair businesses and stationed agents at Yongkang and Fangyan stations to greet luggage bearing their red strips.

Competition was fierce, especially between Cheng Zhenxing and Cheng Renchang. Though side by side, Cheng Renchang's northern position gave it an advantage—guests heading for Cheng Zhenxing passed its door first and were often intercepted. Cheng Zhenxing agents personally escorted wealthy groups (especially Shaoxing customers) all the way back; for ordinary guests they relied on station agents grabbing red-striped luggage. When station agents failed, first-time pilgrims walking past Cheng Renchang might be pulled in. Cheng Zhaofu strategized with sixth brother Cheng Zhaogong: Zhaofu provided timber and wages to build an inn in Chenglu at the street's north end; once repaid, it belonged to Zhaogong. Named Cheng Longchang, it displayed the Cheng Zhenxing sign at its entrance, directing guests a li south to Cheng Zhenxing. Ying Yexiang still preserves both signs.

Even brothers competed. Ying Yexiang recalled agent Cheng Shuijing proposing a merchants' association with rules: no stealing guests whose luggage bore another inn's red strip; no approaching guests once they reached an inn's door or stepped inside; agents stood at fixed positions. But without enforcement power, associations formed several times and always became toothless.

Ultimately, service and pricing determined success. Most pilgrims stayed one night with two meals: arrive afternoon, snack, climb Fangyan, leave after breakfast; or arrive evening, climb after breakfast, leave after lunch. On arrival, staff stored luggage, seated guests in the hall, served sugar tea, pastries, lotus root starch, and vegetarian noodles. Before climbing Fangyan, dinner was vegetarian for piety; after visiting Lord Hu, the hall displayed two red candles and lavish meat-and-chicken dishes—vegetables abundant to the extreme. Evenings brought small singing troupes performing Wuju opera excerpts like Baishou Tu, Longhu Dou, Zhang Taishi Huichao. Cheng Renchang had three service tiers differing 20-30 fold in price, yet even third-tier guests drank and ate freely with a whole chicken. Cheng Zhenxing, favored by wealthy Shanghai/Suzhou/Hangzhou/Shaoxing guests, served late-night longan and lychee soup, customized meals, and departure gifts: ham, flower umbrellas, tea, lotus root starch, Fangyan Guide, and Fangyan maps. Extra services included selling incense, renting three-animal sacrifices, carrying sacrifices up and down the mountain, and guiding first-time pilgrims.

Peak seasons were the 1st, 2nd, 8th, 9th, and 10th lunar months. New Year's Eve locals raced to burn the first incense at Fangyan. The 25th of the 2nd month was considered Lord Hu's birthday, with two days and three nights of opera at Guangci Temple. From the 9th of the 8th month to the 14th of the 9th month was the month-long Hu Gong Temple Fair—all 72 Yongkang townships came to perform arhat dances, with peaks on the 13th of the 8th month and Double Ninth. The Kuchuan Hu Genealogy records the 13th of the 8th month as Hu Ze's birthday; locals considered it the day Lord Hu reviewed arhat troupes or was deified. Daily crowds exceeded 10,000. Post-farm-work season brought out-of-town pilgrims, making inns overflow. On Mid-Autumn (15th of 8th month), inn owners fed all staff to foster unity for the busy month. A folk song warned: Don't marry your daughter to Yanxia Street, eighth and ninth months she's just a signboard; rub your eyes, parents can't even drag her away. Staff were mostly temporary, paid daily with modest wages but generous tips distributed by the reward-diligence-punish-laziness principle (owner excluded)—considered a good job despite hardship. Large inns like Cheng Zhenxing hired 30-40 to 70-80 seasonal staff.

Inn jargon (hanghua) was distinctive—called the speech of those who eat Lord Hu, understood by general merchandise and incense sellers too. It avoided embarrassing guests when inns borrowed tofu, eggs, bed boards, or bedding. Vivid substitutions: fish = water-shuttle, noodles = long, soy sauce = red water. Character splitting: rice = eight-wood, fire = two-dot-person. Some jargon persists today.

Wartime Provincial Government: On July 7, 1937, the Marco Polo Bridge Incident; July 29 Beijing fell; August 13 Battle of Shanghai; November 12 Shanghai fell; November 5 Jiaxing/Huzhou lost, Hangzhou critical—Nationalist Zhejiang provincial government moved to Jinhua. Early December, decision to move to Fangyan. December 24 Hangzhou fell. On January 1, 1938, the government began operating in Shoushan Valley north of the old street.

With highways occupied and the Hangjiang Railway destroyed by Nationalist forces, pilgrims declined, but the street welcomed cultured, well-dressed urbanites with families. Inns became procuratorates, civil affairs departments, accounting offices. Fangyan's terrain and superior lodging made it the wartime capital.

The government operated from January 1, 1938 to May 1941 (moved to Songyang), returned August 1941, moved again to Songyang in May 1942, then to Yunhe in August 1942 until victory—total 4 years 5 months. It held two provincial administrative conferences, one police conference, one land administration conference, plus regular committee meetings, party-government joint meetings, capital-fall commemorations, mobilization months, and national holidays. In early April 1939, Zhou Enlai (as Deputy Director of the Military Committee Political Department) visited Fangyan and planted two parasol trees with Governor Huang Shaohong in Shoushan Valley. On July 7, 1939, the government built a War Martyrs Monument in front of Taohua Peak, holding annual commemorative rallies.

Accompanying the government were 20-30 parallel agencies, 20+ subsidiary departments, and 20+ organizations—many scattered on the old street or in temporary buildings along Wufeng Road. Civil servants, families, and refugees rented inns and homes long-term. Elders recall nearly every house had rooms for rent; private homes used second-floor storage space, while inns kept ground-floor rooms for occasional pilgrims and rented upper floors. The north end and Chenglu Village, nearest Shoushan Valley, were especially crowded.

The street's middle section concentrated civil servants; the south section housed cultural figures. Zhejiang High Court and its procuratorate stood near Huilong Bridge; Court President Zheng Wenli, Chief Prosecutor Wang Bingyi (now 158 Yanshang Street), and Accounting Bureau Chief Chen Baolin (now 138 Yanshang Street) lived there. Elder Cheng Shouyue west of Huilong Bridge recalled his 11-room house sheltered several doctors.

The government's presence boosted the economy and culture. Though rents were low or unpaid, empty rooms earned something; stable government income was a luxury in wartime. Without these urbanites, the street's economy might have collapsed. Like the Southern Song move to Hangzhou bringing officials, scholars, and northern technology, the Zhejiang government's refuge brought celebrities and turned Yongkang into a provincial logistics and information hub. With the Zhejiang-Jiangxi Railway and Qiantang River route cut, goods went via Wenzhou by water to Lishui (handled by guotang hang transport agents), then 75 km to Yongkang by handcart teams—each cart max 225 kg, 6 days round trip. Much material reached the old street valley. The Nationalist government built a gravel road connecting Chenglu and Nanyan at Fangyan's foot, which half a century later replaced the old street as the main pilgrim route.

Urban civil servants brought city culture grafted onto local tradition: Peking opera in ancestral halls, Western-style weddings—rare in other Jiangnan villages. Chen Zhihua (who came with his father) recalled wandering artists performing Shaoxing opera on the open ground before Cheng Chengchang Inn; the government military band sometimes performed in Cheng Zhaofu Hall. Elder Cheng Jufang recalled silent films occasionally shown at the Sanjiantou square; Governor Huang Shaohong would lead a gong-and-drum procession from Shoushan Valley to watch and back.

Outsiders integrated: police maintained order on Fangyan during pilgrim surges; military doctors treated locals; schools enrolled both officials' children and Cheng descendants. Former Civil Affairs Department Director Ruan Yicheng wrote of his Fangyan home: bamboo fences and thatched huts, pine forests, a stream with a wooden bridge—my happiest rural dwelling, locals call it Back Mountain. In the 7th lunar month 1940, my third son was born here, named Dafang to mark his birth at Fangyan.

Without Shoushan Valley's air-raid protection and the unreachable Wufeng Academy caves, the government would not have chosen Fangyan. Without the urbanites, wartime economic damage would have been severe. Blessed perhaps by Guangci Temple and Lord Hu, the street suffered no damage and actually grew economically. Post-war inns peaked: 11 new inns opened in 1944-1946 alone (Cheng Baochang, Cheng Qianchang, Cheng Zhengchang, Cheng Zhongxing, Cheng Zhengxing, etc.), mostly small husband-wife operations in two-story street buildings, with income supplemented by farming or other trades in off-season.

Post-Liberation Changes: The street's inns mirrored major historical events in the first 30 years of New China. Locals remembered the Nationalist government as approachable urbanites who built roads, installed streetlights, and moved cesspits—many villagers regretted their departure. They shared ancestor Cheng Quan's anxiety about officials' precarious lives in a new dynasty. Early liberation suppressed religious activity; inns closed for lack of pilgrims; land had long been insufficient; elders recall the hardest times, with young people forced to migrate for work.

In the early 1950s, land reform abolished landlord land ownership and redistributed to poor peasants. The Land Law protected property directly used for industry and commerce. The valley's tiny fields were measured in ba (70 ba = one mu ≈ 667 sq m; one ba ≈ 9.5 sq m, a 3m square)—a Degeng Residence side room exceeded two ba. Cheng Chongshun near Songmen Bridge, though not wealthy compared to shop owners, held 800 ba; teaching away from home with young children meant 300 ba rented out and 500 ba farmed by hired labor—making him a top rich peasant, losing house and land. Large inn families (Cheng Longchang, Xin Longxing, Lao Longxing) who farmed themselves were classified as middle peasants. Except for super-large Cheng Zhenxing and Cheng Renchang, most inns were part-time (rooms used for storage or empty in off-season), so their property was not protected as commercial. Insufficient labor with hired farmhands meant landlord or rich-peasant classification even for inn property—Cheng Hongchang Inn at the north end was divided. Conversely, Xin Zhenxing, Cheng Zhenxing, Xin Longxing, and Cheng Renchang were classified as industry-and-commerce-plus-landlord: their land was divided but inns protected, and they became protected businesspeople rather than bearing landlord labels. In 1951, five inns (Xin Longxing, Cheng Renchang, Xin Zhenxing, Cheng Zhenxing, Ying Baoxing in West Village) reopened.

In 1956, socialist transformation of private industry and commerce began via peaceful buyout and joint state-private operation. Conditions: capital over 3,000 yuan, 5+ employees, voluntary application. For inn owners, joint operation seemed the only path—buildings sold to the state at appraised value with quarterly payments over years; private personnel could stay as regular workers on fixed wages but no longer make decisions. In April 1957, Cheng Renchang, Xin Zhenxing, Cheng Zhenxing, Ying Baoxing, and Guangci Temple Guesthouse merged into the joint state-private Fangyan Hotel. Xin Longxing, with too few usable rooms, closed. The characters Fangyan Hotel (half-meter tall) remain on Cheng Zhenxing and Cheng Renchang exterior walls.

In 1958, the Great Leap Forward and people's communes brought communal dining halls—Cheng Zhengwei Ham Shop's spacious courtyard with food-processing facilities became the village canteen. Villagers rushed to make steel, taking blue bricks from courtyard walls; Lord Hu was set aside. Fangyan Hotel stayed open but served business travelers, meeting attendees, and factory-organized tour groups rather than pilgrims.

Then came three years of economic hardship. Even the Jiangnan fish-and-rice region suffered severe famine. Yongkang fared better due to handicrafts, yet of 300,000 people, roughly 100,000 migrated along the Zhejiang-Jiangxi Railway to Jiangxi (an old revolutionary base with better conditions). Blood-related Cheng descendants quarreled over grain sharing after hail damage; the street split at Lao Longxing Inn into Yanshang Brigade and Yanxia Brigade. Hardship revived devotion to Lord Hu atop Fangyan, and nearby pilgrims reappeared.

The brief 1961-1962 improvement ended with the 1963 Four Cleanups Movement (clean accounts, warehouses, work-points, property). A work team from the county stayed in nearby Paixi; in Cheng descendants' words, their goal was to make Lord Hu stink. Officials held meetings in Cheng Zhaofu Hall (where opera once entertained pilgrims), criticizing former Xin Renchang and Cheng Zhongxing inn owners. Inns cooled again; private personnel transferred to state units like supply and marketing cooperatives.

In May 1966, the Cultural Revolution began. By late August, most Guangci Temple buildings including Heling Shrine were demolished. The Pingfeng Pavilion, slated for demolition by Fangyan Commune, was preserved on a pretext—used as a brigade meeting hall, warehouse, post and telecommunications school, and 602 Factory. For a decade, religion and culture halted; remaining private staff at Fangyan Hotel were replaced; Cheng Zhaofu Hall became the village meeting place. Inn furniture was removed or sold; the hotel became an instrument factory and warehouse.

Two years after the Cultural Revolution, on the 1st day of the 8th lunar month 1978, two or three women secretly carried a newly sculpted Hu Ze statue up the mountain to Pingfeng Pavilion. The next year, Guangci Temple welcomed its first abbot in over a decade—Yunming. Suppressed people poured in to worship. Though not wealthy, they donated generously, hoping to rebuild Guangci and secure lasting peace. Unlike before, some returning pilgrims skipped the three-li old street, traveling by tractor or bus along the gravel road to the pass west of Huilong Bridge. Reform policies affirmed farmers' rights to private plots and livestock, encouraging sideline businesses. Confident Yanxia farmers opened small restaurants on roadside private plots; business boomed, evolving from temporary sheds to spacious brick houses兼营 inns. In 1985 the gravel road became a dozen-meter concrete road; more inns, restaurants, and shops opened. By the mid-1990s, all inns had new facades on the new street—ground floors fully open for dining and parking, upper floors half with bathrooms and half usable as private dining rooms or budget guest rooms. Everything seemed changed, yet the industry culture endured: one night two meals at high/middle/low tiers; a pilgrim spends one-fifth of the Fangyan entrance ticket for lodging and meals; jargon like water-shuttle and pointy-mouth persists.

Fangyan Merchandise: Tourism requires distinctive souvenirs. Republican-era Yongkang Gazetteer records over 100 shops on Yanxia Street and 20+ on Fangyan Mountain peak, mostly selling Beijing-style goods and groceries. After reform, Fangyan Town had 280+ shops and 70+ stalls, mostly traditional local crafts called Fangyan goods: children's toys, paper flowers, and umbrellas.

Paper Flowers: The most distinctive Fangyan product. Origins unknown, but the 1991 Yongkang Gazetteer records flourishing production in the Qianlong era (1736-1795). Paper in bright red, light red, pink, yellow, and blue, with varied cutting, layering, and color-overprinting, produces camellia, peony, chrysanthemum, rose, wild rose, azalea, hibiscus, and violet varieties. On the street once called Flower Corridor at Fangyan's foot, only one paper-flower stall remains, run by elderly Bai Longlong. Not a local, she moved from Lechang, Guangdong with her father before liberation, married into the Cheng family, and lives in Yanshang Village. She recalled learning paper-flower making around age 30 from a wanderer called Old Yang from Jinyun, Lishui, who lived in Luohan Cave halfway up Fangyan—women traded meals for lessons in peony-making, then developed variations.

Production has three steps: chiseling (a crescent-bladed small chisel outlines petals/leaves on stacks of colored paper), dyeing (subtle color at petal tips for depth and vividness—the most critical and time-consuming step), and assembly. Bai Longlong brings dyed paper stacks in shoe-box-sized boxes with wire and paste up the mountain, making a lifelike flower in minutes. After the Cultural Revolution, as pilgrims increased, business boomed—on the less-than-100-meter street, 70-80 stalls with 50-60 paper-flower sellers; pilgrims called them Lord Hu Flowers. In off-season, women took dyed paper to other provinces—Bai went to Fujian, Jiangxi, Hunan, with best business in Hubei and Guangdong, staying about a month until materials ran out. At five fen per bunch, rising train fares ended the practice. In recent decades, rising Fangyan ticket prices and plastic alternatives have reduced sellers, but village women (including Bai's daughter and daughter-in-law) still make paper flowers for festivals, dragon lanterns, or occasional sale.

Toys: Fangyan toys have a long history, diverse types, and fine craftsmanship—cute, movable, and sound-making. Examples: bamboo xiao flutes and jointed dragons; clay crowing roosters; wooden chick-pecking-rice toys and small swords; cast metal bells and cymbals. Most famous are whistles of various materials: common cast tinplate whistles (loud); bamboo-wood trumpets (thumb-sized, rough sound, bamboo mouthpiece in a red-lacquered wooden cylinder); clay whistles shaped as fish or chicks (blown at the animal's mouth, vibrating hollow interior); and the distinctive clay leather-tiger (two clay discs painted as tiger head and tail, connected by crinkled paper, sounding as hands compress and release). Traditional toys face competition from plastic and electric toys.

Umbrellas: Fangyan oil-paper flower umbrellas are widely known. Three styles: large for the elderly; patterned for adults (with pretty flower umbrellas for young women); small for children with a whistle-shaped handle. Production involves dozens of steps. Mao bamboo for frames comes from Zhiying (thicker walls): soaked, dried, cut into sections, strung with hair-thread into ribs, edges wrapped with cotton yarn for durability. Cotton paper for canopies comes from Zhuji and Yiwu: cut into triangles, sized in persimmon oil. Persimmons are bought unripe from Xinlou in May-June, crushed, seeded, and stored in water-filled vats. Sized paper is mounted on ribs, dried, then painted with flowers, birds, mountains, and water. Elder Cheng Zhenfeng (b. 1939, Chong generation, 21st) said the hardest step is oiling—a tung oil and green oil mixture applied and dried over open flame; heat control is critical (too old = brittle, too tender = won't open). Near Huilong Bridge at the street's south end, several umbrella shops once operated. Elder Cheng Shouyue's family made and sold umbrellas before liberation; in peak season demand exceeded supply, so they bought Wenzhou umbrellas to resell to Wenzhou pilgrims. As steel-frame cloth and plastic umbrellas replaced paper ones, he switched to lacquer work. Cheng Zhenfeng's grandfather and father made paper umbrellas at the street's end; one year on the 9th day of the 9th lunar month they sold over 1,900 umbrellas.

Jinhua Ham: Another famous specialty. Historically Yongkang, Yiwu, Dongyang, Pujiang, and Jinhua were all under Jinhua Prefecture, so ham from these areas is called Jinhua Ham—Yongkang is a major producer. Folk curing dates to the Tang dynasty; Chen Cangqi's 713-742 Bencao Shiyi notes ham from Jinhua is best. By Southern Song, Jinhua Ham was a tribute item. Before liberation, every household raised pigs, slaughtered after the 15th of the 12th lunar month. Most families sold legs to ham merchants, keeping heads and offal for New Year; only the wealthy cured legs for their own use and made sausages from intestines. Winter-cured ham (dongtui) tastes better and stores longer than spring-cured; street ham used hind legs (front legs too fatty). Process: rub legs with salt, cure in large vats for over ten days each time, three rounds; steam one night; sun-dry three days; hang in corridors for over half a month. Cured ham keeps two years.

Several ham shops operated on the street, most famously Cheng Zhengwei Ham Shop at Shanghu Gully mouth. Elder Cheng Shengyan (b. 1935, Sheng generation, 22nd) is a descendant. Great-grandfather Cheng Zhengwei built a two-courtyard east-west mansion adjacent to the street's west side in the mid-19th century, with a cobblestone eight-trigram pattern in the front courtyard. Grandfather Cheng Xuanyao expanded with a north-south two-courtyard building against the west wall. The old house, exquisitely carved and once a protected heritage site, burned down. The ham shop closed with Xuanyao's death before liberation, but the building bears witness: corridor and hall beams are extra thick and dense, with wooden pegs protruding every four inches—Cheng Shengyan explains these hung ham; formerly corridors, halls, front and rear courtyards held over a thousand hams. Because ham business is seasonal like inns, large inns cured ham in off-season—Cheng Renchang is an example. According to Yongkang Famous Shops and Factories, after autumn pilgrimage season, the inn prepared up to 4,000 hams yearly under the brand Xuefang Renchang Longji, numbered by the eight characters Tian-Di-Xuan-Huang, Yu-Zhou-Hong-Huang, and shipped to Hangzhou. Smaller shops like Cheng Zhenchang survived competition by diversifying: one side sold Fangyan goods and Qingtian stone carvings, the other homemade ham.

第四章 岩下老街的建筑和空间
Chapter 4: Architecture and Space of Yanxia Old Street
岩下老街的空间格局与商业形态
Spatial Layout and Commercial Form of Yanxia Old Street
一、老街 | The Old Street

要点 Highlights

  • 全长约三里，宽仅二三米，青石板铺路，鹅卵石镶嵌
  • 松门桥（橙麓村与岩下村村界）、回龙桥（光绪二十三年1897年建，民国29年1940年重建）
  • 旅店鳞次栉比：程同昌、程元昌、程洪昌、程隆昌、程乾昌、程大昌、程仁昌、新仁昌、程振兴、新振兴、程成昌、新隆兴、义丰雅记、义丰老店、程永成、程阮阮、老隆兴
  • 周氏宗祠及路亭横跨老街
  • 民国36年（1947年）重阳初八夜香客盛况
  • 房头接客制度、香头组织、红布条系行李
  • 行话：鱼=水梭、面条=长、酱油=红水、米=八、木火=二点人

岩下老街全长约三里，宽度仅二三米，青石板铺路，鹅卵石镶嵌。老街北头第一段属橙麓村境内，在大溪桥附近拐了两拐，路面临水一侧有连排石栏杆，刻有香客捐造字样，其中一个为光绪四年（1878年）建造。从大溪桥到松门桥，右手一侧肩并肩坐落着程同昌、程元昌、程洪昌、程隆昌和程乾昌等一连串旅店。松门桥是长约10米、宽约1.5米的石板桥，也是橙麓村和岩下村的村界。过了松门桥，老街稍适宽阔，路边店面变得稀疏，夹道而坐的是卖柑橘、菱角、小五金和熏豆腐干的小商贩，以及乞丐。乞丐嘴里哼唱着上岩烧香、下岩修福、有福不修、回家火烧屋等小曲儿。这一段老街西边是突兀陡峭的狮子山，东边是一片田地，山谷北侧是寿山坑坑口。街两边除程大昌一家外，余下多是没有内院的临街店面。

继续往前，老街上横跨有两个路亭，路亭之间大门紧锁，门上写着周氏宗祠四个字。据说是草墅周氏的后代，几百年前在这里居住过很短时间，后来不知什么原因搬走了，却留下了这个祠堂和祠堂后面山坡上的祖坟。路亭一侧坐着从远道赶来的艺人，趁着阴凉填饱肚子后打个盹儿，好在当晚唱遍各家旅店。由此向南是老街最热闹的段落，两三米宽的街道两旁店家林立，其中程仁昌、新仁昌、程振兴和新振兴是超大型豪华旅店，程成昌和新隆兴规模也不小。人们摩肩接踵簇拥在高墙林立、狭窄的街道上。透过虚掩的大门向旅店院里张望，院子角落里摆着好几摞凳子，旁边竖着许多一米多宽的木板，地上堆了许多稻草，是给打地铺的客人准备的。这段老街末尾是两家几乎面对面的火腿店。

过了火腿店，视野再次开阔。眼前夹在方岩山和蜈蚣山之间的是一片绿油油的田地。田地之间靠蜈蚣山一侧，孤零零矗立着一幢连排大宅子，紧紧卡在老街和方岩溪之间。这幢连排宅子有三个院子，原属三兄弟，其中两家香火不盛，才有空房开旅店，分别为义丰雅记和义丰老店。义丰雅记有一百五十多年历史，内部建筑雕饰异常精美；义丰老店则是老街上历史最长的旅店之一，风风雨雨已将近百年。

老街最后一段是程氏迁到这里时最早的聚居地，建筑鳞次栉比，街道三步一曲、九步一折。旅店只有规模不大的程永成和程阮阮，以及老街上第一家旅店老隆兴。余下都是临街卖百货、食品或香烛的小店。香客们趁机在晚饭前采购当地特产：花花绿绿的雨伞、长长短短的蜡烛、以假乱真的纸花、会动的小鸡吃米、能出声的皮老虎、腌制好的整只火腿、各式各样的小五金及竹编制品。每走不远，还可以透过两边建筑之间的夹道看到后面的水塘，以及坐在水塘边洗衣服、唠家常的妇女们和追逐嬉戏的孩童。

老街尽端是回龙桥，一座小型石拱桥。桥栏杆上记载着它的历史：建于光绪二十三年（1897年），重建于民国29年（1940年）。回龙桥前面老街被一分为二，继续向南依照桥栏杆上所刻处州方岩大路通向处州；走回龙桥跨过方岩溪向西近百米，才是上方岩山的山路。至此，三里长的老街画上了句号。

— —

Yanxia Old Street stretches about three li (1.5 km) in length and is merely two to three meters wide, paved with bluestone slabs inlaid with cobblestones. The northern section falls within Chengli Village. Near Daxi Bridge the street bends twice, with a continuous stone railing on the waterside carved with donor inscriptions, one dated to the fourth year of Guangxu (1878). From Daxi Bridge to Songmen Bridge, a row of inns stands shoulder to shoulder on the right side: Chengtongchang, Chengyuanchang, Chenghongchang, Chenglongchang, and Chengqianchang. Songmen Bridge, a stone slab bridge about 10 meters long and 1.5 meters wide, marks the boundary between Chengli Village and Yanxia Village. Beyond it the street widens slightly and shops become sparser, with vendors selling citrus, water chestnuts, hardware, and smoked dried tofu, alongside beggars humming tunes such as 'Burn incense on the upper rock, cultivate blessings below; fail to cultivate blessings, and your home will burn.' To the west rises the precipitous Lion Mountain; to the east lie fields; to the north is the mouth of Shoushan Ravine.

Further along, two roadside pavilions straddle the street, with a locked gate between them bearing the inscription 'Zhou Family Ancestral Hall.' According to legend, descendants of the Caoshu Zhou clan lived here briefly centuries ago before moving away, leaving behind the hall and ancestral graves on the hillside behind. Traveling performers rest in the pavilions before performing at inns that evening. South of here is the busiest stretch, with two-to-three-meter-wide streets flanked by shops. The super-large luxury inns include Chengrenchang, Xinrenchang, Chengzhenxing, and Xinzhenxing; Chengchengchang and Xinlongxing are also substantial. Through half-open doors one glimpses courtyards stacked with stools, one-meter-wide wooden planks, and straw for guests sleeping on the floor. At the end of this stretch stand two ham shops facing each other.

Beyond the ham shops the view opens up again. Between Fangyan Mountain and Centipede Mountain lie green fields. Against Centipede Mountain stands a row of three large courtyards wedged between the old street and Fangyan Stream, originally belonging to three brothers. Two branches had no male heirs and converted rooms into inns: Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Laodian. Yifeng Yaji, over 150 years old, features exceptionally refined architectural ornamentation; Yifeng Laodian is one of the oldest inns on the street, weathering nearly a century.

The final section is the earliest settlement area of the Cheng clan, with densely packed buildings and a winding street. Inn operations are limited to smaller establishments like Chengyongcheng and Chengruanruan, plus Laolongxing—the first inn on the street. The rest are small shops selling general goods, food, and incense. Pilgrims stock up on local specialties before dinner: colorful umbrellas, candles of various lengths, lifelike paper flowers, mechanical 'chicks eating rice,' sounding 'paper tigers,' whole cured hams, hardware, and bamboo ware. Through gaps between buildings one glimpses ponds where women wash clothes and chat, and children play.

At the street's end stands Huilong Bridge, a small stone arch bridge built in the 23rd year of Guangxu (1897) and rebuilt in the 29th year of the Republic (1940). Beyond it the street splits: continuing south follows the 'Chuzhou-Fangyan Road' inscribed on the railing toward Chuzhou; crossing Huilong Bridge over Fangyan Stream and heading west for nearly 100 meters leads to the mountain path up Fangyan Mountain. Thus ends the three-li old street.

得耕居——早期住宅建筑
Degeng Residence — Early Domestic Architecture
二、住宅 | Residences

要点 Highlights

  • 岩下村程氏祖屋，现存建筑中历史最悠久
  • 七世祖程鑅（1474—1548年）建于十六世纪初
  • 乾隆年间大火，仅前厅和树德堂幸存（礼部侍郎程文德墨宝保佑）
  • 乾隆己酉年（1789年）重建
  • 面宽近28米，深约54米，前厅后堂两进大院，两侧各有厢房15间
  • 背山面水：蜈蚣山为靠山，狮子山为祖山，朱公山为案山
  • 半月形前塘，池底卵石镶嵌双龙戏珠图案，形成玉带环腰吉象
  • 重檐楼牌式门楼，门额砖刻得耕居，双狮抢球、麒麟、花鸟砖雕
  • 花厅（树德堂）、日月井水池上的两层高戏台、后堂
  • 程文德题树德堂横匾及栋宇初成家有庆，簪缨世显国同休楹联

得耕居是现存建筑中历史最悠久的一幢，被岩下村程氏尊为祖屋。得耕两字是为纪念岩下村程氏始祖程权（1333—1385年），以及他所建造的厅堂中有匾为得耕的住宅。这座住宅的原址就在得耕居门前水塘的东侧。得耕居最早由七世祖程鑅（1474—1548年）建于十六世纪初，但是清乾隆年间（1736—1795年）的一场大火烧毁了除前厅和树德堂以外的所有房屋。村里的人认为，前厅之所以能幸免于难，是因为有礼部侍郎程文德的墨宝悬挂于此。乾隆己酉年（1789年），得耕居得以重建，并保留至今。

得耕居坐落在上坑坑口内，老街西侧，朝向基本上是坐北朝南。俗话说山旺人丁水旺财，程鑅在选址和建造这座新宅的时候，为了程家日后能人丁兴旺、财源茂盛，不但充分考虑了周围的环境，还对不利的因素人为地做了相应的调整，使其具有背山面水的理想格局。得耕居以蜈蚣山为靠山，住宅的北墙几乎紧挨着其西端的第一个小山包。祖山是在蜈蚣山北面更为高大的狮子山。案山则是在它南面一百多米的朱公山。朱公山又叫前山头，是蜥蜴山的第一个小山包。在正对着得耕居的地方，山上原有一棵大柏树，柏树下原有供奉朱相公的本保庙。朱相公的原型为朱丹溪（1281—1358年），金华人，元代著名医学家，死后成为当地主要的民间神祇之一。

依照风水术的描述，玉带环腰是吉宅的一大特点，也称作腰带水，指有河流呈圆形、半圆形或弧形从建筑前流过。在得耕居对面，正好有下坑溪沿蜥蜴山北麓蜿蜒流过，但是这条溪水却在前山头脚下的一座院子前拐了两拐，沿着院墙继续向西，最终汇入坑口外的方岩溪，所以在得耕居前并没有形成玉带环腰的形态。从另一个角度来看，风水术还提到低一寸即为水，因此路也可以算作水。得耕居前的水池为半月形，这是村里唯一一个弧形的水池。如此一来，沿着水池南岸的路，得耕居就形成了玉带环腰的态势。

这个半月形的水池叫前塘，据《世远公宗谱》记载，池底有卵石镶嵌成的双龙戏珠图案。水池旁边原建有一亭，是程鑅晚年自在逍遥并宴请宾客的地方。因亭在方岩山的东面，他也因此得名为东亭居士。水池和建筑之间是用卵石作为铺地，宽敞的场坪。从水池和场坪到老街之间，原有一个花园，并有一条小径穿过花园与老街相连。据说花园里原本除了奇花异草之外，还摆放着被当地人称作基戈的形状各异的赏石。如今，在花园的旧址上，有几间两层高的一进房屋，从建筑样式推测，它们可能是在二十世纪初，由于旅店业的兴起，而先后盖起来的一些店面兼住宅。

得耕居面宽近28米，深约54米，包括前厅后堂两进大院，两侧各有厢房15间。从独松村留下来的几幢同时期的住宅来看，这种平面格局代表了当时住宅的一种典型布局形式。得耕居的正立面中间部分为砖砌，并雕刻精美的重檐楼牌式门楼，两侧辅以东西厢房素平的山墙。门楼主体结构是在乾隆己酉年（1789年）重修的。门楼的门额正中砖刻得耕居，并在两侧辅以麒麟，下方衬以花鸟，门匾上还有雕刻精美的双狮抢球。此外，在两边的墙上还有祥云、花鸟、鲤鱼、骏马、人物等精美砖雕。

第一进院是一个近方形的三合院，正房三开间，通常被称作花厅。花厅里原悬挂礼部侍郎程文德的墨宝，即树德堂横匾和栋宇初成家有庆，簪缨世显国同休的楹联。村民们认为，正是因为有程文德的保佑，花厅在乾隆年间的大火中才免遭一劫。花厅正面全部为这扇门，需要时可以全部开敞，厅内空间高大通畅。背面通常只有在明间设门，在门前、后金柱位置设屏风，又称太师壁，用以遮挡视线。屏风后有一组楼梯通向花厅后面两层高的戏台。前院两侧各有三间厢房，再加上花厅两侧各有两间，共五间。厢房和花厅山墙之间有走廊，通往后院。后院是一个长方形的院落：花厅的后面是一个两层高戏台，架空在一个被称为日月井的水池之上；与戏台相对的是三开间的后堂；院落两边各有厢房十间。后堂分上下两层，一层明间属公有，直接向院落开敞，次间和厢房一样属于居住空间。在明间内靠后墙的位置，有楼梯通往二层。二层三间连通，属家族公有空间。平日里，二层和一层明间一起用来布置红白喜事，在演戏的时候则是看戏人的天地。

与得耕居同一时期盖的院落，在平面布局上都有一个特点：围合院落的几排房并没有连在一起，而是被山墙分成一段一段的，山墙之间还有走道通向外面。以得耕居为例，在花厅每侧的厢房之间，各有一条一米多宽的东西向走道，外侧设门，通向院外。追其原因可能有三点，一是因为院落太大，用山墙分开力求在失火时把损失减到最小；二是对于这种大型的院落，如果分开分段建造，在技术上也会相对容易；三是对于共同居住在这里的众多家庭，多设几个门，也方便进出。

作为岩下村现存历史最悠久、面积最大的住宅，得耕居还兼有祭祀的功能，同时也是村里文娱活动的中心。在正月里，凡是家里没有厅堂的族人，就会在花厅内侧的山墙上悬挂自己太公太婆的画像。宅前的空场地还是每年闹龙灯的重要空间。后院的戏台，每年在除夕或正月的时候会演一次平安戏。平日里，碰上村里有钱的人家做寿，就会有戏班在这里演三天三夜的戏。即使在家家有电视的年代，演戏仍旧是仅次于闹龙灯和赶庙会之外，村里最热闹的事了。

— —

Degeng Residence is the oldest surviving building in Yanxia Village, revered by the Cheng clan as their ancestral home. The name 'Degeng' (Cultivating the Land) commemorates Cheng Quan (1333–1385), the founding ancestor of the Yanxia Cheng clan, and the hall bearing that name. The original site lay east of the pond in front of the current residence. It was first built in the early 16th century by the seventh-generation ancestor Cheng Heng (1474–1548). A fire during the Qianlong period (1736–1795) destroyed everything except the front hall and the Shude Hall, which villagers believed survived because of the calligraphy by Cheng Wende, Vice Minister of Rites, hanging there. It was rebuilt in the 54th year of Qianlong (1789) and has been preserved to this day.

Degeng Residence sits at the mouth of Shangkeng Ravine, west of the old street, facing roughly south. Following the geomantic principle that 'mountains foster descendants and water fosters wealth,' Cheng Heng carefully selected and adjusted the site to achieve the ideal configuration of 'backing onto mountains and facing water.' Centipede Mountain serves as the backing mountain, with the residence's north wall nearly touching its westernmost hillock. The ancestral mountain is the taller Lion Mountain to the north of Centipede Mountain. The approach mountain (anshan) is Zhu Gong Mountain, 100 meters to the south, also called Qianshantou—the first hillock of Lizard Mountain. Directly opposite the residence once stood a large cypress tree, beneath which was a local temple dedicated to Lord Zhu, identified with Zhu Danxi (1281–1358), a famous Yuan dynasty physician from Jinhua who became a local deity after death.

According to geomancy, a 'jade belt around the waist'—a river curving in a circle, semicircle, or arc in front of a building—is a hallmark of an auspicious home. Although Xiakeng Stream winds along the northern foot of Lizard Mountain opposite Degeng, it bends twice before a courtyard at the foot of Qianshantou and flows west into Fangyan Stream, failing to form the desired curve. However, geomancy also holds that 'a drop of one inch counts as water,' so roads can serve as water. The half-moon pond in front of Degeng—the only curved pond in the village—combined with the road along its southern bank, creates the 'jade belt around the waist' configuration.

This half-moon pond is called Qiantang (Front Pond). According to the Shiyuan Gong Genealogy, its bottom features a 'twin dragons playing with a pearl' pattern inlaid with cobblestones. Beside the pond once stood a pavilion where Cheng Heng entertained guests in his later years; because it faced east toward Fangyan Mountain, he called himself the 'Scholar of the Eastern Pavilion.' Between the pond and the buildings was a spacious cobblestone courtyard. Between the courtyard and the old street was a garden with a path connecting to the street, said to contain exotic flowers and strangely shaped ornamental stones called 'jige.' Today, several two-story buildings from the early 20th century—likely shop-residences built during the inn boom—stand on the garden site.

Degeng Residence measures nearly 28 meters wide and about 54 meters deep, comprising two main courtyards (front hall and rear hall) with 15 wing rooms on each side. This layout represents a typical residential form of the period, as seen in contemporary residences in Dusong Village. The central part of the front facade is brick-built with an exquisitely carved double-eaved gatehouse, flanked by the plain gable walls of the east and west wings. The gatehouse was rebuilt in 1789. Its lintel bears the brick inscription 'Degeng Residence,' flanked by qilin and birds-and-flowers motifs, with a 'twin lions competing for a ball' carving above. The side walls feature refined brick carvings of auspicious clouds, birds, flowers, carp, horses, and human figures.

The first courtyard is a nearly square three-sided compound with a three-bay main hall called the Flower Hall (Hua Ting), which originally displayed Cheng Wende's calligraphy: the 'Shude Hall' horizontal plaque and the couplet 'When the building is first completed, the family celebrates; with official generations显耀, the nation shares in repose.' Villagers believe this blessing saved the hall from the Qianlong fire. The front of the Flower Hall consists entirely of removable doors, allowing full opening when needed, creating a tall, airy space. The rear typically has a door only in the central bay, with a screen wall (taishi bi) between the front and rear gold pillars to block the view. Behind the screen, a staircase leads to a two-story stage behind the Flower Hall. The front courtyard has three wing rooms on each side, plus two rooms on each side of the Flower Hall, totaling five. Corridors between the wings and the Flower Hall's gable walls lead to the rear courtyard. The rear courtyard is rectangular: behind the Flower Hall stands a two-story stage elevated over a pond called the Sun-Moon Well; opposite it is the three-bay rear hall; ten wing rooms line each side. The rear hall has two stories: the ground-floor central bay is communal space open to the courtyard, while side bays and wings are residential. A staircase at the back wall of the central bay leads to the second floor, where three bays connect as communal family space. On ordinary days, both floors host weddings and funerals; during performances, it becomes the audience area.

A characteristic of residences from this period is that the buildings enclosing the courtyard are not connected but divided by gable walls into segments, with passageways between them leading outside. In Degeng, between the wing rooms on each side of the Flower Hall, there is a one-meter-wide east-west passage with an exterior door. Three reasons account for this: first, dividing large courtyards with gable walls minimizes fire damage; second, segmented construction is technically easier for large compounds; third, multiple doors provide convenient access for the many families living together.

As the oldest and largest residence in Yanxia Village, Degeng also serves ritual functions and is the village's cultural center. During the first lunar month, clansmen without their own halls hang portraits of ancestors on the inner gable walls of the Flower Hall. The open space in front is the main venue for the annual dragon lantern festival. The rear stage hosts a 'peace play' every New Year's Eve or first lunar month. On birthdays of wealthy villagers, opera troupes perform for three days and three nights. Even in the age of television, opera performances remain the village's liveliest event after dragon lanterns and temple fairs.

程立重宅院——十八世纪中叶的多进院大型住宅
Cheng Lizhong Compound — Mid-18th Century Multi-Courtyard Residence
二、住宅 | Residences

要点 Highlights

  • 程立重（1736—1793年），字逢时，号毅庵，岩下程氏第十五世祖
  • 岩脚竖花厅，丈人倒贴银的故事
  • 院套院形式，建筑面积比两个得耕居加在一起还大
  • 屋里塘（澈月塘）和屋里井，深宅大院和财富的代名词
  • 八尺路小巷，菜地、花园
  • 三厅两堂的三进院落，月梁、卷龙图案梁托、卷草纹雀替
  • 后面两进院子解放后逐渐坍塌
  • 巨石和夯土垒砌的近一层高基础

程立重（1736—1793年），字逢时，号毅庵，是岩下程氏第十五世祖。程立重的祖父备受乡人尊敬，并拥有个人祠堂的作勳公；其父亲是作勳公的小儿子，经臣，即恭常房祖；程立重和他的三个哥哥又分为发、强、刚、毅四房。目前老街上程立重的后人最多，家谱上称这支为恭下毅常，他们还有自己的祠堂，即绍常祠堂。据说，这幢三厅两堂的宅院是程立重的丈人，在送女儿过门之前，派人来村里盖的。这段岩脚竖花厅，丈人倒贴银的故事，一直都是村里人茶余饭后的美谈。依照对程立重的年龄推算，这幢住宅的建造年代应该在十八世纪中叶。

程立重宅院位于下坑口的以南，蜥蜴山的西麓。在它建成之前，村里的住宅基本上都位于下坑口内，下坑溪和方岩溪交汇的三角河谷地段。这里地势较高又相对平坦，易于建造，住在这里既可以上山砍柴，又方便照料位于下坑里的田地，建筑内院还可以沐浴在早晨的阳光里。随着人口的不断增加，程氏后人不得不在其他地方建造新宅，程立重宅院就是建在下坑口以外的第一幢住宅。老街在出了下坑口以后，就紧紧地贴着几乎是拔地而起的蜥蜴山。这段蜥蜴山又被称为前山头，又因山上的朱公庙而得名为朱公山。程立重宅院大体上坐北朝南，它的东西院墙恰到好处地卡在南北走向的老街和方岩溪之间。院落的西南角还就着方岩溪的走势抹了一个角，做成了弧线形。

这座院落在平面布局上采用院套院形式，所以建筑面积比两个得耕居加在一起还要大许多。院墙里面不但有气势恢弘的三进宅院用于居住，还有菜地、花园、屋里塘和屋里井。由于老街和方岩溪在这里有将近一层的高差，所以院落内的居住部分位于地势较高的东侧临街，水塘和菜地等位于西侧近水。从方岩溪这边看去，整组建筑坐落在用巨石垒砌的高台之上，更显威严。

程立重宅院的主入口位于院落东墙偏南的位置，面向老街。进门以后是一条被称作八尺路的小巷，一直通向位于西墙上的另一个院门。走在八尺路上，左手边先是开阔的菜地和花园，右侧则是被灰墙黑瓦围合起来用于居住的院落。紧贴这组院落的西墙外，是一个面积比一般住宅还大的长方形水塘，叫澈月塘，跨过八尺路和水塘相对的还有一个水井。这种在建筑内部的塘和井又被称作屋里塘和屋里井。在那个时代，屋里塘和屋里井通常是深宅大院和财富的代名词。水塘西边原本就是院墙，现在只剩下两米多宽、用石块和夯土垒砌的墙基。据说沿墙有廊，廊下的空间既可以做储藏，又可以养家畜。

居住空间坐北朝南，原本是一个拥有三厅两堂的三进院落。这组院落以一座单檐门楼式大门作为入口，眉檐上的砖雕还清晰可见。第一进院落是一个三合院，明堂为方形，尺度与得耕居一进院落明堂相当。其檐廊下的雀替和二层栏板的样式都属于明末清初当地民居的典型做法，老街上只有同时期建造的绍常祠堂和义丰雅记采用同样做法。比较这两幢建筑，可以看出那个时期建筑的另一个特点就是在正房和厢房的转角处各做一个小的天井以便通风采光，但是在这幢院落中天井则被两间厢房替代。这种改变可能是因为人口的增加，也可能发生在将住宅改作旅店的年代。

院落的维护结构已几经重建，小木装饰也基本没有保留，但是房屋的大木构架相对保存完好，甚至包括一些精美雕饰。三面走廊全部采用月梁，正房檐廊的梁托上刻卷龙图案，厢房檐廊则用卷草。雀替采用的雕刻主题也有区别，正房一侧镂雕花鸟走兽，厢房则是卷草。正房明间月梁梁头上的卷草纹更是线条优美，比例匀称。居住在这里几十年的卢奶奶讲：正房明间原有六扇精美的雕花门扇；次间用于居住，各有一组雕花门窗；明间太师壁两侧有门，可以通向后面的院落。

后面两进院子在解放后便逐渐坍塌，只能从留下来的房基和台明铺地上寻找它们的痕迹。二进院是一个四合院，明堂宽度和一进院相同，进深大约减半。第三进院落又是一个三合院，明堂进深再次减半。宅院内原本是菜地和花园的位置，现在零零散散地盖满了几幢相对简易的住宅。除了八尺路南侧的住宅始建于十九世纪初以外，其他住宅应该建于土改前后，由分到地的穷困人家建造的。

— —

Cheng Lizhong (1736–1793), courtesy name Fengshi, art name Yi'an, was the 15th-generation ancestor of the Yanxia Cheng clan. His grandfather was the respected Zuo-xun Gong, who had his own ancestral hall; his father was Jingchen, the youngest son of Zuo-xun Gong and founder of the Gongchang branch. Cheng Lizhong and his three brothers established the Fa, Qiang, Gang, and Yi sub-branches. Today, Cheng Lizhong's descendants are the most numerous on the old street, known in the genealogy as 'Gongxia Yichang,' with their own hall—the Shaochang Ancestral Hall. According to legend, this three-hall, two-main-hall compound was built by Cheng Lizhong's father-in-law before sending his daughter to marry him—a story of 'building a flower hall at the foot of the cliff, with the father-in-law footing the bill' that remains a village favorite. Based on Cheng Lizhong's age, the residence dates to the mid-18th century.

The compound lies south of the Xiakeng ravine mouth, on the western foot of Lizard Mountain. Before its construction, village residences were concentrated within the Xiakeng ravine mouth, in the triangular valley where Xiakeng Stream and Fangyan Stream meet—an area elevated, relatively flat, convenient for both firewood gathering and tending fields, with morning sunlight in the courtyards. As population grew, the Cheng clan had to build elsewhere; Cheng Lizhong's compound was the first residence outside the ravine mouth. Beyond the ravine mouth, the old street hugs the nearly vertical Lizard Mountain, also called Qianshantou or Zhu Gong Mountain after the temple on it. The compound faces roughly south, with its east and west walls neatly wedged between the north-south old street and Fangyan Stream. Its southwest corner follows the stream's curve in an arc.

The compound employs a 'courtyard within courtyard' layout, making its built area larger than two Degeng Residences combined. Inside the walls are not only an imposing three-courtyard residential complex but also vegetable plots, a garden, an 'in-house pond,' and an 'in-house well.' Because the old street and Fangyan Stream differ in elevation by nearly one story, the residential portion occupies the higher eastern side facing the street, while the pond and vegetable plots lie on the lower western side near the water. Viewed from Fangyan Stream, the entire complex sits atop a massive stone platform, appearing even more imposing.

The main entrance is on the southern part of the east wall, facing the old street. Inside, a lane called 'Bachi Road' (Eight-Foot Road) leads to another gate on the west wall. Along Bachi Road, the left side opens to vegetable plots and a garden; the right side is the gray-walled, black-tiled residential compound. Against the west wall of the residential compound lies a rectangular pond larger than those of ordinary residences, called Cheyue Pond (Clear Moon Pond), with a well opposite it across Bachi Road. Such internal ponds and wells are called 'in-house ponds' and 'in-house wells'—in that era, synonyms for grand estates and wealth. The west side of the pond was originally a courtyard wall, now reduced to a two-meter-wide stone-and-rammed-earth foundation. A corridor reportedly ran along the wall, with space underneath for storage and livestock.

The residential space faces south, originally a three-courtyard compound with three halls and two main halls. It entered through a single-eave gatehouse with visible brick carvings on the eyebrow eave. The first courtyard is a three-sided compound with a square courtyard (mingtang) comparable in scale to Degeng's first courtyard. The sparrow brackets (queti) under the eaves corridor and the second-floor railing panels follow the typical late-Ming/early-Qing local residential style, shared only by the contemporary Shaochang Hall and Yifeng Yaji. Another characteristic of the period is small light wells at the corners between the main hall and wings for ventilation and light; in this compound, however, these wells were replaced by two wing rooms—possibly due to population growth or conversion to an inn.

The enclosure has been rebuilt several times and most small-wood ornamentation is gone, but the large timber frame is relatively well preserved, including some refined carvings. All three corridors use crescent beams (yueliang). The beam brackets on the main hall's eaves corridor are carved with coiled dragons; those on the wings use scrolling foliage. Sparrow bracket themes also differ: the main hall features openwork birds and beasts, while the wings use scrolling foliage. The scrolling foliage on the crescent beam ends of the main hall's central bay is particularly elegant and well-proportioned. Mrs. Lu, who lived there for decades, recalled that the main hall's central bay originally had six exquisitely carved door leaves; the side bays, used for residence, each had a set of carved doors and windows; and doors on either side of the taishi wall in the central bay led to the rear courtyard.

The rear two courtyards gradually collapsed after 1949, leaving only foundations and platform paving. The second courtyard was a four-sided compound with the same width as the first but about half the depth. The third was another three-sided compound with further reduced depth. The former vegetable plots and garden are now scattered with simpler residences—those south of Bachi Road dating to the early 19th century, the rest to the land reform period, built by poor families assigned land.

义丰雅记建筑群——十八世纪末的单进三合院住宅
Yifeng Yaji Complex — Late 18th Century Single-Courtyard Residences
二、住宅 | Residences

要点 Highlights

  • 三幢宅院并肩：义丰雅记（中间，最早）、义丰老店（北边）、义丰南院（南边，最晚）
  • 前厅13间，娘屋9间，后厅13间
  • 义丰雅记建于嘉庆二年（1797年），正堂挂怡金堂匾
  • 程乐镛（1832—1866年）从程立重后人处买下，三儿子分风、雅、颂
  • 品字形天井（官高一品寓意），后被厢房取代
  • 三幢建筑对比：排水沟/鹅卵石铺地、月梁/雀替/栏板、雕饰风格演变
  • 义丰南院雕饰最精美，花窗饰满花草鸟兽，梁托雕尽亭台楼阁
  • 义丰老店最简朴，仅简单格子窗

在蜈蚣山西侧，老街与方岩溪之间，孤孤单单三幢宅院并肩坐落在那里。这三幢院落的西墙紧贴着方岩溪，跨过溪水就是山谷里现存面积最大的一片稻田。远远望去，经过几百年雨水冲刷而略带绛红色的青砖和黑瓦，在青山和绿野的映衬下，显得格外的深邃和凝重。这三幢宅院中建造最早的是中间的义丰雅记，其次是北边的义丰老店，南边的最晚，姑且称它为义丰南院，它们一起构成了前厅13间，娘屋9间，后厅13间的建筑群。

可以看出，这三幢建筑在建造过程中，对周围的环境进行了一系列的改造。在平面上，义丰雅记的西墙在逆时针方向上转了几度。可以猜测，这是方岩溪一百多年前的走向。义丰南院在建造的时候，并没有顺着义丰雅记西墙的方向继续建造，而是建成了一个方方正正、完全对称的院落。由此认为，义丰南院的建造者把方岩溪原有的河道向西推了推，这或许就是方岩溪在义丰南院南墙外，拐了一个近九十度大弯的原因。在剖面上，和程立重宅院相同，这三幢院落都稳稳当当地坐落在巨石和夯土垒砌起来的、近一层高的基础上。使用这种做法的原因是，街靠山，溪沿谷，老街的高度随地形起伏变化，所以在局部区域就会和山谷有近一层的高差。这种抬高院落地平的做法相对费时费工，所以到十九世纪初，这里的院落都采用了另外一种形式，即下层系建筑在谷里，中层沿街，上层为楼。

老街上的大部分人都认为，义丰雅记的建造者也是十五世祖程立重。但是住在义丰雅记里的程人龙老人讲，这座宅院建造于嘉庆二年（1797年），由此推算，建造者应该是程立重（1736—1793年）的后人。岩下老街的程氏在十六世纪初分为福、禄、寿、柏四房。这四房中柏房的人口最少，福、寿两房虽说人丁还算兴旺，但是和禄房相比就逊色很多。作勳公和他的子孙，还有后来街上开店的大多数人家都是禄房下的。在义丰雅记建好的几十年后，福房下的程乐镛（1832—1866年），即程人龙的太公，却从程立重的后人那里买下了义丰雅记。程乐镛是程礼进（1778—1862年）唯一的儿子，据家谱中记载，他们父子俩都曾任州司马。从另一方面考虑，卖房卖地一向是封建家族在财力不济时的最后选择。由此推测，程乐镛当时可能只买下了义丰雅记和义丰老店这两幢建筑，随后为他的三儿子加建了义丰南院。

县志中记载程义丰旅店开业于清咸丰年间（1851—1861年），即由程乐镛创建。程乐镛有三个儿子，程昭贡、程昭梭和程昭明。据老街上九十岁高龄的程崇舜老人讲，这三座院落曾以三兄弟的房号为名，自南向北分别被作风、雅、颂。分家之后，小儿子程昭明住北院继续经营程义丰旅店并改名为义丰老店；老二程昭梭住中间，以房号为店号经营义丰雅记；老大程昭贡住南院，因为家中有四个儿子，住房紧张，所以未曾开店。

义丰雅记原本是一个正房三间、厢房一边各三间的三合院，沿院落南墙有走廊连接东西厢房。院落的入口原本在南面门楼正中的，当义丰南院建好之后，义丰雅记的入口就挪到了院落东南角，和老街直接相通。据程人龙老人讲，义丰雅记的正堂内曾挂匾，上书怡金堂，据说这是先人做官时别人赠的，所以义丰雅记又被称为怡金堂。

比较这三幢建筑，可以看出在短短半个多世纪内老街上建筑的发展与变化。从平面布局上看，义丰雅记正房和厢房的转角处一边各有一个长方形的小天井。于是在平面上，天井和明堂一起构成了一个品字，品字形的天井通常含有官高一品的寓意。在另外两幢住宅中，天井的位置都各被两间厢房取而代之。三幢建筑正房的进深基本相似，但是由于义丰雅记的檐廊较宽，正房和后墙之间又有一个走廊，所以明间进深相对较小，室内采用整根月梁纵跨正堂。义丰雅记和义丰南院的正房明间均明显宽于次间，义丰老店的做法和老街上大部分宅院相同，即三间正房宽度仅有略微不同。义丰雅记和义丰老店正房高度均为八米左右，义丰南院却矮了半米。老街上随后建成的院落，正房高度均为七米五左右，显然这个新的高度代表了住宅建筑一个新的模数和做法。

义丰雅记檐廊下采用雀替，二层栏板的样式与同时期建造的程立重宅院和绍常祠堂相同。另外两幢建筑廊下既没有雀替，栏板的做法也简单许多。三幢院落明堂的做法也各不相同：义丰雅记台明外侧做排水沟，明堂用条石铺地；义丰老店明堂用鹅卵石铺地，无排水沟；义丰南院有排水沟，却用鹅卵石铺地。比较老街上的其他建筑，排水沟是一种早期的做法，日后建成的建筑均用鹅卵石满铺明堂，雨水通过明堂四角的落水口排出。另外一点显著的不同则体现在木构件的雕饰上：义丰老店的檐廊下既没有采用月梁，小木装修也非常朴素，仅采用了简单的格子窗；义丰雅记的大木雕饰或小木装修多采用抽象的几何图案作为装饰母题，雕刻精美却不失典雅与质朴之美；义丰南院的雕饰最为精美却略显做作，不但花窗中饰满花草鸟兽，就连檐廊下的小小梁托两侧也雕尽亭台楼阁。

以上这些建筑细节的演变，固然在一定程度上客观地反映了建造者当时的财力状况。但是如果把这些变化作横向的比较，就可以看出人们对建筑空间和建筑本身的价值观和审美取向的细微转变。在程立重的年代，建造者的财力体现在宽大高敞的空间，品字形的天井，厅堂中两根粗犷却不乏雕饰的月梁，铺满条石的明堂，以及画龙点睛的雕饰。而对于清朝末年的建造者们，由于人口的增加，新的经济形式的出现以及人们对居住空间功能需求的变化，这使他们更关心建筑的实用性与经济性。相反，由于建筑技术的发展在当时已经相对停滞，所以导致不论是建造者还是工匠都对建筑装饰还是一味追求。

— —

On the western side of Centipede Mountain, between the old street and Fangyan Stream, three residences stand side by side in isolation. Their west walls hug Fangyan Stream, beyond which lies the largest remaining rice paddy in the valley. Viewed from afar, the blue-gray bricks and black tiles, weathered by centuries of rain to a faint crimson, appear particularly deep and solemn against the green mountains and fields. Of the three, the central Yifeng Yaji was built first, followed by Yifeng Laodian to the north, and Yifeng Nanyuan to the south—together forming a complex of '13 front rooms, 9 middle rooms, 13 rear rooms.'

The three buildings show deliberate environmental modifications. Yifeng Yaji's west wall rotates several degrees counterclockwise, presumably following Fangyan Stream's course over a century ago. Yifeng Nanyuan, however, was built as a perfectly square, symmetrical compound rather than following Yifeng Yaji's wall direction—suggesting its builder pushed the stream's original course westward, which may explain the nearly 90-degree bend in Fangyan Stream outside Yifeng Nanyuan's south wall. In section, like Cheng Lizhong's compound, all three sit on massive stone-and-rammed-earth foundations nearly one story high. This is because the street follows the mountain while the stream follows the valley, creating local elevation differences of nearly one story. Raising the courtyard level was labor-intensive, so by the early 19th century, residences adopted a different form: 'lower story in the valley, middle story at street level, upper story as loft.'

Most villagers believe Yifeng Yaji was also built by the 15th-generation ancestor Cheng Lizhong. However, Cheng Renlong, who lived in Yifeng Yaji, stated it was built in the second year of Jiaqing (1797), placing its builder among Cheng Lizhong's (1736–1793) descendants. The Yanxia Cheng clan divided into four branches—Fu, Lu, Shou, and Bai—in the early 16th century. The Bai branch was smallest; Fu and Shou were modest compared to the dominant Lu branch, which included Zuo-xun Gong's descendants and most later shopkeepers. Decades after Yifeng Yaji's completion, Cheng Leyong (1832–1866) of the Fu branch—Cheng Renlong's great-grandfather—purchased it from Cheng Lizhong's descendants. Cheng Leyong was the only son of Cheng Lijin (1778–1862); both father and son served as 'zhou sima' (prefectural administrator). Since selling property was a last resort for declining clans, it is推测 that Cheng Leyong bought only Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Laodian, then added Yifeng Nanyuan for his third son.

County records note that the Cheng Yifeng Inn opened during the Xianfeng period (1851–1861), founded by Cheng Leyong. He had three sons: Cheng Zhaogong, Cheng Zhaosuo, and Cheng Zhaoming. According to 90-year-old Cheng Chongshun, the three courtyards were named after the brothers' branch titles, from south to north: Feng (Wind), Ya (Elegance), Song (Ode). After division, the youngest son Cheng Zhaoming lived in the north courtyard and continued the inn, renaming it Yifeng Laodian; the second son Cheng Zhaosuo lived in the middle and operated Yifeng Yaji; the eldest Cheng Zhaogong lived in the south courtyard but, with four sons and tight housing, never opened an inn.

Yifeng Yaji was originally a three-sided compound with a three-bay main hall and three wing rooms on each side, with a corridor along the south wall connecting the wings. Its entrance was originally at the center of the south gatehouse; after Yifeng Nanyuan was built, the entrance moved to the southeast corner, directly connecting to the old street. According to Cheng Renlong, the main hall once displayed a plaque reading 'Yijin Hall' (Hall of Harmonious Gold), said to be a gift from an official ancestor—hence Yifeng Yaji's alternative name.

Comparing the three buildings reveals architectural evolution over half a century. In plan, Yifeng Yaji has rectangular light wells at each corner between the main hall and wings, forming a 'pin' (品) character pattern with the courtyard—symbolizing 'official rank elevated by one grade.' In the other two residences, these wells were replaced by two wing rooms each. All three have similar main-hall depth, but Yifeng Yaji's wider eaves corridor and rear passage reduce its central-bay depth, requiring a single full-length crescent beam spanning the hall. Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Nanyuan have noticeably wider central bays than side bays; Yifeng Laodian follows the common local pattern of nearly equal bay widths. Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Laodian stand about 8 meters high; Yifeng Nanyuan is half a meter shorter. Later residences on the street standardized at about 7.5 meters, representing a new modular norm.

Yifeng Yaji's eaves corridor uses sparrow brackets (queti), and its second-floor railing panels match those of the contemporary Cheng Lizhong compound and Shaochang Hall. The other two buildings lack sparrow brackets and have simpler railings. Courtyard paving also differs: Yifeng Yaji has drainage channels outside the platform and stone-slab paving; Yifeng Laodian uses cobblestone paving without drainage; Yifeng Nanyuan has drainage but cobblestone paving. Drainage channels are an early feature; later buildings use cobblestone throughout with water draining through corner outlets. Most strikingly, timber ornamentation differs: Yifeng Laodian has no crescent beams and simple lattice windows; Yifeng Yaji uses abstract geometric patterns in both large and small woodwork—refined yet elegant and rustic; Yifeng Nanyuan has the most elaborate (if slightly contrived) ornamentation, with flower-and-bird carvings in windows and even pavilion-and-tower carvings on the small beam brackets under the eaves.

These architectural evolutions objectively reflect the builders' financial circumstances, but横向 comparison reveals subtle shifts in values and aesthetics. In Cheng Lizhong's era, wealth manifested in spacious rooms, pin-shaped light wells, robust yet ornamented crescent beams, stone-slab courtyards, and accent carvings. By the late Qing, population growth, new economic forms, and changing functional needs made builders prioritize practicality and economy—while architectural technology had stagnated, leading both builders and craftsmen to pursue ornamentation ever more fervently.

程成昌旅店——二十世纪初的旅店式四合院住宅
Chengchengchang Inn — Early 20th Century Inn-Style Courtyard Residence
二、住宅 | Residences

要点 Highlights

  • 程昭丰（1855—1928年）创建，程立重五世孙
  • 约建于1909—1924年间，典型旅店式合院住宅
  • 正房三间，左右厢房各五间，倒座三间，共16间（比义丰雅记多7间）
  • 倒座房朝北——旅店功能需求，房间越多越好
  • 西间（正房明间）为公共空间和礼制中心：吃饭、休息、祭祖、吊唁、设香案拜胡公
  • 太师壁、上大房/下大房、里厢房/外厢房/小西间/元房/过路间
  • 名堂宽12米进深7.5米，鹅卵石铺地，四角落水口，屋里井
  • 1958—1960年做过养老院、制伞厂，1980年恢复营业，1991年迁新街改名新程成昌

程成昌旅店是一家典型的旅店式合院住宅，它位于老街以东，下胡坑坑口以北，院落东墙紧贴着狮子山的一角。其建造年代虽然不能确定，但是可以通过周围院落的建造时间做出推测。在程成昌旅店的南侧，有两座院落属于程振兴旅店。南端的院落于1880年落成，中间的建于19年后。在程成昌北面、老街西侧，程振兴旅店在1924年又拥有了属于它的第三幢院落。由此推断，程成昌旅店应该建造于1909年至1924年之间。老街的形成过程，总体上来说是一个以上坑为中心，逐渐向南北线性发展的过程。街上许多相邻的建筑，多数情况下是沿街道向南或向北依次建成的。特别是属于一家人的几幢院落，如果可能的话都会并肩建造，并在空间上相互连通。

程成昌旅店的创始人叫程昭丰（1855—1928年），他的二哥程昭夫拥有老街上最大的旅店，堂号程振昌；六弟程昭贡则创建了位于老街北头、橙麓村境内的程隆昌；四弟程昭路开的程振昌杂货店也在文革后转做旅店生意。可以说在二十世纪初，远道来方岩的香客，至少有三分之一住在这弟兄四人开的旅店中。程昭丰其实是程立重的五世孙，其父为程乐涞，祖程礼书，曾祖程宜棚。程礼书作为程宜棚的长子，继承了建在程立重宅院内花园上的一处小宅院。程礼书本无后，程乐涞是礼书的三弟礼齐过继给兄长的，这样程乐涞便继承了其父亲的全部财产，包括这幢住宅。乐涞一家的生活应该不算太拮据，所以他的每个儿子在成家后都能从父亲那里得到一点本钱和部分生活必需品，搬出去独立生活并开始经营自己的小买卖，而乐涞也曾利用自家的住宅经营过一个小旅店。

继子和独子的存在，在中国封建父系社会里是两种很特别并且有意义的现象。兄弟之间相互过继儿子，在短时间内当然不会对这个家族的人口有任何影响。但是从长远考虑，家庭背景及生活环境的多样性，大大增加了整个家族持续繁衍生息的可能性。翻阅老街上程氏的《世远公宗谱》就不难发现，这种过继现象非常普遍。有时收养继子的人并不是没有儿子，只是不多，这时如果他的兄弟有较多的儿子，也会过继一个给他；有时送子的人家可能也只有两个儿子，但是为了整个家族的利益，也会忍痛把小儿子过继给没有儿子的兄弟；有时兄弟几个，一人无后，其他兄弟便每人过继给他一个；如果亲兄弟之间不能过继，偶尔也会从同宗的堂兄弟之间选择。如果说多生儿子是为了更好地延续香火，那么独子就是一种很不利的情况。但是独子的最大意义就在于，他能免受分家的痛苦，顺顺当当地继承父亲的全部财产。老街上旅店业的创始人基本上都是程氏十九世的子孙们，在这个行业里做得不错的有三家兄弟，其中两家兄弟的父亲都是家中的独子，或许这种巧合中也有一定的客观因素。

程昭丰去世后，他的独子程宣儞继承了父业。在宣儞的四个儿子中，长子早亡；二儿子迁居异乡；小儿子程镇仪一直在经营程成昌旅店，并于1991年搬到新街上，改名为新程振昌；三儿子程镇康一家，以及店里的一些伙计还依旧住在老房子里。

程成昌旅店的平面布局和建筑形式是十九世纪末、二十世纪初老街上新建住宅的典型。同以前建造的宅院相比，这些新建院落最大的不同就是建造者已经把开旅店作为建筑的主要功能来考虑，而这种变化最直接的反映就是在建筑的平面上。以前以居住为主要功能的院落，不论是十六世纪初建造的得耕居，十八世纪末的程立重宅院和义丰雅记，还是十九世纪中后期建造的程正伟火腿店及其加建，它们或许是一进三合院，或者是由多个三合院组成的多进院落，也就是说，院落里没有房间朝北。北向日照不好，冬天冷、夏天热，不适宜居住，舍去是情理之中的事。这样做的另一个好处就是，院落立面可以变得比较自由，起起伏伏，更利于突出中间的门楼式入口。但是如果用来做旅店，则是房间越多越好。程成昌和程振兴旅店主要院落的占地面积和义丰雅记差不多大小，都约为430平方米，明堂面积也大相径庭，但是义丰雅记一层只有九间房，舍弃两个小天井的义丰老店和义丰南院也不过有十三间，而这些新建的旅店却有十六间。究其原因，以程成昌旅店为例，其正房和厢房的进深相比义丰雅记都各短一米左右，这样省下来的面积就转化成了内院北侧的一排房。相比同时期橙麓村的住宅，由于没有开旅店这种功能需求，所有院落不论朝向，都没有倒座这排房，当地人还以一层房间数目的多少，将这些院落俗称为九间头、十一间头等。

程成昌旅店大体上为正南北朝向，有正房三间，左右厢房各五间，倒座三间。正房和两侧厢房之间各有一个一米多宽的夹道，有楼梯通向二楼。正房中间的一间，在这里被称为西间，它既是旅店和住宅的公共空间，也是这个家庭的礼制中心：一家老少在这里吃饭；客人来了在这里休息、喝茶；逢年过节在这里祭祖；亲人去世在这里吊唁；就连因为各种原因上不了方岩的香客，也会在这里设香案，朝向方岩山祭拜胡公。西间的后金柱之间是太师壁，即使是平时的时候，太师壁上也会挂着祖宗的像。由于功能的需要，西间通常可以全部开敞，如果设门的话，也是可摘除的板门壁，太师壁后面是储藏间，通过旁边的一个耳门进入。西间的东西两间分别被称为上大房和下大房，东为上，地位略高，通常在分家的时候会优先分给长子，下大房给次子。东边的厢房自北向南分别叫里厢房、外厢房、小西间、元房和过路间。西厢房临街，作为门面房，也叫店堂。靠南的两间店堂顺街势向外突出了几米，最北的一间在二层还形成了一个小的跨楼，横跨老街。遗憾的是，这两间房在程昭夫手上被卖了出去，余下的三间自南向北依次称作上店堂、中店堂以及下店堂，其中下店堂这个开间就是院落及旅店的入口。倒座三间自东向西分别是东朝厅、朝厅与西朝厅。朝厅进深较小，所以是院落内，不论一层二层，唯一没有檐廊的一面。

名堂宽为12米，进深约7.5米，即三间正房和两间厢房的宽度。名堂用鹅卵石铺地，地平中间略高，四周稍低，并在四个角落设落水口，和街道两边的排水沟相接。院落中间还有代表财力和地位的屋里井。井水清澈甘甜，至今还是程镇康一家人饮用水的主要来源。经过院落东南角的过路间，就是三开间宽的储物间并卫生间，出了储物间北边的门，便是后院。后院里有一口小水塘，大约有半间屋那么大，以前是清洗马桶用的，现在用来清洗农具。紧贴着储物间的山坎儿上，还有一块小菜地。院落北边临街的位置原本是一个面积很大的伙房，这是因为伙房里不但要做饭，还要酿酒，更要养猪、鸡、鸭、鹅等牲畜。在旺季，程昭丰的家人并带伙计也会住在伙房的二楼，以便把更多的房间腾给香客。解放后，一部分伙房卖给了外人；十九世纪八十年代，余下的伙房倒塌，于是改用小西间做厨房。

程成昌旅店的营业初期，客房十分简陋，主要接待来自于自东阳、义乌、永康、金华、蒲江、嵊县等地的香客。他们的要求不高，大可以满足于两人挤一张床的住宿条件。所谓床就是用两个凳子架一块一米多宽木板，上面铺薄薄一层稻草。逢上八九月的香汛，只要能在有屋顶的地方打个地铺，客人也就心满意足了。在这种情况下，楼上楼下一共能住小三百人。但是随着胡公的名声越来越大，来自于上海和绍兴等大城市的客人也随之增加，他们便对旅店的饮食和设施等方面提出了更高的要求。程成昌在这方面最终没能和像程振兴这样的大旅店竞争，所以只能接待一些中等或下等的香客。

二十世纪四十年代，在浙江省国民政府进驻方岩的那几年，和其他大部分旅店一样，程成昌几乎停止了营业。虽然没有了香客，但是程成昌变得更热闹了。出出进进的都是在民政厅工作的公务员及其家属，店堂还出租做了一个印刷厂。这些公务员对居住条件的要求较高，为此程成昌还进行了一系列的改造，其中包括在一层地面上加铺木地板以便防潮；把位于外墙上的窗户加大以利于采光；还有出于对安全的考虑，在过路间的位置加建了一个楼梯。

抗日战争结束以后，程成昌恢复营业，旅店生意又红火了几年。从1949年到1980年，旅店开了停，停了又开了若干次。在1958—1960年期间，还先后做过养老院、芝英人民公社制伞厂和岩下大队制伞厂。不过庆幸的是，由于家里人口多，所以房子没有在土改时分出去，这也是这幢院子至今还保存良好的重要原因。1980年8月，旅店再次恢复营业，随着生意的逐渐好转，程振仪于1991年把旅店迁到了位于新街旁边的现代化小楼里，改名为新程成昌。

— —

Chengchengchang Inn is a typical inn-style courtyard residence located east of the old street, north of the Xiahukeng ravine mouth, with its east wall hugging a corner of Lion Mountain. Its construction date is uncertain but can be inferred from surrounding buildings: to its south, two courtyards belonging to Chengzhenxing Inn were completed in 1880 and 19 years later; to its north, Chengzhenxing acquired a third courtyard in 1924. Thus Chengchengchang was likely built between 1909 and 1924. The old street developed linearly north-south from an upper-ravine center, with adjacent buildings often constructed sequentially; family-owned courtyards were typically built side by side and interconnected.

The founder was Cheng Zhaofeng (1855–1928). His second brother Cheng Zhaofu owned the street's largest inn (Chengzhenchang); his sixth brother Cheng Zhaogong founded Chenglongchang at the street's north end in Chengli Village; his fourth brother Cheng Zhaolu's general store converted to an inn after the Cultural Revolution. In the early 20th century, at least one-third of Fangyan pilgrims stayed at inns run by these four brothers. Cheng Zhaofeng was the fifth-generation descendant of Cheng Lizhong—his father Cheng Lelai, grandfather Cheng Lishu, great-grandfather Cheng Yipeng. Cheng Lishu, as Cheng Yipeng's eldest son, inherited a small residence built on the garden of Cheng Lizhong's compound. Cheng Lishu had no biological heir; Cheng Lelai was adopted from his third brother Liqi, thus inheriting all his father's property including the residence. The family was reasonably well-off, so each son received startup capital and necessities upon marriage to live independently and run small businesses; Lelai himself once operated a small inn from his home.

The institutions of adopted sons and only sons held special significance in China's patriarchal society. While brother-to-brother adoption had no immediate demographic impact, it increased the clan's long-term survival prospects through diverse family backgrounds and environments. The Shiyuan Gong Genealogy shows adoption was common: sometimes a brother with few sons would receive one from a brother with many; sometimes a family with only two sons would give the younger to a childless brother for the clan's sake; sometimes several brothers would each give a son to a childless brother; if biological brothers could not adopt, cousins were chosen. While having many sons ensured lineage continuation, being an only son meant avoiding family division and inheriting all property intact. Notably, the street's successful inn founders were mostly 19th-generation Cheng descendants, and among the three most successful brother groups, two had fathers who were only sons—suggesting an objective factor in this coincidence.

After Cheng Zhaofeng's death, his only son Cheng Xuanni inherited the business. Of Xuanni's four sons: the eldest died young; the second moved away; the youngest Cheng Zhenyi continued the inn and relocated to the new street in 1991, renaming it Xinchengzhenchang; the third son Cheng Zhenkang's family and some inn staff still live in the old building.

Chengchengchang's plan and form typify late-19th/early-20th century new residences on the street. The key difference from earlier compounds is that inn operation became the primary function, most directly reflected in the plan. Earlier residential compounds—from the 16th-century Degeng, to late-18th-century Cheng Lizhong and Yifeng Yaji, to mid-19th-century Cheng Zhengwei's ham shop—were either single three-sided courtyards or multi-courtyard complexes with no north-facing rooms. North orientation offered poor sunlight, cold winters, and hot summers, making it unsuitable for residence; omitting it also allowed freer, undulating facades emphasizing the central gatehouse. For inns, however, more rooms meant more income. Chengchengchang and Chengzhenxing's main courtyards occupy about 430 square meters—similar to Yifeng Yaji—yet Yifeng Yaji has only 9 ground-floor rooms, Yifeng Laodian and Yifeng Nanyuan at most 13, while these new inns have 16. In Chengchengchang's case, the main hall and wings are each about one meter shallower than Yifeng Yaji's, freeing area for a row of rooms on the courtyard's north side (the daozuo/reverse hall). Contemporary residences in Chengli Village, lacking inn function, have no reverse halls regardless of orientation; locals call them 'nine-room heads,' 'eleven-room heads,' etc., based on ground-floor room count.

Chengchengchang faces due north-south, with three main-hall bays, five wing rooms on each side, and three reverse-hall bays. Between the main hall and each wing is a meter-wide passage with a staircase to the second floor. The central main-hall bay, called 'West Room' (Xijian), serves as both public space and ritual center: the family eats there; guests rest and drink tea there; ancestors are worshipped during festivals; the deceased are mourned there; even pilgrims unable to climb Fangyan set up incense tables there to face the mountain and worship Lord Hu. Between the rear gold pillars of the West Room is a taishi wall, which displays ancestor portraits even on ordinary days. The West Room is usually fully open; if doors are installed, they are removable panel doors. Behind the taishi wall is a storage room accessed through a side door. The two side bays are called Upper Great Room (east, higher status, inherited by the eldest son) and Lower Great Room (west, for the second son). The east wing rooms, from north to south, are: Inner Wing, Outer Wing, Small West Room, Primary Room, and Passage Room. The west wing faces the street as shop fronts; the two southern bays project several meters with the street, and the northernmost has a second-floor overpass spanning the old street. These two were unfortunately sold by Cheng Zhaofu; the remaining three are called Upper Shop, Middle Shop, and Lower Shop, with Lower Shop serving as the courtyard and inn entrance. The three reverse-hall bays, from east to west, are East Facing Hall, Facing Hall, and West Facing Hall—shallower in depth and the only courtyard sides without eaves corridors on either floor.

The courtyard (mingtang) is 12 meters wide and 7.5 meters deep—spanning three main-hall bays and two wing bays. It is paved with cobblestones, slightly raised at the center and lower at the edges, with water outlets at the four corners connecting to street-side drainage. In the courtyard center is an 'in-house well,' symbolizing wealth and status; its water remains clear and sweet, still the main drinking water source for Cheng Zhenkang's family. Through the southeast Passage Room is a three-bay storage and bathroom; beyond its north door lies a rear courtyard with a small pond (half a room in size), formerly for washing chamber pots, now for farm tools. Against the hillside beside the storage room is a small vegetable plot. North of the courtyard, facing the street, was originally a large kitchen—needed not only for cooking but also brewing wine and raising pigs, chickens, ducks, and geese. During peak season, family and staff lived on the kitchen's second floor to free more rooms for pilgrims. After 1949, part of the kitchen was sold; the rest collapsed in the 1980s, and the Small West Room became the kitchen.

In its early years, Chengchengchang's guest rooms were simple, mainly serving pilgrims from Dongyang, Yiwu, Yongkang, Jinhua, Pujiang, and Shengxian. Their needs were modest—two people sharing one 'bed' was acceptable. A 'bed' meant two stools supporting a one-meter-wide plank with a thin layer of straw. During the August-September pilgrimage season, any place with a roof for a floor mattress satisfied guests. Under these conditions, the inn could house nearly 300 people upstairs and downstairs. But as Lord Hu's fame grew, guests from Shanghai and Shaoxing demanded better food and facilities. Chengchengchang could not compete with large inns like Chengzhenxing and ultimately served only 'medium' or 'lower' grade pilgrims.

In the 1940s, when the Zhejiang Provincial Government was stationed at Fangyan, Chengchengchang, like most inns, nearly ceased pilgrimage operations. Instead it bustled with civil servants from the Civil Affairs Department and their families; the shop front was rented as a printing house. These civil servants had higher housing standards, prompting renovations: wood flooring on the ground floor for damp protection, enlarged windows for light, and an additional staircase at the Passage Room for safety.

After the war, Chengchengchang resumed business and thrived for several years. From 1949 to 1980, it opened and closed repeatedly. Between 1958 and 1960, it served as a nursing home, an umbrella factory for the Zhiying People's Commune, and an umbrella factory for the Yanxia brigade. Fortunately, the large family meant the house was not divided during land reform—a key reason it remains well-preserved. The inn reopened in August 1980; as business improved, Cheng Zhenyi relocated to a modern building near the new street in 1991, renaming it Xinchengchengchang.

程仁昌旅店、程振兴旅店、程铿别墅——二十世纪中叶的中西合璧式建筑
Chengrenchang Inn, Chengzhenxing Inn, Cheng Keng Villa — Mid-20th Century Chinese-Western Hybrid Architecture
二、住宅 | Residences

要点 Highlights

  • 程仁昌旅店：四层高木结构单体楼宇，潘工程师建议改独栋式宅院
  • 程昭业（1882年建程仁昌），程宣祜改店号新仁昌
  • 每层栏杆29块精美雕花栏板，西方建筑影响体现在空间布局
  • 程铿别墅：程昭纯（1864生）建，程铿（1895—1968年）为名画家
  • 坐西朝东，一米多高石砌台基，三开间六空间四面有廊
  • 每间卧室双向开窗通风，这房子很凉快
  • 程振兴旅店洋楼：北边1930年代程昭夫建二层六开间砖石结构
  • 南边1942年程宣楣、程宣吉委托杭州营造厂建三层七开间木构洋楼
  • 砖砌壁柱、圆形小窗、宽大玻璃窗、砖发券拱形窗、西洋式门楼
  • 程宣楣子程崇学改店号新振兴

在二十世纪中叶，老街上先后落成了几幢中西合璧式的建筑。这其中有两幢分别属于程仁昌旅店和程振兴旅店的一部分，另一幢则被称为程铿别墅。

属于程仁昌旅店的这幢中西合璧式建筑位于狮子山西麓，老街西侧，程仁昌老店的北墙外。这原本是一幢四层高的木结构单体楼宇，其建筑材料、建造方法以及装饰母题都遵循当地传统建筑的特点，西方建筑的影响主要体现在空间布局上。据程仁昌旅店的后人程镇隆老人回忆，当初建房时，程昭业本想盖一幢传统的四合院，是在潘工程师的建议下才改成独栋式宅院的。与老街上的其他合院建筑不同，程仁昌旅店这幢建筑的院落紧邻老街，平面近方形的三开间楼宇坐落在院落西侧，房间与院落之间用前廊过渡。院落地平与山谷同高，位于老街上的入口处相当于二层的高度，进门之后通过两侧台阶到达一层。空间布局的另一点不同表现在剖面上。老街上的宅院二层原本并不住人，只做储藏，所以二层栏板的做法只是把几块木板竖向排列在框架里。相比西方建筑，高层空间一向都是居住的主要空间，所以栏杆自然成为装饰的重点。旅店业兴起之后，二层空间在香汛期间成为重要的居住空间，二十世纪中叶以后建成的建筑多少都对栏板做了些装饰。这幢建筑每层的栏杆都有二十九块十分精美的雕花栏板。三十年代中期，程昭业的大儿子程宣祜继承了这幢楼房和后面的一个小院，改店号为新仁昌。程宣祜有三子，解放初由于经济条件不好，被迫卖掉了建筑的三层和四层。

程铿别墅位于方岩山入口处，老街南侧。程铿（1895—1968年），依家谱记载名程宣铿，为程昭纯长子。据程昭纯之孙程振锋（1939年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人回忆，程昭纯（1864生，卒年不详）才是这幢别墅的建造者，在他去世后，别墅为长子和二子共有，三子即其父，分得了老屋。程铿作为二十世纪初当地一位小有名气的画家，他年轻时就离开家到上海发展，八一三事变后回到永康定居。虽然他可能并没有在这里居住过，而且也不拥有整幢建筑，人们还是因为他的名气而称这幢建筑为程铿别墅。程铿别墅也是一幢单体木结构建筑，相比程仁昌旅店，西方建筑对空间布局的影响在这里更为显著。整幢建筑坐落在一个一米多高的石砌台基上，坐西朝东，四周有院落空间环绕。平面三开间，由六个近方形的空间组成，四面有廊。前、后正间为开敞或半开敞的厅堂，南北四间居住，向南、北走廊开门。这样布局的最大好处之一就是每间卧室都向两个方向开窗，有利于通风，难怪住在这里的程铿后人强调这房子很凉快。与程仁昌旅店相同，程铿别墅的二层栏板也有精美的镂空木雕。

在程振兴旅店所拥有的众多建筑中，有两幢连排西洋式楼屋。北边的一幢由程昭夫建于二十世纪三十年代，这是一座二层高六开间的砖石结构建筑。它北面和程昭夫祠堂南墙相接，东墙紧邻老街。从外面看，几乎看不到当地建筑的影子，取而代之的是西方建筑中常见的砖砌壁柱、圆形小窗及宽大明亮的玻璃窗。南边的一排建于1942年，由程昭夫的儿子程宣楣和程宣吉委托杭州营造厂建造。这座三层高七开间的洋楼其实依旧是一个木构建筑，所有房间坐北朝南并南北通风，各层房间用走廊连接并向南面的内院开敞。西方建筑元素主要体现在维护砖墙的细部处理上，例如用砖发券的拱形窗和西洋式的门楼。二十世纪四十年代中，程振兴一分为二，程宣楣的儿子程崇学继承了这两幢建筑，改店号为新振兴。解放初，由于经济原因卖掉了第二幢洋楼中的一层，现仅剩两层。

— —

In the mid-20th century, several Chinese-Western hybrid buildings were completed on the old street. Two belong to Chengrenchang Inn and Chengzhenxing Inn respectively; the third is known as Cheng Keng Villa.

The Chengrenchang hybrid building stands at the western foot of Lion Mountain, west of the old street, outside the north wall of the original Chengrenchang Inn. It is a four-story freestanding timber building whose materials, construction methods, and decorative motifs follow local tradition, with Western influence primarily in spatial layout. According to Cheng Zhenlong, a descendant of the Chengrenchang owners, Cheng Zhaoye originally planned a traditional four-sided courtyard but changed to a freestanding villa on the advice of Engineer Pan. Unlike other courtyard compounds on the street, this building's courtyard hugs the old street, with a nearly square three-bay building on the west side and a front porch transitioning between rooms and courtyard. The courtyard level matches the valley floor, so the street-level entrance is at second-floor height, with steps on both sides leading down to the first floor. Another difference appears in section: traditionally, second floors were for storage only, with simple vertical plank railings. In Western architecture, upper floors are primary living space, making railings a decorative focus. After the inn boom, second floors became important guest space during pilgrimage season, and post-mid-20th-century buildings increasingly ornamented their railings. This building has 29 exquisitely carved railing panels on each floor. In the mid-1930s, Cheng Zhaoye's eldest son Cheng Xuanhu inherited the building and rear courtyard, renaming the inn Xinrenchang. Xuanhu had three sons; in the early years after 1949, financial hardship forced the sale of the third and fourth floors.

Cheng Keng Villa lies at the entrance to Fangyan Mountain, south of the old street. Cheng Keng (1895–1968), genealogically named Cheng Xuankeng, was the eldest son of Cheng Zhaochun. According to Cheng Zhenfeng (b. 1939, 21st generation), Cheng Zhaochun (b. 1864, death date unknown) was the actual builder; after his death, the villa was shared by the eldest and second sons, while the third son—Cheng Zhenfeng's father—inherited the old house. Cheng Keng was a locally known painter in the early 20th century who left for Shanghai as a young man and returned to settle in Yongkang after the 1937 August 13 Incident. Although he may never have lived there and did not own the entire building, it bears his name due to his fame. Cheng Keng Villa is also a freestanding timber building, with stronger Western spatial influence than Chengrenchang. It sits on a stone platform over one meter high, faces east, and is surrounded by courtyard space. The three-bay plan comprises six nearly square rooms with corridors on all four sides. The front and rear central bays are open or semi-open halls; the four north-south rooms serve as bedrooms, opening to the north and south corridors. A key advantage is that every bedroom has windows on two sides for cross-ventilation—hence Cheng Keng's descendants emphasize that 'this house is very cool.' Like Chengrenchang, its second-floor railing panels feature refined openwork wood carving.

Among Chengzhenxing Inn's many buildings are two connected Western-style structures. The northern one, built by Cheng Zhaofu in the 1930s, is a two-story six-bay brick-and-stone building. Its north wall adjoins Cheng Zhaofu's ancestral hall, and its east wall hugs the old street. From the exterior, almost no local architectural features remain—replaced by Western brick pilasters, small round windows, and large bright glass windows. The southern row was built in 1942 by Cheng Zhaofu's sons Cheng Xuanmei and Cheng Xuanji, commissioned from a Hangzhou construction firm. This three-story seven-bay 'foreign building' is actually timber-framed, with all rooms facing south and cross-ventilated north-south, connected by corridors and opening to a southern inner courtyard. Western elements appear mainly in brick wall details: brick-arched windows and Western-style gatehouses. In the mid-1940s, Chengzhenxing split in two; Cheng Xuanmei's son Cheng Chongxue inherited these two buildings and renamed the inn Xinzhenxing. In the early post-1949 years, financial hardship forced the sale of one floor of the second building, leaving only two stories today.

程氏宗祠——橙麓村程氏总祠
Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall — Main Hall of the Cheng Clan in Chengli Village
三、宗祠 | Ancestral Halls

要点 Highlights

  • 明正德己亥年（1515年）始建，程氏迁来后第一个祠堂
  • 唯一被收入县志的程氏祠堂：三十五都程宗祠，始祀闰二闰三
  • 坐西朝东，东侧螺蛳塘山有三世祖程权之墓
  • 嘉靖年间改建前后两进，道光丙午年重修成五开间三进院落，石柱代木柱
  • 宽二十多米，进深近五十米，牌门一座，享堂五间，寝室九间
  • 1942年5月13日中共浙江省委书记刘英在祠堂内遇害
  • 1992年1月21日随刘英烈士陵园开工而被拆毁
  • 部分石柱、柱础、门楣送文楼村保存

在农耕社会里，人们无论是盖房、修路，或是开沟渠、整田亩，还是抵抗各种外来的天灾人祸，都不是一个人或是一个家庭可以独立完成的，而是需要许多人的相互扶持和共同努力。在中国几千年的父系社会里，建立在血缘和亲情基础之上的父系家族就很自然地承担起了这个责任。于是，聚族而居和血缘村落就自然而然地成为一种普遍的现象。这种客观现象的存在，以及历代中国社会都尊崇孝道的伦理准则，便为宗祠的产生提供了前提条件。纵观中国历史，北方长年战乱并且人口流失严重，相比之下南方相对安定，更易于血缘村落的形成和发展。所以在江浙地区，特别是像岩下老街这样，地处丘陵地带、交通不发达的村落，一般都保留有较强的宗族意识和相对完整的世族体系。

宗祠的基本功能在于，它是一个供奉和祭拜祖先的仪式性场所。这里的祠堂里都供奉着历代先人的牌位；逢年过节和祭祀时，还要挂上本房祖宗的画像。有的宗祠里还设有谱房以便管理宗族人口的派系、行辈、婚姻等问题。祠堂同时也是商议族内大事、调解族人矛盾、旌表功名、办学堂、存放公共器物的场所。在大一点儿的祠堂里还有戏台，在特殊的日子里，宗祠便暂时放下它的神圣地位，而成为全村的文化娱乐中心，甚至还允许族人在这里赌博娱乐。以上这些都体现了宗祠存在的意义和重要性，即通过一系列祖先崇拜的仪式及活动来加强程氏家族的内部凝聚力。加强宗族的内聚力，就是加强人际合作的社会单元的能量，这是人们生存并发展的最重要依靠，从而促成了祖先崇拜，也便是血缘认同。

虽然橙麓村和岩下村供奉的是同一始祖程锴，但是老街旁曾先后建造了大大小小至少八座程氏各房派的祠堂。它们在村里都是最重要，通常也是建造最精美的建筑。在江浙地区的多数村落中，祠堂通常会位于村落相对中心的地带，其位置和朝向多少都会影响到村落的布局。但是老街的发展由于受到地形的限制，所以这里的祠堂大多都沿街坐落，并没能对村落的空间序列起到决定性的影响。即便如此，在用地紧张的情况下，祠堂前通常都有水塘或空地，这个空间依旧是村落生活的舞台。

程氏宗祠始建于明正德己亥年（1515年），位于橙麓村象鼻岗山脚。它是迁居这里的程氏所建的第一个祠堂，也是岩下老街众多祠堂中唯一被收入县志的，三十五都程宗祠：始祀闰二闰三。祠堂坐西朝东，东侧是螺蛳塘山，山上建有三世祖程权之墓。在嘉靖年间，祠堂曾改建为前后两进；在道光丙午年重修成五开间，前后三进院落，并且用石柱代替已经糟朽的木柱。据当地人回忆：整个祠堂宽二十多米，进深近五十米；有牌门一座，享堂五间，寝室九间，还有厢房位于南北两侧。在抗日战争时期，中共浙江省委书记刘英被关在程氏宗祠内，并于1942年5月13日在祠堂内遇害。1992年1月21日，程氏宗祠随着刘英烈士陵园的开工而被拆毁。在当地程氏后人的努力下，部分石柱、柱础和门楣等建筑构件被送到文楼村保存，以便日后重建。居住在橙麓村的程家麟老人还花钱买下了一些柱础，这些柱础现在还依然摆放在他家的院墙外。还有一些柱础上面架上了石条，成了村里人小憩和唠家常的地方。在橙麓村的水塘边和小路旁，还能看到许多似乎曾被用作为台明石的大块石料；水塘里的一块洗衣石上还有两个刚劲的楷体大字之墓。老人们已经不记得这块石头的来历，更无法寻找另一半墓碑的下落。这些点点滴滴的痕迹，都记录着那幢曾经矗立在村口长达五百年之久的建筑的辉煌。

— —

In agrarian society, building houses, repairing roads, digging irrigation channels, tending fields, and resisting natural and man-made disasters were never tasks an individual or single family could accomplish alone—they required mutual support and collective effort. In China's millennia-old patriarchal society, the patrilineal clan, founded on blood ties and kinship, naturally assumed this responsibility. Thus, clan-based settlement and kinship villages became widespread. This social reality, combined with the Confucian ethic of filial piety revered throughout Chinese history, provided the preconditions for ancestral halls. Historically, northern China suffered prolonged warfare and population loss, while the south was relatively stable, favoring the formation and development of kinship villages. In the Jiangsu-Zhejiang region—especially in hilly, inaccessible villages like Yanxia Old Street—strong clan consciousness and relatively complete lineage systems generally persisted.

The basic function of an ancestral hall is as a ritual space for enshrining and worshipping ancestors. The halls here house ancestral tablets of successive generations; during festivals and memorial rites, portraits of branch ancestors are hung.
