Some halls have a genealogy room to manage lineage branches, generation rankings, and marriages. Halls also serve as venues for clan affairs, dispute mediation, honoring scholarly achievements, running schools, and storing communal objects. Larger halls have stages; on special occasions, the hall temporarily sets aside its sacred character to become the village's cultural and entertainment center—even allowing gambling. All this reflects the hall's significance: strengthening clan cohesion through ancestor-worship rituals. Strengthening clan cohesion means strengthening the energy of the social unit for human cooperation—the most important reliance for survival and development—thus fostering ancestor worship, which is kinship identity.

Although Chengli Village and Yanxia Village share the same founding ancestor Cheng Kai, at least eight ancestral halls of various Cheng branches were built along the old street. They were the village's most important and usually most exquisitely built structures. In most Jiangsu-Zhejiang villages, halls occupy relatively central locations, their position and orientation influencing village layout. But due to topographic constraints on the old street's development, halls here mostly line the street without determining the village's spatial sequence. Even so, given tight land use, halls usually have ponds or open spaces in front—spaces that remain stages for village life.

The Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall was first built in the 10th year of Zhengde (1515) at the foot of Xiangbi Hill in Chengli Village. It was the first hall built by the migrating Cheng clan and the only Cheng hall recorded in the county gazetteer: 'Cheng Ancestral Hall, 35th District: first enshrines Run-er and Run-san.' The hall faces west, with Luositang Mountain to its east, where the tomb of the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan lies. During the Jiajing period, it was rebuilt into two courtyards (front and rear); in the Bingwu year of Daoguang, it was reconstructed as a five-bay, three-courtyard complex, with stone pillars replacing decayed wooden ones. According to local recollection: the hall was over 20 meters wide and nearly 50 meters deep, with one ceremonial gate, five bays of worship hall, nine bays of resting hall, and wing rooms on north and south sides. During the War of Resistance, Liu Ying, Secretary of the Zhejiang Provincial Committee of the CCP, was imprisoned in the hall and killed there on May 13, 1942. On January 21, 1992, the hall was demolished to make way for the Liu Ying Martyrs Cemetery. Through efforts of local Cheng descendants, some stone pillars, column bases, and lintels were transported to Wenlou Village for preservation and future reconstruction. Cheng Jialin, an elderly resident of Chengli Village, purchased some column bases that still stand outside his courtyard wall; others support stone slabs, becoming places for villagers to rest and chat. By the village pond and along paths, large stone blocks that once served as platform stones can still be seen; a laundry stone in the pond bears two vigorous regular-script characters 'zhi mu' (tomb). Elders no longer remember its origin, nor can the other half of the tombstone be found. These scattered traces record the glory of a building that stood at the village entrance for 500 years.

世远公祠及作勳公祠——岩下村程氏核心祠堂
Shiyuan Gong Hall and Zuoxun Gong Hall — Core Cheng Clan Halls of Yanxia Village
三、宗祠 | Ancestral Halls

要点 Highlights

  • 世远公祠：纪念程坦（1411—1477年），字世远，六世祖，岩下村程氏始祖
  • 作勳公祠：纪念程际烈（1646—1725年），十三世祖，又叫友恭常祠
  • 作勳公祠建于十八世纪中叶，世远公祠可能在随后一二十年
  • 两幢建筑轴线几乎垂直，世远公祠朝东南，作勳公祠朝西南
  • 世远巷（一米多宽小道）连接两祠
  • 源流塘（长方形水池）紧贴世远公祠东墙和南墙
  • 望屏桥（两侧有斜撑的平板石桥）
  • 世远公祠：长约26米宽约17米，五开间，抬梁式屋架，石柱（除前厅10根木柱）
  • 正堂得耕堂，八根圆柱配巨型覆盆柱础
  • 享堂五开间，地面比正堂高40公分，左昭右穆摆放牌位
  • 作勳公祠：三合院，正厅三开间抬梁式，东西厢房各三间穿斗式

世远公祠，俗称小祠堂，是为纪念程坦（1411—1477年）而建造的。程坦字世远，为永康程氏六世祖。作勳公指的是十三世祖程际烈（1646—1725年）。作勳公祠又叫友恭常祠，友常和恭常分别代表作勳公的两个儿子程伊臣（1691—1765年）和程经臣（1697—1771年）的房派。

村里的老人们已经不记得这两座祠堂具体的建造年代了，但是他们大多都很肯定一点，那就是虽然世远公比作勳公早出生二百多年，但是作勳公祠建在世远公祠之前，并且它们都比不远处的绍常祠堂要早。从两个祠堂的相互关系上看，世远公祠位于作勳公祠前，并挡住了其正门，也从侧面支持了世远公祠建造较晚的说法。现住在作勳公祠里的程茂发（1934年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，作勳公祠是程伊臣和程经臣为其父亲共同建造的，所以又叫友恭常祠；而世远公祠是作勳公的孙子建造的。这种说法听起来也不无道理，而且在时间上也是成立的，即作勳公祠建于十八世纪中叶，世远公祠可能在随后的一二十年后。而曾参加过抗美援朝的程龙兴（1930年生，圣字辈，程氏22代）则振振有词地说，世远公祠是在明嘉靖年间（1522—1566年），由世远公的孙子建造的。家谱中记载，世远公的长孙生活的年代为1494—1568年，所以这在时间上也是可能的。

这两座祠堂的先后建成，标志着岩下村已经从一个杂姓聚居的村落，转变成了一个程氏的血缘村落。之所以人们为作勳公建造祠堂，不仅是因为程际烈家境富足，更是因为他资雄里闬，每遇岁歉必出粟赈饥，凡有求者缓急靡不相济。人皆德之。不仅如此，程际烈的两个儿子也广受乡亲的尊敬。家谱记载其长子伊臣文艺精通，并且独修宗谱；次子经臣善承家业。为世远公建造祠堂则意义更为重大，岩下村的程氏一直奉世远公为始祖，村里新修好的家谱就叫《世远公宗谱》。究其原因为，虽然三世祖程权是岩下村的始迁祖，但是他的子孙除程坦外，即世远公，均外迁他乡。自六世祖程坦以后，这支程氏才世代留居在了岩下村。

这两座祠堂位于上坑口外，老街以西。方岩溪在它们的西边，紧贴着世远公祠的后墙绕了一个半圆后，继续向北。这两幢建筑的轴线几乎相互垂直，世远公祠朝东南，作勳公祠朝西南。外墙之间只有一条一米多宽的小道，叫世远巷。紧贴着世远公祠的东墙和部分南墙，有一个长方形的水池，叫源流塘。水塘本身与世远公祠或作勳公祠之间不存在诸如轴线、对称或呼应等空间关系，却像是世远公祠吃掉了水塘的一个角。所以世远公祠也不能像其他祠堂一样，把入口冠冕堂皇地设在正中，而是设在了东北角上。即便如此，还是有一部分入口，落在了水塘边界以内。世远公祠的另外一个入口，也是紧贴着水塘，位于侧立面的东南角上。次入口门外不远处，方岩溪缓缓流过，祠堂入口几乎正对着溪上的一座小桥。这是一座两侧有斜撑的平板石桥，叫望屏桥。过桥不远，便是绍常祠堂。

这组祠堂建筑群，没有一般宗祠建筑所具有的那种威严和霸气，反倒是十分生动而亲切。其灵活与不对称的空间构成，不禁让人想起著名的圣马可广场。祠堂建筑和老街之间有一个院落的距离，这样极好地回避了香客们的喧嚣。走在老街上，通过两座院落之间的世远巷，也可以隐约看到祠堂和水塘的一角。这两个祠堂，以及源流塘所围合的空间，虽然不大，却是村里重要的公共空间之一。经常可以看到附近的村民来这里浣洗衣物；到了吃饭的时间就会有人坐在水塘边的石板上吃饭；也会有人在水塘和作勳公祠之间不大的空地上晒晒干柴。

按村里的说法，世远公祠是全村人都有份的祠堂。它的平面布局及构造，代表这个地区的一种普遍形式。祠堂长约26米，宽约17米，在横向上有五个开间。中间三个开间纵向全部开敞，直接暴露抬梁式屋架，从前到后分别是前厅，天井，正堂，戏台及左右两个小天井，最后是享堂。侧面是穿斗结构的厢房，并有夹层。除了前厅里面的十根木柱外，其余的柱子均为石柱。三种不同尺寸的柱子与五种规格各异的柱础之间的不同搭配，间接界定了空间的主次。

最重要的空间当然是正堂，正堂位于三个天井之间，有八根圆柱并辅以有覆盆的巨型柱础。正堂的堂号依旧取三世祖程权终身得耕陇亩足矣中的得耕二字，为得耕堂。厢房和享堂内的柱子均为方柱，但是只有正堂两侧的柱子和享堂中间的四根，才配以一种规格较高的方形柱础；余下的只是采用很简单的，大小和柱子截面相当的四面倒锥形柱础。前厅中间面向正堂的两根檐柱采用的是石圆柱，辅以鼓形柱础。前厅内余下的柱子皆为较细的木柱，柱础也采用规格更小的鼓形柱础。

正堂两个前金柱内侧分别写有六世衍宗祧有典有则，千秋隆孝享是训是行，后金柱上的对联则在前面，为后嗣相承祖德与方山并寿，前徽弗替孙谋偕丽水俱长。得耕堂的匾也挂在后金柱之间，略向下倾斜。很显然，这两副对联和横匾共同指向的地方，就是程氏子孙在祠堂里双膝碰地的地方。

享堂宽五开间，两侧没有厢房，它的地面比正堂高四十公分。享堂后面原本是按照左昭右穆摆放牌位的地方，虽然这些木构的台面、神龛现已全部缺失，但是从柱子侧面的榫口可以推断出它们的位置和高度。以前老街上如果有程氏男丁过世，家人就要做一个牌位送到世远公祠里来；如果是友恭常下的后人，还要做一个送到作勳公祠；如果又是恭下毅常的，则要做第三个送到绍常祠堂；对于已经结婚的男丁，则要做夫妻牌位。如果当时妻子还在世，则用一块红布把妻子的牌位暂时罩起来；如果妻子先走一步，则要等到丈夫去世后一起进祠堂。

正堂两侧的厢房主要用于储藏一些祭祀用品。二层的夹层则是堆放一些大型的器物，例如每年舞龙灯时用的龙头和龙尾。祠堂南墙外还有一个不规则的小屋，平时是教书先生办公及居住的地方，在过年和清明的时候用做灶堂。

作勳公祠的建筑规格则小许多，简单地说就是一个三合院。正厅为三开间，抬梁式结构，尺度和世远公祠正堂的尺寸十分接近。东西厢房各三间，为穿斗式结构，靠南的两间有夹层。厢房的首间和正厅围合形成了一个进深约三米的内院。

自从1958年生产大跃进后，作勳公的房产由四家人共有。据住在西厢房的程茂发（1934年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，他的继父程宣占由于很穷，在1946年便分到了作勳公祠的一部分房产，他和继父，以及生父一家人就住了进来。在这以前由于家里穷没有房，也会到祠堂里租房子住。之后，又有三家人买房。由于居住的需要，作勳公祠如今被划分出了许多小的隔间。但是据程茂发老人讲，以前祠堂里的正房都是开敞的，现在各家用来当作门窗的隔板就是原来正堂里排位前面的门板拆下来的；祠堂里以前有一块写着贡元的匾，不幸在文革期间被损坏。

— —

Shiyuan Gong Hall, commonly called the Small Hall, was built to commemorate Cheng Tan (1411–1477), courtesy name Shiyuan, the sixth-generation ancestor of the Yongkang Cheng clan. Zuoxun Gong refers to the 13th-generation ancestor Cheng Jilie (1646–1725). Zuoxun Gong Hall is also called Yougongchang Hall—'Youchang' and 'Gongchang' representing the branches of his two sons Cheng Yichen (1691–1765) and Cheng Jingchen (1697–1771).

Village elders no longer remember the exact construction dates, but most agree that although Shiyuan Gong was born over 200 years before Zuoxun Gong, Zuoxun Gong Hall was built first, and both predate the nearby Shaochang Hall. The spatial relationship—Shiyuan Gong Hall stands in front of Zuoxun Gong Hall, blocking its main entrance—supports the later construction of Shiyuan Gong Hall. Cheng Maofa (b. 1934, 21st generation), who lives in Zuoxun Gong Hall, says it was jointly built by Cheng Yichen and Cheng Jingchen for their father (hence 'Yougongchang'), while Shiyuan Gong Hall was built by Zuoxun Gong's grandson—plausible, with Zuoxun Gong Hall dating to the mid-18th century and Shiyuan Gong Hall a decade or two later. Cheng Longxing (b. 1930, 22nd generation), a Korean War veteran, asserts Shiyuan Gong Hall was built during the Jiajing period (1522–1566) by Shiyuan Gong's grandson; genealogy records Shiyuan Gong's eldest grandson as living 1494–1568, making this also possible.

The sequential construction of these two halls marks Yanxia Village's transition from a multi-surname settlement to a Cheng clan kinship village. Zuoxun Gong was honored not only for wealth but for 'being prominent in the village, always distributing grain during famines, and helping all who sought aid.' His two sons were also respected: the eldest Yichen was 'skilled in arts and letters and solely revised the genealogy'; the second Jingchen 'ably inherited the family estate.' Building Shiyuan Gong Hall was even more significant—the Yanxia Cheng clan has always revered Shiyuan Gong as their founding ancestor, and the newly revised genealogy is called the Shiyuan Gong Genealogy. Although the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan was the first to settle in Yanxia, all his descendants except Cheng Tan (Shiyuan Gong) migrated elsewhere; only from the sixth-generation Cheng Tan onward did this branch remain in Yanxia for generations.

These two halls lie outside the Shangkeng ravine mouth, west of the old street. Fangyan Stream, to their west, hugs Shiyuan Gong Hall's rear wall in a semicircle before continuing north. The buildings' axes are nearly perpendicular: Shiyuan Gong Hall faces southeast, Zuoxun Gong Hall faces southwest. Between their exterior walls runs a narrow lane just over a meter wide called Shiyuan Lane. Against Shiyuan Gong Hall's east and part of its south wall lies a rectangular pond called Yuanliu Pond (Source-Flow Pond). The pond has no axial, symmetrical, or responsive spatial relationship to either hall—it appears as if Shiyuan Gong Hall 'ate away' a corner of the pond. Consequently, unlike other halls, Shiyuan Gong Hall's main entrance is not centered but at the northeast corner, with part of it falling within the pond's boundary. A second entrance, also hugging the pond, lies at the southeast corner of the side facade. Beyond it, Fangyan Stream flows slowly, and the entrance nearly faces a small bridge—a flat stone slab bridge with diagonal supports on both sides called Wangping Bridge (Screen-Viewing Bridge). A short distance beyond the bridge stands Shaochang Hall.

This hall complex lacks the imposing grandeur typical of ancestral halls; instead it is vivid and intimate. Its flexible, asymmetrical spatial composition evokes the famous Piazza San Marco. The halls are set one courtyard back from the old street, well away from pilgrim noise. From the street, through Shiyuan Lane between the two courtyards, one glimpses a corner of the halls and pond. The space enclosed by the two halls and Yuanliu Pond, though small, is an important village public space: villagers come to wash clothes; at mealtime people sit on stones by the pond to eat; others dry firewood in the small open area between the pond and Zuoxun Gong Hall.

By village account, Shiyuan Gong Hall belongs to all villagers. Its plan and construction represent a common regional form. The hall is about 26 meters long and 17 meters wide, with five bays across. The central three bays are fully open longitudinally, exposing the post-and-lintel (tailiang) roof frame: from front to rear—front hall, courtyard, main hall, stage with two small side courtyards, and finally the resting hall. The sides are column-and-tie (chuandou) wing rooms with mezzanines. Except for ten wooden pillars in the front hall, all pillars are stone. Three pillar sizes paired with five distinct column-base types indirectly define spatial hierarchy.

The most important space is the main hall, situated between three courtyards, with eight cylindrical pillars on massive inverted-bowl column bases. Its name, Degeng Hall (Cultivating-the-Land Hall), derives from the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan's lifelong maxim 'content to cultivate the fields.' Wing rooms and the resting hall use square pillars, but only the pillars flanking the main hall and the four central pillars of the resting hall have high-grade square bases; the rest use simple four-sided tapered bases matching pillar cross-section. The two front eaves pillars of the front hall facing the main hall are stone cylinders on drum-shaped bases; the remaining front-hall pillars are thinner wooden pillars on smaller drum bases.

Inside the two front gold pillars of the main hall is inscribed: 'Six generations extend the lineage, with rites and rules; a thousand years exalt filial sacrifice, with teachings and conduct.' On the rear gold pillars: 'Descendants inherit ancestral virtue, enduring with Fang Mountain; former glories persist, grandsons' plans enduring with Li River.' The Degeng Hall plaque hangs between the rear gold pillars, tilted slightly downward. Clearly, these couplets and plaque point to the spot where Cheng descendants kneel in the hall.

The resting hall has five bays with no side wings, its floor 40 centimeters higher than the main hall. Behind it, tablets were originally arranged by the 'left zhao, right mu' generational system; although wooden platforms and shrines are now gone, their positions and heights can be inferred from mortises on pillar sides. Formerly, when a Cheng male died, his family made a tablet for Shiyuan Gong Hall; if a Yougongchang descendant, an additional one for Zuoxun Gong Hall; if also a Gongxia Yichang descendant, a third for Shaochang Hall. Married men received husband-and-wife tablets; if the wife was still living, her tablet was temporarily covered with red cloth; if she died first, it waited to enter the hall with her husband.

The wing rooms flanking the main hall mainly store ritual supplies. The second-floor mezzanines hold large objects such as the dragon head and tail used in annual dragon lantern dances. An irregular small building outside the hall's south wall served as the schoolmaster's office and residence, and as a kitchen during New Year and Qingming festivals.

Zuoxun Gong Hall is much smaller—simply a three-sided courtyard. The main hall has three bays in post-and-lintel construction, with dimensions close to Shiyuan Gong Hall's main hall. The east and west wings each have three bays in column-and-tie construction, with the two southern bays having mezzanines. The first bay of each wing and the main hall enclose an inner courtyard about three meters deep.

After the 1958 Great Leap Forward, Zuoxun Gong's property was shared by four families. According to Cheng Maofa (b. 1934), who lives in the west wing, his stepfather Cheng Xuanzhan, being very poor, was allocated part of the hall in 1946, and he moved in with his stepfather and biological family. Before that, too poor to own a house, they had rented rooms in the hall. Three other families later bought in. For residential needs, the hall is now divided into many small compartments. But Cheng Maofa recalls that the main hall was originally fully open; the panels now used as doors and windows by various families were originally the door panels in front of the ancestral tablets in the main hall. A plaque reading 'Gongyuan' (Tribute Scholar) was unfortunately damaged during the Cultural Revolution.

绍常祠堂——恭下毅常房派祠堂
Shaochang Ancestral Hall — Hall of the Gongxia Yichang Branch
三、宗祠 | Ancestral Halls

要点 Highlights

  • 程立重（1736—1793年）为报答丈人赠银之恩，于乾隆五十三年（1788年）建造
  • 程立重字逢时号毅庵，作勳公之孙，程经臣之子，排行最小（毅常=小常=绍常）
  • 坐北朝南，位于方岩溪以西，背后斜对方岩山
  • 平面布局和世远公祠基本相似，总面积却是世远公祠的两倍
  • 正立面左右两端高低错落马头墙，主入口居中，两边次入口
  • 明堂四面有廊，十二根檐柱，每根檐柱上方有雕卷龙/鸟兽/花木的牛腿
  • 牛腿上有精美叠斗，叠斗外侧镂雕插叶，内侧盘龙形蟮梁
  • 正堂三间九檩进深，明间四柱前后双步梁加中间五架梁
  • 两根上金檩、前下金檩、檐檩和挑檐枋底面有镂空浮雕花鸟鱼纹
  • 享堂五开间，地面两步高台基，门上雕二十四孝
  • 戏台两侧各有近方形天井，厢房自南向北分五个空间

据说绍常祠堂是程立重（1736—1793年）为报答丈人在岩脚竖花厅的赠银之恩，于乾隆五十三年（1788年）建造的。程立重字逢时，号毅庵，为作勳公之孙，程经臣之子。程经臣的四个儿子各以发、强、刚、毅为房派，所以程立重的后代又被称为恭下毅常。由于程立重排行最小，所以毅常又被俗称为小常。永康话小与绍同音，所以得名为绍常祠堂。

绍常祠堂坐北朝南，位于方岩溪以西，背后斜对着方岩山。在建造绍常祠堂以前，这可能只是一片田地。在绍常祠堂建成以后的很长一段时间里，都是孤零零地独自坐落在方岩溪的这一侧。

祠堂前面有一块较大的空场，这里既是秋收后村里人晒谷子的草场，又是正月里迎龙灯的高潮所在。空场的南面还有一幢几十年前建的电影院，如今家家都有了电视，电影院便冷清了。空场的东缘由方岩溪界定，溪水东岸就是程立重丈人出资修建的，有屋里塘、屋里井，有花园、菜地的大宅院。在空场的南、北两端各有一个小桥横跨方岩溪通往宅院。从绍常祠堂向北不远，还可以通过望屏桥走到世远公祠和作勳公祠。

绍常祠堂的平面布局和世远公祠基本相似，正堂和享堂的尺寸也相当，但是总面积却是世远公祠的两倍。与世远公祠相比，绍常祠堂在平面上的主要区别体现在如下几点：前厅和厢房进深较深，厢房和轴线上的主要空间之间局部有走廊，三个主要天井面积都比较大，在正堂两侧的厢房之间还各有一个有楼梯的小天井。

祠堂的正立面左右两端为高低错落的马头墙，主入口在中间，两边各有一个次入口连接厢房和前厅的走廊。除此之外，在这青砖墙上还高低错落地安插了几个方形和圆形的小窗，主要用来给尽端厢房和前厅次间通风。

前厅明间通高，次间二层的空间与两侧厢房相连。明间用月梁，前后单步梁，中间双步梁上又立童柱，上层采用穿斗结构。穿过前厅是和前厅同宽的明堂，明堂四面有廊，有十二根檐柱环绕。每一根檐柱上方均有一个雕着卷龙、鸟兽或花木的牛腿，牛腿上面还有一组精美的叠斗，用以支撑上面的挑檐枋。叠斗外侧均有镂雕插叶，内侧为盘龙形蟮梁。

正堂三间梁架全部露明，为九檩进深。明间有四柱，采用前后双步梁加中间五架梁；次间用五柱，穿斗与抬梁相结合，柱间有四组双步梁。正堂整个屋顶的重量依次通过檩枋、叠斗、大斗、大斗下的花墩、月梁、梁托，最后传到柱子上。在支撑檩枋的叠斗和柱子之间，还用蟮梁做连接，蟮梁不但有极强的装饰效果，还能起到在纵向上稳定结构的作用。除了蟮梁以外，叠斗、雀替、花墩、梁头和梁托都是绍常祠堂大木结构雕饰的重点。此外，在两根上金檩、前下金檩、檐檩和挑檐枋底面还有精美的镂空浮雕花鸟鱼纹。

正堂后面就是一个平面为方形的戏台，戏台两侧各有一个近方形的天井。从这里开始，厢房的进深变浅，从而使轴线上的主要空间可以拓宽成五开间。

享堂五开间，位于一个两步高的台基上。享堂的梁架结构和正堂大体类似。不同的是前后的双步梁变成了单步梁；后面的单步梁外面又增加了穿枋结构的一个廊，作为摆放神橱的空间；明间前面由于需要和戏台衔接，所以对梁架做了一些变化。享堂中间三开间在金柱间设门，但是可以全部开敞。据老人们讲，门上曾雕有精美的二十四孝，用以教育逢年过节来这里祭拜的程氏子孙。享堂的大木雕饰比正堂的稍简洁，在檩下叠斗之间没有用蟮梁，只是在叠斗两侧饰卷草形插叶。

东西厢房自南向北分别被分隔成大小不同的五个空间。中间三间各自朝向不同的天井开窗采光，尽端的两间则利用外墙开窗通风。厢房与前厅、一进天井、正堂之间均有走廊，在正堂侧壁后面的厢房之间还各有一个小天井，天井一侧各有一个楼梯通往二层。

— —

Shaochang Hall is said to have been built by Cheng Lizhong (1736–1793) in the 53rd year of Qianlong (1788), to repay his father-in-law's gift of silver for 'building a flower hall at the foot of the cliff.' Cheng Lizhong, courtesy name Fengshi, art name Yi'an, was the grandson of Zuoxun Gong and son of Cheng Jingchen. Cheng Jingchen's four sons established the Fa, Qiang, Gang, and Yi branches; Cheng Lizhong's descendants are called Gongxia Yichang. As the youngest son, his branch was called 'Xiaochang' (Small Branch); in Yongkang dialect 'xiao' and 'shao' are homophones, hence the name 'Shaochang Hall.'

Shaochang Hall faces south, west of Fangyan Stream, with its back angled toward Fangyan Mountain. Before its construction, this was likely just farmland. For a long time after completion, it stood alone on this side of Fangyan Stream.

In front of the hall is a large open space—used as a threshing ground after autumn harvest and as the climax venue for the first-month dragon lantern festival. South of the space stands a cinema built decades ago, now quiet since every household has a television. The east edge is defined by Fangyan Stream; across it lies the grand compound built by Cheng Lizhong's father-in-law, with its in-house pond, in-house well, garden, and vegetable plots. Small bridges at the north and south ends of the open space cross the stream to the compound. A short distance north from Shaochang Hall, Wangping Bridge leads to Shiyuan Gong Hall and Zuoxun Gong Hall.

Shaochang Hall's plan resembles Shiyuan Gong Hall's, with similar main hall and resting hall dimensions, but its total area is twice as large. Key differences: deeper front hall and wings, partial corridors between wings and axial spaces, larger main courtyards, and small courtyards with staircases between the wings flanking the main hall.

The front facade has staggered horse-head gable walls at both ends, a central main entrance, and two side entrances connecting wings to the front hall corridor. The blue-brick wall also features staggered square and round small windows for ventilating the end wings and front hall side bays.

The front hall's central bay is full-height; the second-floor space of the side bays connects with the wings. The central bay uses crescent beams (yueliang), with front and rear single-step beams and a central double-step beam supporting child pillars; the upper level uses column-and-tie construction. Beyond the front hall lies a courtyard (mingtang) of the same width, surrounded by corridors on four sides with twelve eaves pillars. Above each eaves pillar is a corbel (niutui) carved with coiled dragons, birds and beasts, or flowers and trees; above each corbel is a set of refined stacked brackets (diedou) supporting the projecting eave purlin. The outer sides of the stacked brackets have openwork leaf carvings; the inner sides have dragon-form shan beams.

The three-bay main hall has fully exposed roof framing, nine purlins deep. The central bay has four pillars with front and rear double-step beams plus a central five-purlin beam; the side bays use five pillars in a combined column-and-tie and post-and-lintel system, with four sets of double-step beams between pillars. The entire roof load transfers sequentially through purlins, stacked brackets, large brackets, carved blocks beneath large brackets, crescent beams, and beam brackets to the pillars. Between the stacked brackets supporting purlins and the pillars, shan beams provide connection—both highly decorative and structurally stabilizing longitudinally. Besides shan beams, stacked brackets, sparrow brackets (queti), carved blocks, beam heads, and beam brackets are the focus of large-timber ornamentation. Additionally, the undersides of two upper gold purlins, the front lower gold purlin, the eaves purlin, and the projecting eave purlin feature refined openwork relief carvings of birds, flowers, and fish.

Behind the main hall is a square stage, with nearly square courtyards on each side. From here, the wings become shallower, allowing the axial main space to widen to five bays.

The five-bay resting hall stands on a two-step-high platform. Its framing resembles the main hall's, except that the front and rear double-step beams become single-step beams; behind the rear single-step beam, an additional column-and-tie corridor serves as space for shrine cabinets. The central bay's front framing is modified to connect with the stage. The central three bays have doors between gold pillars that can be fully opened. According to elders, the doors were once carved with the refined 'Twenty-Four Paragons of Filial Piety' to educate Cheng descendants who came to worship during festivals. The resting hall's large-timber ornamentation is slightly simpler than the main hall's—no shan beams between purlins and stacked brackets, only scrolling-foliage leaf carvings on the bracket sides.

The east and west wings are divided from south to north into five spaces of varying sizes. The middle three open onto different courtyards for light; the end two use exterior wall windows. Corridors connect the wings to the front hall, first courtyard, and main hall; between the wings behind the main hall's side walls are small courtyards, each with a staircase to the second floor.

周氏宗祠——草墅周氏始祖祠堂
Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall — Hall of the Caoshu Zhou Founding Ancestor
三、宗祠 | Ancestral Halls

要点 Highlights

  • 老街上唯一非程姓祠堂
  • 始祖周时文公（1254—1315年），元至元十七年至二十八年（1280—1291年）任两浙提举
  • 从金华泮塘迁居永康升平乡草墅，称草墅周氏
  • 始祖墓位于祠堂后公道山狮子撰屙洞穴里
  • 光绪三十四年（1908年）重修
  • 接近方形四合院，临街两侧马头山墙，正门居中两旁偏门
  • 一进三开间七檩拜厅（雕饰最精美），天井后高台五开间享堂
  • 两侧各五间厢房供祭祖者暂住
  • 祠外两旁路亭（跨老街架设），楼屋八间守墓人住所
  • 浙江省国民政府驻方岩时作为浙江省警察大队办公场所，含小型监狱
  • 守墓人周岩好夫妇及其大嫂居住，曾祖起守墓逾百年

周氏宗祠位于狮子山西麓的一个小山坳里，坐东朝西，紧邻老街。祠堂的位置是这段老街地势最高的部分，它和方岩山之间没有任何建筑物遮挡，有与方岩对视、俯瞰山谷的感觉。

周氏宗祠是老街上唯一一座非程姓的祠堂。据《游川周氏宗谱》记载，这支周氏被称为草墅周氏。其始祖时文公（1254—1315年）在元至元十七年至二十八年（1280—1291年）期间任两浙提举，因奉敕兼管督理台温分司盐课，秩满道经永康，爱其风俗之美，所以从金华泮塘迁居永康升平乡草墅，由此得名为草墅周氏。自此，时文公的子孙便从草墅迁往永康各地。

周氏宗祠建在这里是因为始祖周时文公墓位于它后面不远处，一个叫公道山狮子撰屙的洞穴里。据说周姓人家在老街上只居住了很短的时间，并在程氏迁到这里之前就离开了；也有人说周氏从未在这里居住过，只是在清明的前十天会有上百人来这里祭奠周氏始祖。《游川周氏宗谱》中记载这里原来只有平厅三楹，厅前又有庵屋数间，用以在清明时给远道来祭祖的子孙提供休息场所。后来由游川、岘川、璋川三宗扩建成现在的规模，现存建筑为光绪三十四年（1908年）重修。在国民党浙江省政府迁驻方岩的时候，这里曾作为浙江省警察大队的办公场所，其中还有一个小型的监狱。

周氏宗祠是一个接近方形的四合院。临街一面两侧是马头山墙，白色的墙头上曾绘有彩画，但是如今只能依稀看见斑驳的色彩。正门居中，两旁各有一个偏门通向院内走廊。一进为三开间、七檩进深的拜厅，这是整幢建筑中雕饰最为精美的地方；天井后面，高台之上为五开间、进深略浅的享堂；两侧各有五间厢房供前来祭祀者暂住。不仅如此，祠外两旁小厅，并楼屋八间，为看守者安身之宅也。这小厅指的是周氏宗祠两边，跨过老街架设的路亭，这两个路亭是老街上为处不多的既能避风雨，又能小憩的地方。楼屋八间现在只有三间归周氏所有，守墓人周岩好夫妇及其大嫂现居住在这里。家谱中记载，祠墓应由游川、岘川、璋川三宗轮流派可靠能干的子孙驻守，而岩好公为游川小四房子孙，自其曾祖起住守始祖公祠墓至今已逾百年。

— —

The Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall sits in a small hollow at the western foot of Lion Mountain, facing east, directly adjacent to the old street. It occupies the highest point of this section of the street, with no buildings between it and Fangyan Mountain—offering a view facing Fangyan and overlooking the valley.

It is the only non-Cheng ancestral hall on the old street. According to the Youchuan Zhou Genealogy, this branch is called the Caoshu Zhou. Their founding ancestor, Lord Shiwen (1254–1315), served as Commissioner of the Two Zhe Provinces from 1280 to 1291. While overseeing salt taxation for the Taizhou-Wenzhou branch, he passed through Yongkang after his term and, charmed by its customs, moved from Pantang in Jinhua to Caoshu in Shengping Township, Yongkang—hence the name Caoshu Zhou. His descendants subsequently spread from Caoshu to various parts of Yongkang.

The hall was built here because the tomb of founding ancestor Zhou Shiwen lies nearby, in a cave called 'Lion Excreting' on Gongdao Mountain behind the hall. According to one account, the Zhou family lived on the old street only briefly, leaving before the Cheng clan arrived; another says they never lived there, but a hundred or more people would come ten days before Qingming to worship the founding ancestor. The genealogy records that originally there were only three bays of a plain hall, with several nun's huts in front to provide rest for descendants traveling to worship during Qingming. Later, the Youchuan, Xianchuan, and Zhangchuan branches expanded it to its current scale; the existing building was rebuilt in the 34th year of Guangxu (1908). When the Nationalist Zhejiang Provincial Government was stationed at Fangyan, it served as the office of the Zhejiang Provincial Police Brigade, including a small prison.

The hall is a nearly square four-sided courtyard. The street-facing side has horse-head gable walls on both ends; the white gable tops once bore colored paintings, now only faintly visible as mottled patches. The main gate is centered, with side gates on each side leading to interior corridors. The first section is a three-bay, seven-purlin-deep worship hall—the most ornately carved part of the building. Beyond the courtyard, on a high platform, stands a five-bay, slightly shallower resting hall. Five wing rooms on each side provide lodging for visiting worshippers. Additionally, 'small halls on both sides outside the shrine, plus eight two-story rooms, serve as dwellings for the caretakers.' The 'small halls' refer to roadside pavilions on both sides of the hall, spanning the old street—among the few places on the street offering shelter from rain and a place to rest. Of the eight two-story rooms, only three now belong to the Zhou clan; tomb keeper Zhou Yanhao, his wife, and his sister-in-law live there. The genealogy records that the hall and tomb should be guarded in rotation by capable descendants of the Youchuan, Xianchuan, and Zhangchuan branches; Yanhao is a descendant of the Youchuan Xiaosi branch, and his family has guarded the founding ancestor's hall and tomb for over a century, starting from his great-grandfather.

其他祠堂——闰二宗祠、程昭夫祠堂、启文公祠、禄房祠堂
Other Halls — Run'er Hall, Cheng Zhaofu Hall, Qiwengong Hall, and Lufang Hall
三、宗祠 | Ancestral Halls

要点 Highlights

  • 闰二宗祠：橙麓村东侧，明代始建，三进正堂品字形天井，土改归国有
  • 闰二宗祠：先后做小学校、橙麓村老年协会活动场所，2006年修葺，钢桁架屋顶
  • 程昭夫祠堂：狮子山西麓老街西侧，1924年程昭夫建，程振兴旅店一部分
  • 程昭夫祠堂：形制和世远公祠基本相同，平面尺寸略大
  • 程昭夫祠堂：香汛做客房，中间开敞空间做厅堂、晚宴、唱戏
  • 程昭夫祠堂：浙江省政府驻方岩时承办政府会议、筵席、婚礼
  • 启文公祠：回龙桥西侧，程昭纯（1864生，字启文）建，1900年开程成兴雨伞
  • 启文公祠：布局原本和世远公祠类似，现仅剩中间三开间
  • 禄房祠堂：下胡坑坑口靠西，程昭纯主持修建并任管事
  • 禄房祠堂：规模比世远公祠略小，前后两进院落，约建于二十世纪初
  • 禄房祠堂：文革期间疏于维护，毁于白蚁和火灾，仅存残垣断壁

闰二宗祠位于橙麓村东侧，始建于明代。从建筑布局上看，应该和世远公祠类似，即三进正堂、品字形天井布局。基于土改时期的政策，闰二宗祠属于五代以上的祠堂，被归为国有。于是它先是作为小学校，又在1988年改作橙麓村老年协会的活动场所。2006年，祠堂内部被修葺一新，地面和部分墙面用混凝土重做，中部的大跨度屋顶也改用钢桁架结构。

程昭夫祠堂位于狮子山西麓，老街西侧。由程昭夫建于1924年，为程振兴旅店的一部分。祠堂形制和世远公祠基本相同，平面尺寸略大。祠堂在香汛时期也做客房使用，中间开敞空间不仅作为旅店的厅堂来招待香客、设摆晚宴，晚上还会请艺人在这里唱戏。浙江省国民政府临时迁驻方岩那段时间，这里还是承办政府会议、各式筵席甚至举办婚礼的场所。

启文公祠位于回龙桥西侧，其建造者为程振兴旅店老板的弟弟程昭纯。程昭纯（1864生，卒年不详），字启文，为程乐涞五子，并于1900年在老街南端开办了程成兴雨伞。据程昭纯孙程振锋（1939年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，祠堂布局原本和世远公祠类似。现在可惜只剩下了中间的三开间。

老街上还有一个几乎已经被人们忘记的祠堂，这就是位于下胡坑坑口靠西的禄房祠堂。据程振锋老人回忆，禄房祠堂是其祖父程昭纯主持修建的，并作为祠堂管事。祠堂的规模比世远公祠略小，只有前后两进院落。这样算来，祠堂大约建于二十世纪初。不幸的是在文革期间，由于疏于维护，这座祠堂最终毁于白蚁和火灾。目前仅存一些残垣断壁，连基本的平面布局也无法辨认。

— —

Run'er Hall lies east of Chengli Village, first built in the Ming dynasty. Its layout likely resembled Shiyuan Gong Hall's—three main halls with a pin-shaped (品) courtyard arrangement. Under land reform policy, as a hall of five or more generations, it was nationalized. It first served as a primary school, then in 1988 became the activity venue for the Chengli Village Senior Citizens Association. In 2006, the interior was renovated: floors and部分 walls were redone in concrete, and the central long-span roof was replaced with a steel truss structure.

Cheng Zhaofu Hall stands at the western foot of Lion Mountain, west of the old street. Built by Cheng Zhaofu in 1924, it formed part of Chengzhenxing Inn. Its form matches Shiyuan Gong Hall's, with slightly larger plan dimensions. During pilgrimage season it served as guest rooms; its central open space functioned as the inn's hall for receiving pilgrims and hosting banquets, and in the evenings hosted opera performances. During the Nationalist Zhejiang Provincial Government's temporary residence at Fangyan, it hosted government meetings, various banquets, and even weddings.

Qiwengong Hall lies west of Huilong Bridge, built by Cheng Zhaochun—younger brother of the Chengzhenxing Inn owner. Cheng Zhaochun (b. 1864, death date unknown), courtesy name Qiwen, was the fifth son of Cheng Lelai and opened the Chengchengxing Umbrella Shop at the south end of the old street in 1900. According to his grandson Cheng Zhenfeng (b. 1939, 21st generation), the hall's layout originally resembled Shiyuan Gong Hall's. Unfortunately, only the central three bays remain today.

Another nearly forgotten hall is Lufang Hall, west of the Xiahukeng ravine mouth. According to Cheng Zhenfeng, it was built under the supervision of his grandfather Cheng Zhaochun, who served as its manager. Slightly smaller than Shiyuan Gong Hall, it had only front and rear courtyards, dating it to the early 20th century. Tragically, during the Cultural Revolution, neglect led to its destruction by termites and fire. Only ruined walls remain, with even the basic plan unrecognizable.

宗教建筑——经堂、本保庙、万成庙
Religious Architecture — Jingtang Nunnery, Benbao Temple, and Wancheng Temple
四、宗教建筑 | Religious Architecture

要点 Highlights

  • 方岩山广慈寺和佑顺侯祠香火旺盛，老街上宗教建筑相对不发达
  • 泛宗教信仰：胡公大帝后坐如来佛，玉皇大帝脚下供地藏菩萨，广慈寺彩画西游记
  • 经堂：下胡坑溪以南原城头以南老街以西，尼姑驻守，东西向合院，大殿供观音、关公、土地
  • 经堂：每月初一、十五烧香请愿抽签，二十多年前被付之一炬
  • 本保庙（土地庙）：原蜥蜴山前山头大柏树下，石围小神龛供朱相公（朱丹溪原型）
  • 朱丹溪（1281—1358年），名震亨字彦修，滋阴派创始人，金元四大名医之一
  • 朱相公被尊为胡则十兄弟之一，与其他九兄弟供广慈寺前殿胡则像两侧
  • 解放后新建本保庙（南山庙）：蜈蚣山第二个小山包上，一进小院三开间正殿
  • 本保庙正殿：中间朱相公及夫人，左土地公土地婆，右财神公财神婆
  • 万成庙（观音庙）：五峰鸡鸣峰后山坳，入口隐蔽，走十多分钟见正殿
  • 万成庙正殿三开间利用岩体洞穴依山而建，殿内供送子观音（抱男童）、观音菩萨、千手千眼观音
  • 万成庙：较高洞穴前三间僧房曾为尼姑住所，角落供地藏菩萨
  • 万成庙：浙江省政府驻方岩时为浙江省史料征集委员会办公场所，阮毅成阮公馆在此

由于方岩山上的广慈寺和佑顺侯祠历来香火旺盛，村里的人如果遇到了什么大事，就会上山去求菩萨和胡公。对许多人来讲，胡公就是一个能管天管地、神通广大的万能神。即使是对胡公将信将疑的村民，也多会在正月里上山请三炷香、求个签，保佑自己和家人在新的一年能欢欢喜喜、平平安安。正因为如此，岩下老街上的宗教建筑相对并不发达，只有几个并不重要的小庙。老街上的人对求神拜佛的态度，充分体现了汉人普遍见庙就烧香，见菩萨就拜的泛宗教信仰。这一特点也会生动地体现在这里的宗教建筑上，例如，方岩山上胡公大帝后面端坐着如来佛，玉皇大帝脚下供着地藏菩萨，广慈寺前厅屋顶彩画《西游记》，山下观音旁边陪坐关公和土地等。

经堂：经堂原位于下胡坑溪以南，即原城头以南，老街以西的位置，由尼姑常年驻守。听老人们讲，经堂原是一幢东西向的合院建筑，大殿内原主供观音，观音两旁供关公和土地。以前每月初一、十五都会有人去烧香、请愿、抽签。不幸的是，经堂在二十多年前被付之一炬。

本保庙：本保庙，俗称土地庙，通常是用来供奉民间神祇的小庙。岩下村的本保庙原本位于蜥蜴山，前山头上的一棵大柏树下，面向它所保护的村落。所谓庙，其实仅仅是一个用石头围合的，坐南朝北，供奉着朱相公的小神龛。据民间传说，朱相公的原型为朱丹溪。记载，朱丹溪（1281—1358年），名震亨，字彦修，元代金华人，因世居丹溪，故人称朱丹溪。作为滋阴派的创始人，他被称为金元四大名医之一，并于去世后成为当地的主要民间神祇。每年六月初六，地处东阳的岩背村和桂村还有以朱相公为名的庙会。虽然朱相公和胡则生活的年代不同，但因为他们都曾为当地人做过好事，所以在老街上朱相公又被尊称为胡则十兄弟之一，并曾与其他九兄弟一起被供奉在广慈寺前殿，胡则像的两侧。

解放后，新建的本保庙位于原址对面的蜈蚣山上，所以也被称作南山庙。这座建筑高高地坐落在蜈蚣山的第二个小山包上，与周围环境形成鲜明对比。站在门口，可以对下坑口这片建筑群一览无余。本保庙是一个一进的小院，院内有一座三开间的正殿。正殿中间供朱相公及夫人，左边供土地公和土地婆，右边供财神公和财神婆。每月初一和十五都会有人来祭拜，村里也有专门的人负责添加庙里的灯油。

万成庙：远在五峰鸡鸣峰后的山坳里，还有一个观音庙，又叫万成庙。老街上和邻村的年轻夫妇，不免都会来这里拜拜，得子后还会来还愿，所以这里的香火一直也还不错。万成庙的入口，在高大茂密林木的掩映下，十分隐蔽。进门之后还要走上十多分钟才可以看见正殿。正殿三开间，同样是利用岩体本身的洞穴，依山而建。在较高的一处洞穴前，还建有三间僧房，曾作为庙里尼姑的住所。殿内正中供送子观音，左供观音菩萨，右供千手千眼观音。三座观音均为立像，其中送子观音双臂前伸，手中抱一男童。当求子的年轻夫妇拜倒在观音像前的时候，他们刚好可以看到观音手中的男童，正在向他们张开双臂、面带甜甜的微笑。在大殿的角落里，还供有地藏菩萨。想来人们在求子的同时，也会希望这个孩子能够降生在一个国泰民安、风调雨顺的年景里。在浙江省国民政府临时迁驻方岩的那几年，万成庙还曾作为浙江省史料征集委员会的办公场所。民政厅厅长阮毅成的阮公馆也位于万成庙前这块幽静之地。

— —

Because Guangci Temple and the Youshun Marquis Shrine on Fangyan Mountain have always had thriving incense, villagers climb the mountain to seek bodhisattvas and Lord Hu for major matters. To many, Lord Hu is an omnipotent deity governing heaven and earth. Even villagers skeptical of Lord Hu typically climb the mountain in the first lunar month to burn three sticks of incense and draw a fortune, praying for joy and peace for themselves and their families in the new year. Consequently, religious architecture on Yanxia Old Street is relatively undeveloped, with only a few minor temples. The villagers' attitude toward worship reflects the widespread Han Chinese practice of 'burning incense at every temple, bowing to every bodhisattva'—a pan-religious faith. This is vividly embodied in the religious architecture here: behind Lord Hu on Fangyan Mountain sits Tathagata Buddha; beneath the Jade Emperor is enshrined Ksitigarbha; the front hall roof of Guangci Temple is painted with Journey to the West; and at the foot of the mountain, Guanyin is flanked by Guan Yu and the Earth God.

Jingtang (Scripture Hall): Originally located south of Xiahukeng Stream—south of the former Chengtou, west of the old street—manned year-round by Buddhist nuns. According to elders, it was an east-west oriented courtyard compound whose main hall originally enshrined Guanyin, flanked by Guan Yu and the Earth God. On the first and fifteenth of each lunar month, people came to burn incense, make vows, and draw lots. Tragically, Jingtang was destroyed by fire over twenty years ago.

Benbao Temple (Local Protection Temple): Commonly called the Earth God Temple, this is typically a small shrine for folk deities. Yanxia Village's original Benbao Temple stood under a large cypress tree on the front hilltop of Lizard Mountain, facing the village it protected. The 'temple' was merely a stone-enclosed, south-facing small shrine enshrining Lord Zhu. According to folk legend, Lord Zhu was based on Zhu Danxi. Records show Zhu Danxi (1281–1358), named Zhenheng, courtesy name Yanxiu, was a Yuan dynasty native of Jinhua, called Zhu Danxi because his family lived by Danxi Stream. As founder of the 'Nourishing Yin' school, he is ranked among the Four Great Physicians of the Jin-Yuan period and became a major local folk deity after death. Every sixth day of the sixth lunar month, Yanbei and Gui villages in Dongyang hold temple fairs named after Lord Zhu. Although Lord Zhu and Hu Ze lived in different eras, both did good deeds for locals, so on the old street Lord Zhu was honored as one of 'Hu Ze's ten brothers' and was once enshrined with the other nine brothers in the front hall of Guangci Temple, flanking Hu Ze's statue.

After 1949, a new Benbao Temple was built on Centipede Mountain opposite the original site, hence also called Nanshan Temple (South Mountain Temple). It sits high on the second hillock of Centipede Mountain, contrasting sharply with its surroundings. From its entrance, the entire building cluster at the Xiakeng ravine mouth is visible. It is a single-courtyard small compound with a three-bay main hall. The center enshrines Lord Zhu and his wife; the left, the Earth God and Earth Goddess; the right, the God of Wealth and Goddess of Wealth. People come to worship on the first and fifteenth of each lunar month, and a designated villager tends the temple's lamp oil.

Wancheng Temple (Ten-Thousand-Accomplished Temple): Deep in the ravine behind Jiming (Cock-Crow) Peak of the Five Peaks lies another Guanyin temple, also called Wancheng Temple. Young couples from the old street and neighboring villages come to worship, and return to fulfill vows after bearing children—so its incense has always been decent. Its entrance, hidden by tall dense forest, is very secluded. After entering, one walks for over ten minutes before seeing the main hall. The three-bay main hall, like the original Benbao Temple, uses a natural rock cave and is built against the mountain. In front of a higher cave stand three monk's quarters that once housed the temple's nuns. The hall's center enshrines the Child-Giving Guanyin; the left, Guanyin Bodhisattva; the right, Thousand-Armed Thousand-Eyed Guanyin. All three are standing figures; the Child-Giving Guanyin extends both arms forward, holding a male child. When young couples seeking children kneel before the statue, they see the child in Guanyin's arms opening its arms toward them with a sweet smile. In a corner of the hall, Ksitigarbha is also enshrined—presumably, while seeking a child, people also hope the child will be born in a year of national peace and timely rains. During the Nationalist Zhejiang Provincial Government's temporary residence at Fangyan, Wancheng Temple served as the office of the Zhejiang Provincial Historical Materials Collection Committee. The Ruan Residence of Civil Affairs Department Director Ruan Yicheng also stood in the quiet area before Wancheng Temple.

塘堰与井泉——岩下老街的水利系统
Ponds, Weirs, Wells, and Springs — Water Management System of Yanxia Old Street
五、塘堰与井泉 | Ponds, Weirs, Wells, and Springs

要点 Highlights

  • 方岩溪自南向北蜿蜒流过山谷，沿途几条小溪从东西山坑流出汇入
  • 干旱时溪水不够用，动用平时储存在水塘里的水
  • 老街两侧大大小小水塘二十几个，有名字的约十个
  • 澈月塘（程立重宅院内）、源流塘（世远公祠前）、八沢塘/八百把塘（城头外）
  • 火种塘：祠堂或大型宅院附近水塘，重要功能是救火
  • 屋后塘（新隆兴旅店后）、屋前塘（程正伟火腿店前）
  • 深塘：五峰固厚峰东麓，最大水塘，目前居民饮用水主要来源
  • 前塘：得耕居前半月形水塘，池底卵石镶嵌双龙戏珠，玉带环腰吉象
  • 两个屋里塘：澈月塘（程阮阮旅店旁）、后塘（作勳公祠东边院落内）
  • 澈月塘长方形，面积只比绍常祠堂小一点，原属程立重宅院，呼应屋里井
  • 后塘被L形房屋围合，西侧一层和水塘同高，东侧一层和老街同高
  • 1989年《永康县志》：老街两侧共有水井18眼，大部分村里共有，少数大型宅院内
  • 饮泉：狮子山脚下周氏宗祠以南老仁昌旅店前，程宣洁修，碎瓷器镶嵌饮泉二字，水可治病
  • 用水准则：井水饮用；蔬菜水果溪水洗；米家里洗（洗米水喂猪）；水塘浣洗外衣被子；马桶内衣裤褯子特定小水塘洗

塘堰之设，水利之所在也。塘以储水，堰以节出。遇旱则尤资其利。井与泉并列从其类也。山下出泉，蒙象也，易则之以。果行育德。木上有水，井象也，易则之以。劳民劝相。古之为民者，凿井而饮，耕田而食。古之立邑者，相其阴阳，观其流泉，是经制之大端也。

县志里对塘堰和井泉的概述，既明确地说明了它们的作用和重要性，又强调了它们是为民和立邑者首要考虑的问题。

这里主要的溪流是方岩溪，它自南向北，蜿蜒流过岩下老街所在的山谷。沿途又有几条小溪，从东西两侧的山坑中流出，最终汇入方岩溪。这几条溪水便是岩下老街灌溉的主要水源。但正如县志里所说的，每当干旱的时候，溪水就不够用，就要动用平时储存在水塘里的水。

在岩下老街两侧，有大大小小的水塘二十几个。有名字的就约有十个，例如位于程立重宅院内的澈月塘、世远公祠前的源流塘、城头外的八沢塘（又称八百把塘）等。由于位置和大小的不同，它们不光有灌溉作用，还兼有救火、浣洗等功能。在祠堂、或是大型宅院附近，大都有水塘。这些水塘又被称作火种塘，它们的另一个重要功能就是救火。例如，新隆兴旅店后面的屋后塘；还有程正伟火腿店前的屋前塘。这里最大的水塘就是位于五峰固厚峰东麓的深塘，它也是目前老街上居民饮用水的主要来源。最有讲头的水塘，要算是得耕居前的前塘。这个半月形水塘的池底不但有用卵石镶嵌成的双龙戏珠图案，它还给得耕居带来了玉带环腰的吉象。

老街上有两个别名为屋里塘的水塘。其中一个是位于程阮阮旅店旁边的澈月塘，另一个位于作勳公祠东边的院落内，又被称为后塘。澈月塘为长方形，面积只比绍常祠堂小一点儿，它原本属于十五世祖程立重宅院的一部分，与之呼应的还有位于水塘南边的屋里井。屋里塘和屋里井都被当地人视为吉象，不但由于它们的存在能方便日常生活，降低火灾的可能性，更因为能做到有屋里塘或屋里井的，都是极其富有的大户人家。后塘是一个名副其实的屋里塘，它被东西两组层高相差一层的L形房屋围在当中，西侧房屋的一层和水塘同高，东侧的一层和老街同高，约是西侧房屋二层的高度。两组建筑之间围合了一个小的天井，天井即是水塘，只有东北边的那排房有前廊可以到达。现在水塘里长满了绿绿的苔藓，衬着乱石垒砌的、高高的墙基，别有一番风味。

据1989年编写的《永康县志》所述，岩下老街两侧共有水井18眼。这其中大部分水井属于村里共有，也有少数几个位于大型宅院内。最有名的可能就是位于狮子山脚下，周氏宗祠以南，老仁昌旅店前的饮泉。饮泉紧邻老街东侧，井前有一小块开敞的空间，后面就是拔地而起的狮子山，在面朝老街的一面，还有用碎瓷器镶嵌成的饮泉二字。这口水井为老仁昌旅店的老板程宣洁所修，据说井里的水可以治病，所以水井前经常有络绎不绝的取水人。

由于水源紧张，老街上的程氏后人逐渐形成了一些相沿成习的用水准则。例如井水专门用来饮用；蔬菜、水果要拿到溪水里去洗；米则要在家里洗，因为洗米的水要用来喂猪；水塘除了灌溉、救火之外，在应用化学洗涤剂之前，还可以用来浣洗外衣、被子之类的东西；对于马桶、内衣裤和褯子这类的脏东西，只能拿到一些特定的小水塘里去洗。

— —

'The establishment of ponds and weirs is where water management lies. Ponds store water; weirs regulate its outflow. In drought, they are especially beneficial. Wells and springs are grouped with them. Springs emerging at the foot of mountains form the Meng hexagram; the I Ching interprets this as 'resolute action cultivates virtue.' Water above wood forms the Jing (Well) hexagram; the I Ching interprets this as 'toiling the people and exhorting mutual aid.' The ancients who served the people dug wells to drink and plowed fields to eat. The ancients who established settlements examined yin and yang and observed flowing springs—these were the essentials of governance.'

The county gazetteer's overview of ponds, weirs, wells, and springs clearly states their function and importance, emphasizing that they were the primary concern of those who served the people and established settlements.

The main stream here is Fangyan Stream, which winds from south to north through the valley where Yanxia Old Street lies. Along its course, several smaller streams flow out of the ravines on the east and west sides, eventually joining Fangyan Stream. These streams are the main irrigation source for Yanxia Old Street. But as the gazetteer notes, in drought the streams are insufficient, and water stored in ponds must be used.

On both sides of Yanxia Old Street are over twenty ponds of various sizes, about ten with names—such as Cheyue Pond in Cheng Lizhong's compound, Yuanliu Pond in front of Shiyuan Gong Hall, and Baze Pond (also called Bababai Pond) outside Chengtou. Depending on location and size, they serve not only irrigation but also fire-fighting and washing. Near ancestral halls or large compounds, ponds are common. These are called 'fire-seed ponds'—their other important function is fire fighting. Examples include the 'rear-house pond' behind Xinlongxing Inn and the 'front-house pond' in front of Cheng Zhengwei's ham shop. The largest pond is Shen Pond (Deep Pond) at the eastern foot of Guhou Peak of the Five Peaks, currently the main source of drinking water for street residents. The most storied pond is Qiantang (Front Pond) in front of Degeng Residence. This half-moon pond's bottom features a 'twin dragons playing with a pearl' pattern inlaid with cobblestones, and it brings the auspicious 'jade belt around the waist' configuration to Degeng Residence.

Two ponds on the street are called 'in-house ponds.' One is Cheyue Pond beside Chengruanruan Inn; the other, in the compound east of Zuoxun Gong Hall, is called Houtang (Rear Pond). Cheyue Pond is rectangular, only slightly smaller than Shaochang Hall; it originally belonged to the 15th-generation ancestor Cheng Lizhong's compound, paired with an 'in-house well' south of the pond. Both in-house ponds and wells are regarded as auspicious—not only because they facilitate daily life and reduce fire risk, but because only extremely wealthy families could afford them. Houtang is a true in-house pond, enclosed by two L-shaped building groups differing in height by one story: the west building's ground floor is level with the pond, while the east building's ground floor is level with the old street (about the height of the west building's second floor). Between the two groups is a small light well that is itself the pond, accessible only via the front porch of the northeast row of rooms. Today the pond is covered in green moss, contrasting with the high rubble-masonry wall foundations—creating a distinctive charm.

According to the 1989 Yongkang County Gazetteer, there are 18 wells on both sides of Yanxia Old Street. Most are village-owned; a few lie within large compounds. The most famous may be Yinquan (Drinking Spring) at the foot of Lion Mountain, south of the Zhou Clan Hall, in front of Laorenchang Inn. Yinquan hugs the east side of the old street, with a small open space in front and the precipitous Lion Mountain behind. On the side facing the street, the characters 'Yinquan' are inlaid with broken porcelain. This well was dug by Cheng Xuanjie, owner of Laorenchang Inn; its water was said to cure illness, so people came continuously to draw water.

Due to water scarcity, the Cheng descendants on the street developed customary water-use rules. Well water is exclusively for drinking; vegetables and fruits are washed in the stream; rice is washed at home because the rice water feeds pigs; ponds, besides irrigation and fire-fighting, were used (before chemical detergents) for washing outer clothing and quilts; chamber pots, underwear, and diaper cloths could only be washed in specific small ponds.

第五章 方岩山与广慈寺
Chapter 5: Fangyan Mountain and Guangci Temple
方岩山登山道与山顶空间
Fangyan Mountain Climbing Path and Summit Space
一、方岩山 | Fangyan Mountain

要点 Highlights

  • 方岩山海拔约350米，高二百丈周六里，平地拔起四面如削，惟南通一道
  • 下马泉长廊（花廊）：南通一道终点，曾挤满卖方岩纸花摊位
  • 罗汉洞：南岩腰，正德禅师栖息之所，十八罗汉塑像，应绍为尼姑
  • 蛟龙泉：水甘美如饴性沉厚，置铜钱数枚于杯水而水不外溢
  • 百步峻：叠石为磴阶级而升，条石侧面和石栏杆刻捐献者姓名籍贯
  • 步云亭：飞檐翘角横跨路中，憩步邀群仙随云来半山，前身飞虹亭
  • 飞桥：长一百多米沿岩架石栈道，永康十景之一飞桥锁雾，1962年钢筋混凝土重建宽2.5米
  • 透关/天门：两巨石对峙最窄仅一米多，重檐八角亭，民国37年1948年造
  • 天王殿：骑跨路中仿佛城门，殿内只有四大天王无弥勒韦驮，天台宗唐代寺院特征
  • 天门头空基：香汛小吃摊位，永康食果肉麦饼（肥多瘦少猪肉丁拌梅干菜）
  • 天街：全长约半里，前街路，两端石牌坊，1988年重建南侧店面
  • 长生塘：仙女眼泪变成，饮用可长生不老；砚井：寺里和尚饮用水源
  • 母子樟：和广慈寺同龄古樟，旁生许多小樟
  • 程方峰祠堂：明万历年间建，1990年清明重立墓碑，千古英灵安在万代浩气永存
  • 千人坑：方岩山劈成两段山谷深数百米宽不到一米，方腊起义陈十四传说
  • 听泉楼：元邑人胡济源筑，大夫士赋诗成集听泉楼集

方岩山坐落在永康县城东五十里，海拔高度约350米。它位于岩下村的西侧，和东面的狮子山、蜥蜴山和蜈蚣山等一起界定了岩下村的空间范围。清康熙《永康县志》里有一段对方岩山精辟而生动的描写：高二百丈，周六里，平地拔起，四面如削，惟南通一道，至山腰而绝，叠石为磴，阶级而升，曰百步峻。磴上沿岩架石为栈道，曰飞桥。将至顶，有两石对竖，屋之，曰透关，俗呼曰峰门。下有飞虹亭，寺僧慧韬新筑也。入关，地更平旷，约数百亩，有井，曰砚井，有池，可亩余。

对于上方岩的香客，当老街走到底之后，就会向西拐进方岩山脚下的一个山坳里，再顺着南通一道上山。所谓的南通一道其实就是夹在方岩和南岩之间的一条登山小路。进山后，沿着这条路走不远就是因下马泉而得名的下马泉长廊。这里因为以前曾经挤满卖方岩纸花的摊位，所以又被称为花廊。下马泉顾名思义标志着这南通一道的终点，从这里开始人们就可以体会到北宋永康进士应材所描写的那番景色：石磴巍峨促膝行，行时不觉看时惊。纵教良匠描难就，自是天工造化成。

过下马泉不远，就是坐落在南岩腰的罗汉洞。罗汉洞也是当年大悲寺的肇基祖师，正德禅师的栖息之所。相传正德禅师借化缘得来的资助，塑了十八尊罗汉置于洞中，并希望借助罗汉的法力在方岩山顶修建大悲寺，罗汉洞便由此得名。在大悲寺建好之后，罗汉洞便不再被寺院使用。不知从什么时候开始，便有尼姑在这里安家，并一直持续到文革。最后一位尼姑叫应绍为，她在这里不但给人看病，而且还收留流浪的乞丐，所以广受大家尊敬。她去世之后，就被埋葬在罗汉洞对面方岩山腰的山洞里。在罗汉洞的西南角，有一口名为蛟龙泉的古井。其水甘美如饴，性沉厚，尝置铜钱数枚于杯水中，而水不外溢。在《蛟龙泉记》里，还记载着关于这口井的一个美丽传说。据说曾经有一个姓应的尼姑居住在罗汉洞里，夏天，她每天都要翻山越岭到五峰下的龙湫泉取水，供香客饮用。她的举动感动了龙王，于是龙王就变成鲤鱼托梦于她，指出可以打井的地点。经过了数年的努力，她终于打井成功并找到了泉水，因此取名为蛟龙泉。

过了罗汉洞就是叠石为磴的百步峻。由于这段台阶坡度较大，人们在上行的时候，会不由自主地看到每一块条石的侧面和石栏杆上都刻着捐献者的姓名和籍贯，就好像在读一本用石头写的书。这个过程既让人忘却了攀登的辛劳与枯燥，又不禁油然升起一番敬意。百步峻的尽端就是横跨在路中，飞檐翘角的步云亭。步云亭的位置恰到好处，人们可以在这里稍事休息，凭栏远眺。正如挂在上面的对联所说，憩步邀群仙，随云来半山。依清康熙县志的描述，透关下有飞虹亭，寺僧慧韬新筑也，但是这段话在清光绪县志中却被删掉了；再加上朱谨的诗句飞虹亭畔息，贾勇入峰关，这里的峰关应该就是县志里所提的峰门。可以推断飞虹亭可能就是现在的步云亭的前身。这也符合广慈寺前住持允明禅师所说，即步云亭是寺里的和尚建造的。

步云亭再往上就是长一百多米，沿岩架石的栈道，又称飞桥。这段栈道架设在悬崖峭壁之间，曲折有致，在清晨和雨后，就会呈现永康十景之一的飞桥锁雾。现在的飞桥是1962年夏天用钢筋混凝土重建的，宽度近2.5米。已经比当年少了些险峻。据允明禅师讲，飞桥始建于开山祖师时期，它以前的状况可以从题刻在旁边的《重建飞桥记略》中略知一二，原砌以石，宽仅尺许，几经改造，增至五尺。当地人对飞桥的改造还可以从其他文献中找到一些印记，例如在《程公孟举墓表》中有记载程氏四世祖程鹏在明永乐乙酉年（1405年）曾造飞桥，设义浆。

走到飞桥头有两巨石对峙，最窄处仅相隔一米多。从现存最早的明正德《永康县志》，到清光绪《永康县志》里都有同样的记载，即两块巨石之上曾建有题额为透关的亭子。透关亭如今已不复存在，取而代之的是题额为天门，材料采用石材加钢筋混凝土的重檐八角亭。据允明禅师讲，天门下面的部分，是开山祖师最先建的，虽屡经重修，但是一直都有。目前上面的亭子是民国37年（1948年）造的。

正如县志所述，过了天门地势就变得较为平缓空旷，而首先看到的便是天王殿。天王殿的出现预示着佛家圣地近在咫尺，但是它的位置和格局却十分独特。天王殿通常是中国寺院建筑群中轴线上的第一进殿。殿内中间通常供奉弥勒菩萨，并在其后供奉面向北方的韦驮菩萨，东西两旁则供奉四大天王。在广慈寺，天王殿没有和其他的大殿一起位于中轴线上，而是位于寺院主体建筑之前一里多远的位置。这座天王殿骑跨于路中，中间开敞以便走人，仿佛一座城门。不仅如此，殿内只有四大天王在两侧，并没有依常规供奉弥勒和韦驮菩萨。有学者认为，在历史上天王殿最初多见于净土宗的寺院，在两宋以后才开始出现在大部分其他宗派的寺院里。广慈寺是天台宗下的寺院并始建于唐代，这可能就是为什么寺院主体建筑群内并没有天王殿的位置。

天王殿后面这块稍为平坦的范围以前称作天门头空基，每年香汛期间这里都会散落一些卖小吃的摊位，以方便香客歇歇脚，填饱肚子以继续赶路。目前，路旁这些小吃摊位除了经营诸如馄饨、面条、豆干之类的食品外，最有特色的要算是被外地人称为永康食果的肉麦饼了。肉麦饼是用肥多瘦少的猪肉丁拌梅干菜作馅，包法类似于北方的馅饼，只是厚一点儿，最后用又浅又平的大锅烤熟。制作过程中的诀窍是，在捏摺的时候要稍向上提，使空气进入后再封口，这样烤出来的饼里面充满空气，从外面看起来非常饱满，而且还能保证皮又焦又脆。最重要的是，肉麦饼可以事先做好，叠落在一起放在锅边又不致变凉。若有客人来了，可以随来随买，随走随吃，不妨碍香客们赶路。

从小吃街转过一个弯就到了天街，天街全长约半里，目前两端各矗立着一个石牌坊作为标志。天街以前称为前街路，路两边多是两层高的店面，大一点的还有三层高。这其中包括近十家小规模的旅店和以经营包括《方岩指南》等各类图书为主的日新书社。每年从阴历八月到三月之间还会有十余家临时小店开张，它们不但出售京广杂货、铁器、木器、儿童玩具、木梳等，还经营牛肉、油条、肉麦饼、馄饨、豆腐花、豆腐圆和茶水等当地小吃。天街现在只在南侧有一两层高的店面，这些建筑都是在1988年重建的。店面多经营祭祀用品，例如蜡烛、香纸和佛像等，并兼营方岩货。

天街的北侧是长生塘，传说是仙女的眼泪变成的，饮用塘中水后可以长生不老，并由此得名。塘边有井，叫砚井，是寺里和尚赖以生存的饮用水源。在天街中段南侧，有一棵据说是和广慈寺同龄的古樟树，因其旁边生长着许多小樟树，所以叫母子樟。母子樟旁边曾经有明万历年间（1573—1620年）建造的程方峰（1498—1585年）祠堂。现在取而代之的是方岩仙人洞，以借助现代化的手段来制造会说话活菩萨、土地公公破土而出、猪八戒大闹盘丝洞等视听效果来吸引香客。从仙人洞旁散落的巨大的柱基石和洞里依稀可见的方砖铺地，可以推测出程方峰祠堂当年的规模。据允明禅师讲，祠堂里面原有程方峰的塑像，祠堂大门从来都是关着的，只有在清明期间会开，那时候会有五六百人来这里祭祖。程方峰和其夫人孙氏的墓建在祠堂后面。现存的墓碑是1990年清明重立的，旁边刻有千古英灵安在，万代浩气永存的对联。

在天街尽端的东北角是千人坑。在那里，方岩山被劈成一南一北两段，中间的山谷深数百米，且宽不到一米。关于千人坑，县志里还记载着这样的传说：相传昔乡人避寇岩上，寇至援藤升，顿见有蛇，刃挥之，藤断寇坠，死者若干人，遂溃去，此坑所由名也。这里的寇指的是在北宋宣和二年（1120年），永康人陈十四和他率领的农民起义军。陈十四的起义军是永康方岩地区响应方腊起义的一支队伍。当年他们攻破县城，迫使知县赵琪退据方岩山，县志里描述的就是发生在这之后的故事。陈十四的起义虽然在次年就被镇压了，但是人们却借此来纪念当时牺牲的起义军战士。千人坑西边曾有听泉楼，如今楼去泉干，只能借县志中的记载来想象那清脆的泉水声：坑上西行百步许，下有石谷，泉出谷间，如环佩声。旧有楼，曰听泉，里人胡济源作。大夫士赋诗成集，曰听泉楼集。

— —

Fangyan Mountain lies 50 li east of Yongkang county seat, with an elevation of about 350 meters. It stands west of Yanxia Village and, together with Lion Mountain, Lizard Mountain, and Centipede Mountain to the east, defines the village spatial extent. The Kangxi-era Yongkang County Gazetteer offers a vivid description: Two hundred zhang high, six li in circumference, rising sheer from the plain, sheared on all four sides, with only a southern passage that ends at the mountainside; stone steps ascend in tiers, called Hundred-Step Steepness. Above them, a stone plank road clings to the cliff, called Flying Bridge. Near the summit, two giant stones face each other, roofed over, called Tou Pass, commonly known as Peak Gate. Below it is Feihong Pavilion, newly built by monk Huitao. Beyond the pass the land flattens to several hundred mu, with a well called Inkstone Well and a pond of over one mu.

For pilgrims climbing Fangyan, after the old street ends they turn west into a hollow at the mountain foot and ascend via the Southern Passage, a narrow path between Fangyan and South Rock. Not far in stands Xiamaquan Corridor, named after the spring. Once crowded with paper-flower stalls, it is also called Flower Corridor. It marks the end of the Southern Passage; from here, pilgrims experience the scenery described by Northern Song Yongkang jinshi Ying Cai: Stone steps loom steep, knees bent as one climbs; walking one hardly notices, looking one is startled. Even a master artisan could not capture it, truly nature divine creation.

Beyond Xiamaquan, on the waist of South Rock, lies Luohan Cave. It was the dwelling place of Zhengde Chan Master, founding patriarch of Dabei Temple. Legend says Zhengde, using alms donations, cast 18 arhat statues for the cave, hoping their power would help build Dabei Temple on the summit, hence the cave name. After the temple completion, the cave fell out of temple use. Nuns took up residence at some point, continuing until the Cultural Revolution. The last nun, Ying Shaowei, treated the sick and sheltered wandering beggars, earning widespread respect; after her death she was buried in a cave on Fangyan slope opposite Luohan Cave. At the cave southwest corner is Jiaolong Spring, whose water is sweet as malt syrup, dense in quality; placing several copper coins in a cup of it, the water does not overflow. A legend in the Record of Jiaolong Spring tells of a nun surnamed Ying who, in summer, climbed daily to Longqiu Spring below Five Peaks for pilgrim water. Moved by her devotion, the Dragon King turned into a carp and appeared in her dream, pointing to a well site. After years of effort, she struck water and named it Jiaolong Spring.

Beyond Luohan Cave is Hundred-Step Steepness, with stone steps rising in tiers. Because the slope is steep, climbers inevitably notice the donor names and native places carved on each stone side and on the railings, as if reading a book written in stone. The process distracts from the toil and inspires reverence. At its top stands Buyun Pavilion, with upturned eaves spanning the path, perfectly placed for rest and distant viewing. Its couplet reads: Resting steps invite the immortals; following clouds comes halfway up the mountain. The Kangxi gazetteer mentions Feihong Pavilion below Tou Pass, newly built by monk Huitao, but this passage was deleted in the Guangxu gazetteer; combined with Zhu Jin verse Resting by Feihong Pavilion, summoning courage to enter Peak Gate, Feihong Pavilion may be Buyun Pavilion predecessor, consistent with former abbot Yunming account that Buyun Pavilion was built by the temple monks.

Above Buyun Pavilion stretches the hundred-meter-long Flying Bridge, a stone plank road clinging to the cliff. Winding between precipices, it produces one of Yongkang Ten Scenes, Flying Bridge Locked in Mist, at dawn and after rain. The current bridge was rebuilt in reinforced concrete in summer 1962, nearly 2.5 meters wide, less perilous than before. According to Yunming, it dates to the founding patriarch; the Brief Record of Rebuilding Flying Bridge notes it was originally stone, barely a chi wide, widened to five chi through repeated renovations. The Cheng clan fourth-generation ancestor Cheng Peng is recorded in the Epitaph of Cheng Gong Mengju as having built the Flying Bridge and set up free tea in the Yongle Yiyou year (1405).

At the bridge head, two giant stones face each other, barely a meter apart at the narrowest. From the earliest Zhengde-era gazetteer to the Guangxu edition, records mention a pavilion called Tou Pass atop them. Tou Pass Pavilion is gone, replaced by Tianmen, a double-eaved octagonal pavilion of stone and reinforced concrete. According to Yunming, the lower part of Tianmen dates to the founding patriarch and has been repeatedly repaired; the upper pavilion was built in the 37th year of the Republic (1948).

Beyond Tianmen the terrain flattens, and the first building seen is the Hall of Heavenly Kings. Uniquely, it is not on the temple central axis but over a li before the main complex, straddling the path like a city gate with an open center for passage. It contains only the Four Heavenly Kings on the sides, no Maitreya or Skanda as customary. Scholars note that Heavenly Kings Halls originally appeared mostly in Pure Land temples, spreading to other sects only after the Northern and Southern Song. Guangci Temple, a Tang-founded Tiantai sect temple, may thus lack a Heavenly Kings Hall in its main complex.

The flat area behind the hall was formerly called Tianmentou Empty Foundation, where snack stalls gathered during pilgrimage season. Today stalls sell wontons, noodles, dried tofu, and most distinctively, roumaibing, called Yongkang edible fruit by outsiders. Made with fatty diced pork and preserved mustard greens, wrapped like a northern pie but thicker, baked in a shallow flat pan. The trick is to lift the crimping slightly to trap air before sealing, making the cake puff up with a crisp crust. Pre-made cakes stack by the pan without cooling, allowing pilgrims to buy and eat on the go.

Beyond the snack area lies Tianjie, about half a li long, marked by stone archways at both ends. Formerly called Front Street Road, it had two-story, some three-story shops, including nearly ten small inns and the Rixin Book Society selling guides like Fangyan Guide. From the eighth to third lunar months, a dozen temporary shops opened selling general goods, ironware, woodware, toys, combs, and local snacks. Today only the south side has one- to two-story shops, rebuilt in 1988, mostly selling ritual supplies, candles, incense paper, Buddha statues, plus Fangyan souvenirs.

North of Tianjie is Changsheng Pond, said to be formed from a fairy tears; drinking its water grants longevity. Beside it is Yanjing, the monks drinking water source. Midway on Tianjie south side stands an ancient camphor tree said to be as old as Guangci Temple, with many saplings around it, called Mother-and-Child Camphor. Beside it once stood the Cheng Fangfeng ancestral hall, built in the Wanli period (1573-1620). Today it is replaced by Fangyan Immortal Cave, using modern audiovisual effects, talking living bodhisattvas, Earth God emerging from the ground, Zhu Bajie in the Spider Cave, to attract pilgrims. Scattered giant column bases and square brick paving hint at the hall former scale. According to Yunming, the hall contained Cheng Fangfeng statue; its gate was always closed, opening only at Qingming when 500 to 600 people came to worship. The tombs of Cheng Fangfeng and his wife Lady Sun lie behind; the current stele was re-erected at Qingming 1990, inscribed Eternal heroic spirit abides; noble spirit endures through myriad generations.

At Tianjie northeast corner is Qianren Keng, where Fangyan Mountain splits into two segments with a valley hundreds of meters deep and less than a meter wide. The gazetteer records: Legend says villagers once fled bandits onto the rock; bandits climbed vines, suddenly saw a snake, struck it, the vine broke, bandits fell, many died, the rest fled, hence the name. The bandits were Chen Shisi peasant rebels of Xuanhe two (1120), a Yongkang contingent responding to Fang La uprising. They captured the county seat, forcing Magistrate Zhao Qi to retreat to Fangyan. Though suppressed the following year, the legend commemorates the fallen rebels. West of the pit once stood Tingquan Tower, now gone with its spring dry. The gazetteer preserves its description: A hundred paces west from the pit, a stone valley below; spring emerges between rocks, sounding like jade pendants. Once a tower called Tingquan, built by local Hu Jiyuan. Scholars composed poems collected as Tingquan Tower Anthology.

广慈寺千年历史——从大悲寺到胡公大帝
A Millennium of Guangci Temple — From Dabei Temple to Lord Hu the Great
二、广慈寺历史 | History of Guangci Temple

要点 Highlights

  • 广慈寺原名大悲寺，天台宗名刹，唐宣宗大中四年850年始建
  • 正德禅师：天台山国清寺火头和尚，会昌法难后离开国清寺，修头陀行居罗汉洞
  • 正德禅师见方岩山上中下三潭水清澈见底，建茅棚弘扬佛法，大中四年创建大悲寺
  • 胡则借读：北宋端拱元年988年春胡库人胡则来寺借住读书，教授小和尚读书写字
  • 胡则端拱二年989年进士及第，为官47年辗转睦州温州信州福州杭州池州
  • 胡则宝元二年1039年病死他乡葬杭州钱塘县南山履泰乡龙井源
  • 大悲寺治平二年1065年失火重建后易名广慈寺，胡则牌位和小塑像放如来佛前
  • 方腊起义宣和二年1120年：知县赵琪退据方岩，杜撰神人助王师灭寇神话
  • 宣和四年1122年胡则被封佑顺侯（第一次封赏，未言姓名止用方岩神奏）
  • 绍兴二十二年1152年宋高宗降旨敕赐赫灵庙（胡则重孙胡廷直详述始末力求正名）
  • 绍熙三年1192年佑顺嘉应侯；嘉泰三年1203年嘉定三年1210年佑顺嘉应福泽显灵侯
  • 淳祐六年1246年显应公，加正惠，宝祐初再加忠祐
  • 封号演变：佑顺（客观认可），嘉应（赞许肯定），福泽（人民立场褒扬），显灵（超客观神化）
  • 明万历年间聿孤禅师改革子孙派为八房（乾天承地人才宝智）轮流管理
  • 明隆庆五年1571年程方峰借地做坟，卖地十丈改卖地千丈官司，判千人坑长生塘为界
  • 清顺治十一年1654年赫灵庙修葺；康熙年间赫灵祠坍塌，胡则塑像搬入广慈寺前殿（寺庙合一）
  • 嘉庆二十四年1819年前厅重建扩建奠定中轴线最终格局
  • 道光二十九年1849年九月初四大火烧毁整个祠庙及僧房，一年多后重建（走向衰落第一步）
  • 宣统二年1910年人神共庙批判回应：玉皇大帝移地藏殿，胡则像单独供奉屏风阁（坐公吃素）
  • 1966年8月下旬文革拆除几乎所有建筑，仅屏风阁和一幢僧房幸存，字画书籍烧了一天
  • 1978年八月初一黄埔林母亲等四妇女抬新塑小胡公像上山；1979年十月初三允明禅师上山
  • 1980至1984年允明禅师买四座祠堂恢复建筑：古竹畈村厅（大雄宝殿）、汗下周村周氏祠堂、龙油县山賈村牛王殿（天王殿步云亭）、永康市徐氏祠堂（观音殿）

在方岩山顶上，目前并排坐落着一座不大为人知的千年名刹广慈寺和常年香火旺盛的胡公庙。虽然在历史上的很长一段时间里，广慈寺和胡公庙都同时矗立在这里，但是它们的关系却和现在大不相同。现在的胡公庙用地原属广慈寺，而胡公庙曾经位于广慈寺左面，临近长生塘的位置。这一点可以从历年的县志里找到证明，例如明嘉靖《永康县志》里讲，有祠，曰佑顺侯祠。右有佛庐，曰广慈寺；而清道光《永康县志》中却写道，临池有庙，曰赫灵。并庙有寺，曰广慈。这一寺一庙之间相互关系的变化，要从了解广慈寺的历史开始。

广慈寺原名大悲寺，是一座有上千年历史的天台宗名刹。它始建于唐宣宗大中四年（850年），也就是会昌法难结束后，恢复佛教的第四年。佛教自西汉传入中国以来，到隋唐时期已发展到鼎盛。在唐朝建立之初的二百年里，历任皇帝都大力提倡佛教并给予极高的礼遇。到唐朝后期，随着佛教影响力的不断扩大和经济势力的日益膨胀，作为一种宗教信仰，它便逐渐威胁到了唐朝的中央统治。不仅如此，唐武宗（840至846年）本人信仰道教，因此在他即位后，便推行了一系列的灭佛政策，包括下令拆毁寺宇，勒令僧尼还俗等。广慈寺的肇基祖师正德禅师在那个时候也被迫离开了天台山国清寺。正德禅师，原为寺里的一个火头和尚，即负责烧饭的和尚。离开国清寺之前，他曾在国清寺第十二世祖师物外禅师门下修习止观，即是禅定、智慧二学，是修学一切佛法的根本。

公元847年，崇信佛教的唐宣宗即位，改国号为大中元年，并立即下令恢复所有在会昌年间被废的佛寺。由于天台山国清寺没有能被马上恢复，正德禅师可能因此便来到不远的方岩山来修头陀行，即佛教苦行之一，并居住在现方岩山腰罗汉洞内。他见方岩山仍属混沌绝境，遂访山农指点，缘藤而上，匍匐跻攀，透洞始见山巅古树参天，槁木横卧，荆棘遍地，万般寂寥。探至凹处见上中下三潭水清澈见底，涧水淙淙不绝。于是，正德禅师在上、中潭畔先建茅棚以弘扬佛法，并于大中四年（850年）在原地创建了大悲寺。在这之后的一百多年间，方岩山清清静静，只听得到寺院里回荡的晨钟暮鼓和木鱼罄声。

在北宋端拱元年（988年）的春天，永康胡库人胡则来到寺院里借住，专心读书以准备赴考。作为回报，他在读书堂里教授寺院里的小和尚们读书写字，正因为如此，他广受寺院里和尚们的尊敬。据广慈寺前住持允明禅师讲，胡则走的时候，当时的住持资助了胡则上京赶考的路费，并叮嘱他，如果考不上，就一定要回来；如果考上了，也一定要回来报喜。胡则就这样带着老和尚的嘱托和小和尚们的希望走了，他在这里读书的故事也从此被老和尚传小和尚这样一代一代地流传了下来。胡则在离别之前的阴历八月初一，率为五言十二韵，书于屋壁下，题名为《别方岩》，以抒发他临行前的依依不舍之情：寓居峰顶寺，不觉度炎天。山叟频为约，林僧每出禅。虚怀思往事，宴坐息诸缘。照像龛镫暗，通宵磬韵传。宴心资寂寞，琢句极幽玄。拾菌寒云外，烹茶翠竹前。远阴临岳树，清响落岩泉。僻道无来客，深秋足乱蝉。松风生井浪，溪雨长苔钱。自省浮尘世，终难驻永年。遍游曾宛转，欲别重留连。明日东西路，依依独黯然。

胡则于北宋端拱二年（989年）进士及第，并随即开始了他的为官生涯。他辗转睦州、温州、信州、福州、杭州、池州等地，但是却再也没有回到方岩山大悲寺来看看。胡则于北宋宝元二年（1039年）病死他乡，葬于杭州钱塘县南山履泰乡龙井源。当消息传到大悲寺，当年受胡则教诲的小和尚都已经成了老和尚，出于对老师的怀念，他们便给胡则立了一个牌位，供奉在读书堂里。关于胡则读书堂的位置，在明嘉靖《永康县志》里，还有这样一段描写，在广慈寺东边的千人坑之侧有小径，缘崖而登，行二百余步，有石穴，曰读书堂。俗传胡侯弦诵之地。在清光绪《永康县志》里则把读书堂列入了地理志，古迹篇，读书堂：去听泉楼百许步，岩腰有小石洞即胡侍郎读书处。好事者架飞甍为游人临眺之所。但是广慈寺前住持允明禅师却说，读书堂位于长生塘的西侧，又由于宋高宗敕赐赫灵庙额诰中提到遂就旧读书处永康方岩寺池边立祠，可以认为在山腰上的小石穴可能只是胡则的弦诵之地，而在长生塘西侧的读书堂则是他读书及教书的地方，也是后来被改称为佑顺侯祠和赫灵庙的建筑。

在胡则去世后的八十多年里，他可能只有一个排位被寺里的和尚供奉在当年的读书堂，并享用一些来自里人的香火；读书堂也可能依然被用作小和尚的读书处。在这期间，大悲寺于北宋治平二年（1065年）失火，同年重建后易名为广慈寺。据广慈寺前住持允明禅师讲，从那时起胡则的牌位和一尊小的塑像就被放在了如来佛的前面。在近六十年后，方岩山广慈寺在北宋宣和二年（1120年）爆发的方腊起义中，曾作为永康知县的赵琪退守并最终击败起义军的地方。这件貌似偶然且毫无关系的事件却成为胡则从人变成神的关键第一步，其实这两件事之间暗含着深刻的历史原因。

自汉朝以来，中国历代的皇帝都独尊儒术，不但封其为官教，而且把它作为维护统治阶级的一种精神工具。但是儒家学说中教化的理念占到主要成分，它对生命的体会却十分肤浅，甚至不能回答一些有关生、死、祸、福等从皇帝到老百姓都关心的人生根本问题。所以历代皇帝在独尊儒术的同时，又利用佛教或道教来满足这些世俗的需求，同时进一步巩固其统治地位。换句话说，原始宗教的天命观、鬼神观、巫术等，在皇权的暗中保护下，大量地保存下来，并在人民中找到适宜的生存空间。封建皇帝对民间信仰中的神灵也从不一概排斥，甚至对许多地方涌现出的神予以册封。与胡则生活在同一时代，家喻户晓的妈祖（960至987年）就是一个有力的证明。妈祖真名为林默，由于她生前和死后的众多传说，宋、元、明、清的历代帝王都对她有多次的册封，封号也从夫人、天妃、天后到天上圣母，并列入国家祀典。历代帝王们不但通过对神祇的册封来确立神的地位，从而巩固自己的统治，更重要的是借此来证明一种远远比君权神授更高的权力地位，即神权君授。

宋代政权建立之后的前几代皇帝都推崇佛教，但是宋徽宗（在位1100至1125年），却尊信道教，他曾一度命令佛教和道教合流，改寺院为道观，并使佛号、僧尼的名称都道教化。在历史上，每当新任皇帝弃佛从道的时期，起源于异国的佛教为了生存，都不得不对土生土长在中国大地上的道教进行妥协，把一些属于道教的，或者是民间的神祇请入佛堂共享香火。在这一点上，广慈寺也不例外，这也是它能历经千年依然香火旺盛的原因之一。在宋徽宗当政时期发生的一件事，恰好让广慈寺顺理成章地把一位民间神祇纳入了自己的领地。

宣和二年（1120年）十月初九，歙州农民方腊由于不满北宋腐朽统治，借摩尼教的旗号在睦州青溪爆发起义。摩尼教又称明教，在唐会昌年间被禁，随后转入民间秘密流行，历经五代至宋朝仍在闽浙一带有广泛的信徒，因此这次起义迅速得到了广大农民的热烈拥护和响应。北宋王朝的统治者在镇压这次带有宗教色彩的农民起义的时候，也充分利用了地方神祇的力量，宣传神人相助的神话。由于这次起义在短短一年半的时间就被全部镇压，所以在这其中的所有战斗都能最终以正派神祇的胜利作为结束，从而一箭双雕地打击邪教和寇匪。在此期间，北宋王朝大力褒奖地方神祇，因此在方腊起义中显灵庇护官军而报至朝廷请求嘉奖的祠庙不在少数，如上虞遗德庙、嵊县显应庙等。有些即使是未受寇匪侵扰的地方，地方统治者也会宣称是因为有神人暗中相助的结果。

在方腊起义中，永康方岩陈十四也揭竿而起。他们攻破永康县城，迫使知县赵琪退据方岩山。虽然赵琪最终是仓皇逃离了方岩山，但是因为陈十四的起义军不久后就被镇压了，可以推测知县赵琪也顺势迎合皇帝的意愿，杜撰了在方岩山有神人助王师灭寇的神话。而曾经逮事三朝，十握州符，六持使节，并且已经被当地人所熟悉和爱戴的永康人胡则，也顺理成章地成了神人助王师神话的主角。况且当时胡则在广慈寺读书堂里已立有牌位，把他神化成方岩山神便再合适不过了。这些神话故事有许多版本，它们的真实性和逻辑性并不是本文所讨论的重点，所以不妨就引用宋高宗敕赐赫灵庙额诰中所记述的官方版本，民以重罹兵革为患，神独感梦于主将，显灵而阴助之。又贼魁梦一巨人，衣白衣跨白马饮池，黎明视水涸，其徒惊骇，遂至大败。在宋徽宗宣和四年（1122年），也就是方腊起义被全面镇压后的这年，胡则被封为佑顺侯，这也是他去世后得到的第一次封赏。或许因为在同时有众多的地方神祇都因为类似的原因而得到了封赏，以至于王廉访当时奏请失于勘斟，遂致所封敕中不言姓名，而止用方岩神奏。

或许是这次册封的确不重要，以至于没能引起胡氏后人的重视，因为这次册封的诰书并没有收录进《库川胡氏宗谱》。广慈寺在当时推崇道教的大背景下，倒是可能利用了这次册封，把胡则称为方岩神佑顺侯而纳入佛家圣地，并香火供养甚谨。而同时，方岩附近的一些地区也开始供奉胡则。作为一方神祇，胡则是若水若旱若疫若疠，有求无不应，有祷无不答。随着他在当地的名气越来越大，胡氏后人可能不满足于这次未言姓名的封赏，于是阖邑之士状于有司。在绍兴二十一年（1151年），作为建安县丞，也是胡则重孙的胡廷直向当时的宋高宗详述始末，力求正名。在核实了衢州司户参军赵善和绍兴府诸暨县主簿赵彦伦之后，礼部依建炎三年（1129年）颁布的挥神祠遇有灵应即先赐额的圣旨，向皇帝建议赐赫灵庙为胡则祠的匾额。绍兴二十二年（1152年）二月，宋高宗降旨敕赐赫灵庙。

胡则的这次被册封，只是他日后一系列册封的开始。在宋代，册封制度已经逐步完善，规定对于有灵应的神祠要无爵号者赐以额，已赐额者加封爵。初封侯，再封公，次封王。而对于同一级别内封号的字数也有限制，开始为两个字，可逐步增加最多到八个字。关于册封制度的这些具体要求，从胡则的历次册封就可以看出来。在宋绍熙三年（1192年），胡则被宋光宗赐为佑顺嘉应侯；随后在嘉泰三年（1203年）和嘉定三年（1210年），被宋宁宗分两次册封至佑顺嘉应福泽显灵侯。从这些封号用词的选择中，可以看出在表彰胡则时间中态度的变化。佑顺是从皇帝角度上一种客观的认可；嘉应代表了一种赞许和肯定；福泽则是站在人民的立场上一种不遗余力的褒扬；最终到显灵，即一种超客观的神化。与此同时，胡则被庙祀于衢、婺之间，无虑数十。到淳祐六年（1246年），宋理宗封胡则显应公，随后加正惠，宝祐初再加忠祐。

在胡则被一次次加封的同时，方岩山上供奉胡则的读书堂却岁久事殊，址存堂废。宋景定三年（1262年），里人王氏不忍其坏，举而新之夫。在这篇吴邃写的《方岩读书堂记》中还提到了作者本人在读书堂重修之后，谨即斯堂拜公遗像。由此可见，位于读书堂内的胡则塑像，最迟在重修后就已经存在了。据广慈寺前住持允明禅师讲，胡公庙以前就位于读书堂的位置。如此看来，有了胡则塑像的读书堂，日后就逐渐演化成了明嘉靖《永康县志》中提到的佑顺侯祠和清道光《永康县志》里所指的赫灵祠。近一百年后，写于元至正九年（1349年）的《胡侍郎庙碑阴记》中再次提到地方的胡则庙布满于郡境，不啻数十百区，胡则更是被演化成了一位能出云为风雨的万能神。

在经历了崇奉喇嘛教的元代，明代是佛教各个宗派逐步恢复和发展的时期。在这个时期里，广慈寺的香火也逐步趋于鼎盛，值得一提的是在明万历年间（1589至1619年）任住持的聿孤禅师，以及在他任职期间发生的两件事。据说聿孤禅师买了八个小孩当徒弟，改革创寺之始就实行的子孙派，将其分为乾、天、承、地、人、才、宝、智八房，轮流管理寺院香火和常务工作。子孙派规定只接收不超过五岁的幼儿皈依，小沙弥自幼与师父同起居、同饮食，形同父子，并死后合葬。聿孤禅师的改革既保持了子孙派极具亲和力的特点，又使寺院管理可以互相监督，并在此基础上发展了众多下属寺院。这也是广慈寺能历经多种磨难而成为千年古刹矗立在此的主要原因。

另一件事对广慈寺则是一个重创。据允明禅师讲，在明隆庆五年（1571年），附近的文楼村出了一个榜眼叫程正谊，后任四川左布政。程正谊的父亲叫程方峰（1498至1585年），他看上了方岩山这块宝地，便让儿子向当时的住持聿孤禅师借地做坟。聿孤禅师开始并不愿意，但念在自己是程方峰的孙子辈，便写了卖地十丈的地契。若干年后，聿孤禅师圆寂了，程方峰也过世了，程氏子孙把卖地十丈改成了卖地千丈，并在前街路边上的母子樟旁开始建造程方峰祠堂，一场官司便由此而生。寺里的和尚以整个方岩山也没有千丈为据来证明卖地千丈不实。审判结果是卖地是真，千丈是假，并判以千人坑和长生塘为界，南边归程氏，北侧归广慈寺。而这个审判结果在清光绪《永康县志》中也有明确记载，程方峰先生梓墓：方岩上自千人坑流水为界，至峰门悬崖峭壁俱属墓地。万历（1573至1620年）年间奉勘合给。这件事不知过了多久以后，文楼村的程氏看出了方岩山上的商机，开始在天街上做起了香烛、供品等生意。

一半的方岩山归了别人，广慈寺的香火和收入多少都会受到影响。清顺治十一年（1654年），赫灵庙就在胡氏后人的帮助下历经了一次修葺。可是几十年后，清康熙《永康县志》中就已经记载赫灵祠因年久失修，几近坍塌，寺里的僧人便把胡则的塑像搬到了广慈寺内。同时搬过去的应该还有胡则的牌位和那块敕赐的赫灵庙匾，它们一起被供奉在当时广慈寺的前殿内，从而真正意义上做到了寺庙合一。在这以后，从雍正七年（1729年）到道光二十七年（1847年）的一百多年里，奉胡则为伯祖的库川胡氏后人，先后多次出资帮助广慈寺修理或重建前厅和大殿，仅有记载的就有六次。其中嘉庆二十四年（1819年）对前厅的重建和扩建，基本上奠定了广慈寺中轴线最终的平面格局。

据允明禅师回忆，广慈寺中轴线上有四进殿堂，各殿堂之间有廊相连。第一进为广慈寺前厅，挂广慈寺匾；第二进为前殿，挂赫灵庙匾，中间供吃荤的行公胡则，背面为韦驮，两旁供胡则十弟兄；第三进为大雄宝殿，中间供如来佛，前面是乾隆三年（1738年）重塑的胡则像，旁边是文殊和普贤菩萨，殿堂两侧为十八罗汉；第四进是屏风阁，主供玉皇大帝；在大雄宝殿的东侧还建有观音阁。八房僧人的僧房则位于这些殿堂的外围，在平面上形成一个八卦图案。在宣统二年（1910年），出于对人神共庙批判的回应，寺里的僧人把玉皇大帝请到了位于西侧的地藏殿，把如来佛前的胡则像单独供奉于屏风阁，为吃素的坐公胡则。胡公虽然从大雄宝殿里被请了出来，但是据允明禅师说，即使现在胡公像后面依旧有如来、文殊和普贤菩萨的小塑像。

不幸的是，道光二十九年（1849年）九月初四的一场大火烧毁了整个祠庙及僧房。虽然在一年多以后，广慈寺得以重建，但是这已经成为它走向衰落的第一步。据允明禅师讲，清朝末年，当地老百姓的生活十分穷苦。再加上鸦片的泛滥，所以时常有人上山拿走庙里的东西，甚至是拆掉殿宇靠卖木头的钱为生。僧人也因此越来越少，八房僧人也只剩下了四房。《方岩指南》中也提到清光绪年间（1875至1908年），寺僧相率而去。在民国20和21两年，广慈寺还被后钱村来的一个道士霸占，寺内的僧人还被迫流落到附近的下属寺院。

在接下来几十年里一系列的人为灾害中，文化大革命无疑带给了广慈寺前所未有的重创。广慈寺几乎所有的建筑均在1966年8月下旬被拆除，仅有屏风阁和一幢僧房幸免于难。屏风阁原归方岩公社拆除，但是出于近千年对胡则的尊重，老街上的人拒绝这样做。于是，屏风阁被留了下来，并先后被用作大队开会的地方，林场仓库、邮电学校和602工厂。不仅如此，广慈寺内的字画和书籍从山上往下挑了十担，整整烧了一天。虽然《广慈寺寺志》在允明禅师的保护下逃过了这场浩劫，但却不明不白地遗失在永康市宗教科的各种卷宗之间。

文化大革命结束两年后，在1978年八月初一的凌晨，由岩下村村民黄埔林的母亲发起，她和另外三个妇女把一尊新塑的小胡公像抬上了山。文革后的第一任住持允明禅师于1979年十月初三上山，并凭记忆重塑了胡则像和屏风阁里其他的塑像，包括文曲星、财神、龙王和关公等。由于屏风阁还在，所以就有香客慕名来拜胡则。允明禅师用募捐来的香火钱，在1980年到1984年的五年间，买了四座祠堂，用以恢复广慈寺的建筑。第一个是在1980年用3000多元买的古竹畈村的一个厅，用来重建屏风阁前的大雄宝殿。紧接着用比较便宜的价格买下了因建杨汗水库而被迫拆除的汗下周村的周氏祠堂。随后用4000多元买了龙油县山賈村的牛王殿，用来恢复天王殿、步云亭、后山的亭子和做一些修修补补的工作。最后买了永康市里的徐氏祠堂，在观音阁原址上重建了现在的广慈寺观音殿。

— —

On Fangyan summit today stand side by side the little-known millennium-old Guangci Temple and the perpetually incense-thick Hu Gong Temple. For much of history both stood here, but their relationship differed greatly from today. The current Hu Gong Temple site originally belonged to Guangci Temple, while Hu Gong Temple once stood to Guangci left, near Changsheng Pond. This is confirmed in successive gazetteers: the Jiajing-era Yongkang Gazetteer states there is a shrine, called the Marquis of Youshun Shrine. To its right is a Buddhist dwelling, called Guangci Temple; the Daoguang-era gazetteer reads by the pond stands a temple, called Heling. Beside the temple is a monastery, called Guangci. The evolution of their relationship begins with Guangci Temple history.

Guangci Temple, originally named Dabei Temple, is a millennium-old Tiantai sect monastery founded in the fourth year of Dazhong (850) of Emperor Xuanzong of Tang, the fourth year of Buddhist restoration after the Huichang Persecution. Buddhism, introduced from the Western Han, reached its apex in Sui-Tang. For two centuries after the Tang founding, emperors vigorously promoted Buddhism with high honors. By the late Tang, Buddhism growing influence and economic wealth threatened central rule. Moreover, Emperor Wuzong (840 to 846) believed in Daoism and launched persecution, destroying temples, forcing monks and nuns to return to lay life. Guangci founding patriarch, Zhengde Chan Master, was then forced to leave Guoqing Temple on Tiantai Mountain. Zhengde had been a fire-head monk, or cook, at Guoqing, where he studied zhiguan, cessation and insight, under the 12th-generation patriarch Wuwai Chan Master.

In 847, the Buddhist-devout Emperor Xuanzong ascended, reigning as Dazhong, and immediately ordered restoration of all temples destroyed in Huichang. Since Guoqing Temple could not be restored immediately, Zhengde came to nearby Fangyan Mountain to practice dhutanga, ascetic practices, dwelling in what is now Luohan Cave on the mountainside. Finding Fangyan still a primeval wilderness, he sought guidance from mountain farmers, climbed by vines, crawled and scrambled, and through a cave saw the summit with ancient trees towering, withered trees prostrate, thorns everywhere, utterly desolate. Exploring the hollow, he found upper, middle, and lower pools with crystal-clear water and a gurgling stream. He built thatched huts by the upper and middle pools to propagate the Dharma, and in Dazhong four (850) founded Dabei Temple on the site. For over a century thereafter, Fangyan was quiet, echoing only with morning bells, evening drums, wooden fish, and chimes.

In the spring of the first year of Duangong (988), Hu Ze of Huku, Yongkang, came to stay and study for the imperial examination. In return, he taught the young monks to read and write in the study hall, earning their respect. According to former abbot Yunming, when Hu Ze left, the abbot funded his journey to the capital, urging him to return if he failed, or to bring good news if he succeeded. Hu Ze left with the old monk嘱托 and the young monks hopes; his study story was passed down from old monk to young for generations. On the first day of the eighth lunar month before departure, he composed a twelve-rhyme five-character poem on the wall, titled Farewell to Fangyan: Dwelling in the peak-top temple, unknowingly passing hot summer. Mountain elders frequent invitations; forest monks often emerge from meditation. Empty mind ponders past events; seated stillness calms all conditions. Lamp in niche dims image; all night the chime resounds. Quiet mind nourishes solitude; crafting verses reaches the profound. Gathering mushrooms beyond cold clouds; brewing tea before green bamboos. Distant shade reaches mountain trees; clear sound falls on rocky springs. Remote path has no visitors; late autumn abounds with restless cicadas. Pine wind makes ripples in the well; stream rain grows moss coins. Self-examining in the floating world; ultimately hard to halt eternal years. Wandering everywhere once; wanting to leave, lingering again. Tomorrow east and west roads; sadly parting alone.

Hu Ze passed the jinshi examination in Duangong two (989) and began his official career, serving in Muzhou, Wenzhou, Xinzhou, Fuzhou, Hangzhou, Chizhou, and elsewhere, but never returned to Dabei Temple. He died away from home in the second year of Baoyuan (1039), buried at Longjing Source, Lütai Township, South Mountain, Qiantang County, Hangzhou. When news reached Dabei Temple, the young monks he had taught were now old; out of affection for their teacher, they placed a memorial tablet for Hu Ze in the study hall. The study hall location is described in the Jiajing gazetteer: East of Guangci Temple, beside Qianren Pit, a small path climbs the cliff for over two hundred steps to a stone cave called Study Hall, traditionally where Lord Hu recited texts. The Guangxu gazetteer lists it under antiquities: Study Hall: about a hundred paces from Tingquan Tower, a small cave on the rock waist where Vice Minister Hu studied. Enthusiasts built flying eaves for visitors to view. But Yunming states the study hall was west of Changsheng Pond; and since the Song Gaozong edict granting the Heling Temple name mentions establishing a shrine by the pond at Yongkang Fangyan Temple, at the former study place, the mountainside cave may have been Hu Ze recitation place, while the pond-side study hall was where he studied and taught, and later became the Youshun Marquis Shrine and Heling Temple.

For over eighty years after Hu Ze death, only a memorial tablet in the old study hall received local incense; the hall may still have served young monks. During this period, Dabei Temple burned in the second year of Zhiping (1065), was rebuilt the same year, and renamed Guangci Temple. According to Yunming, from then on Hu Ze tablet and a small statue were placed before the Buddha. Nearly sixty years later, during the Fang La uprising of Xuanhe two (1120), Guangci Temple served as the refuge where Yongkang Magistrate Zhao Qi ultimately defeated the rebels, an apparently unrelated event that became the key first step in Hu Ze transformation from man to god, with deep historical causes.

Since the Han, Chinese emperors exalted Confucianism as state orthodoxy and a tool of rule. But Confucianism, focused on moral instruction, offered shallow understanding of life and could not answer fundamental questions of life, death, fortune, and misfortune that concerned emperor and commoner alike. Thus emperors, while exalting Confucianism, used Buddhism or Daoism to satisfy worldly needs and consolidate rule. In other words, primitive religion concepts of fate, ghosts, and sorcery survived under imperial patronage, finding space among the people. Feudal emperors never uniformly rejected folk deities, even enfeoffing many local gods. The famous Mazu (960 to 987), Hu Ze contemporary, is proof: born Lin Mo, with legends in life and death, she received repeated enfeoffments from Song, Yuan, Ming, and Qing emperors, titles from Lady to Heavenly Consort, Heavenly Empress, to Heavenly Holy Mother, entering state sacrifices. Emperors used divine enfeoffment to establish divine status and consolidate rule, more importantly, to assert a power higher than sovereign authority granted by god, divine authority granted by sovereign.

The early Song emperors promoted Buddhism, but Emperor Huizong (r. 1100 to 1125) believed in Daoism, once ordering Buddhism and Daoism to merge, converting temples to Daoist abbeys and giving Buddhist names Daoist forms. Historically, when a new emperor abandoned Buddhism for Daoism, foreign-origin Buddhism, to survive, compromised with native Daoism, inviting Daoist or folk deities into Buddhist halls to share incense. Guangci Temple was no exception, one reason it survived a millennium with thriving incense. An event during Huizong reign allowed Guangci to incorporate a folk deity naturally.

On the ninth day of the tenth month, Xuanhe two (1120), Fang La, a peasant of Shezhou, rebelled in Qingxi, Muzhou, under the Manichaean, or Mingjiao, banner against corrupt Northern Song rule. Manichaeism, banned in Tang Huichang, had spread secretly among the people, persisting in Fujian-Zhejiang through Five Dynasties and Song, so the uprising quickly won peasant support. The Song court, suppressing this religiously colored rebellion, exploited local deities, promoting myths of divine-human assistance. Because the rebellion was crushed within a year and a half, all battles could end in orthodox deities victory, striking both heretical religion and bandits. The court vigorously rewarded local deities; many shrines reported divine protection of government forces and requested rewards, such as Shangyu Yide Temple and Shengxian Xianying Temple. Even places untouched by bandits claimed secret divine aid.

In the Fang La uprising, Chen Shisi of Yongkang Fangyan also rose. They captured Yongkang city, forcing Magistrate Zhao Qi to retreat to Fangyan Mountain. Although Zhao Qi ultimately fled in panic, because Chen Shisi rebels were soon suppressed, it is推测 that Zhao Qi迎合 the emperor will, fabricating a myth of divine assistance to the royal army at Fangyan. Hu Ze, who had served three reigns, held ten prefectural seals, six diplomatic missions, and was familiar and beloved by locals, naturally became the myth protagonist. Moreover, Hu Ze already had a tablet in Guangci study hall, making deification as Fangyan mountain god apt. Many versions exist; their authenticity and logic are not our focus. The official version in Song Gaozong edict granting the Heling Temple name reads: The people suffered repeated warfare; the god appeared in dreams to the commander, manifesting power and secretly aiding. The rebel leader dreamed of a giant in white robes on a white horse drinking at the pond; at dawn the pond was dry, his followers terrified, leading to total defeat. In Xuanhe four (1122), the year after suppression, Hu Ze was enfeoffed as Marquis of Youshun, his first posthumous reward. Perhaps because many local deities received similar rewards simultaneously, the investigating official memorial was negligent, so the edict did not state the name, only using Fangyan God.

Perhaps this enfeoffment was too minor to attract Hu descendants attention, the edict was not included in the Kuchuan Hu Genealogy. Against the Daoism-promoting backdrop, Guangci Temple likely used the enfeoffment to incorporate Hu Ze as Fangyan God, Marquis of Youshun, into the Buddhist sanctuary, with incense and offerings diligently maintained. Meanwhile, areas around Fangyan began worshipping Hu Ze. As a local deity, he was for flood, drought, plague, and illness, every prayer answered, every supplication fulfilled. As his fame grew, Hu descendants, unsatisfied with the nameless enfeoffment, the whole district scholars petitioned the authorities. In the 21st year of Shaoxing (1151), Hu Tingzhi, county magistrate of Jian and Hu Ze great-grandson, detailed the full story to Emperor Gaozong, striving to correct the name. After verifying with Zhao Shan, Quzhou registrar, and Zhao Yanlun, Zhuji county registrar, the Ministry of Rites, following the Jianyan three (1129) decree that shrines with miraculous responses first receive a name plaque, recommended Heling Temple as Hu Ze shrine name. In the second month of Shaoxing 22 (1152), Emperor Gaozong issued the edict Imperially Bestowed Heling Temple.

This was only the beginning of Hu Ze enfeoffments. By the Song, the system was refined: shrines with miraculous responses without a title receive a name; with a name, receive rank. First marquis, then duke, then king. Title characters were limited, starting at two and increasing to a maximum of eight. Hu Ze successive enfeoffments illustrate this: in the third year of Shaoxi (1192), Emperor Guangzong named him Marquis of Youshun Jiaying; in the third year of Jiatai (1203) and third year of Jiading (1210), Emperor Ningzong added characters to Marquis of Youshun Jiaying Fuze Xianling. The word choices reveal evolving attitudes: Youshun, protective obedience, is objective imperial recognition; Jiaying, auspicious response, is approval; Fuze, blessing grace, is praise from the people standpoint; Xianling, manifesting the numinous, is transcendental deification. Meanwhile, Hu Ze was worshipped in temples between Quzhou and Wuzhou, no fewer than several dozen. In the sixth year of Chunyou (1246), Emperor Lizong enfeoffed him as Duke of Xianying, later adding Zhenghui, and in the early Baoyou period Zhongyou.

While Hu Ze was repeatedly enfeoffed, the study hall enshrining him aged with changing times, site remaining but hall ruined. In the third year of Jingding (1262), a local Wang could not bear its ruin and renovated it. Wu Sui Record of Fangyan Study Hall mentions the author reverently bowing to Lord portrait in the renovated hall, indicating Hu Ze statue existed by then at the latest. According to Yunming, Hu Gong Temple formerly occupied the study hall site. Thus the study hall with Hu Ze statue gradually evolved into the Marquis of Youshun Shrine in the Jiajing gazetteer and Heling Shrine in the Daoguang gazetteer. Nearly a century later, the Record of Vice Minister Hu Temple Stele (1349) notes Hu Ze temples spread throughout the prefecture, no fewer than several hundred, with Hu Ze transformed into an omnipotent god able to summon clouds and bring rain.

After the Yuan dynasty Lamaist patronage, the Ming saw Buddhist sects gradually recover and develop. Guangci incense thrived; notable was abbot Yugu Chan Master in the Wanli period (1589 to 1619) and two events during his tenure. Yugu reportedly bought eight boys as disciples, reforming the temple founding descendant system, or zisun pai, into eight branches, Qian, Tian, Cheng, Di, Ren, Cai, Bao, Zhi, taking turns managing incense and administration. The descendant system required accepting only children under five; young novices lived and ate with their masters from infancy, like father and son, and were buried together. Yugu reform preserved the system intimacy while enabling mutual supervision and developing many branch temples, a key reason Guangci survived multiple disasters as a millennium-old temple.

The other event was a severe blow to Guangci. According to Yunming, in the fifth year of Longqing (1571), Wenlou Village produced a bangyan, or second-place jinshi, named Cheng Zhengyi, later Sichuan left administrative commissioner. His father Cheng Fangfeng (1498 to 1585) coveted Fangyan Mountain and had his son borrow land for a tomb from abbot Yugu. Yugu initially refused but, considering himself Cheng Fangfeng grandson-generation, wrote a deed selling ten zhang of land. Years later, after Yugu death and Cheng Fangfeng passing, Cheng descendants altered ten zhang to thousand zhang and began building Cheng Fangfeng Hall by the Mother-and-Child Camphor on Front Street, sparking a lawsuit. The monks argued the entire Fangyan Mountain lacked even a thousand zhang, proving the forgery. The verdict: the sale is real, thousand zhang is false, with the boundary set at Qianren Pit and Changsheng Pond, south to the Cheng family, north to Guangci Temple. The Guangxu gazetteer records: Cheng Fangfeng tomb: from Qianren Pit flowing water as boundary, to Peak Gate cliffs all belong to the tomb. Granted during Wanli (1573 to 1620). Sometime later, Wenlou Cheng family saw commercial opportunity and began selling incense and offerings on Tianjie.

With half of Fangyan Mountain lost, Guangci incense and income suffered. In the 11th year of Shunzhi (1654), Heling Temple was renovated with Hu descendants help. But decades later, the Kangxi gazetteer records Heling Shrine dilapidated from age, near collapse, so monks moved Hu Ze statue into Guangci Temple, along with his tablet and the imperially-bestowed Heling Temple plaque, enshrined in Guangci front hall, truly achieving temple-shrine unity.
