Thereafter, from Yongzheng seven (1729) to Daoguang 27 (1847), the Kuchuan Hu descendants, who revered Hu Ze as their great ancestor, repeatedly funded Guangci front hall and main hall repairs, six recorded times. The 24th year of Jiaqing (1819) reconstruction and expansion of the front hall essentially established the central axis final layout.

According to Yunming, Guangci central axis had four halls connected by corridors. First: front hall with Guangci Temple plaque. Second: front shrine with Heling Temple plaque, center enshrining the meat-eating Traveling Lord Hu Ze, rear with Skanda, sides with Hu Ze ten brothers. Third: Mahavira Hall, center with Tathagata Buddha, in front the Qianlong three (1738) recast Hu Ze statue, beside Manjusri and Samantabhadra, sides with Eighteen Arhats. Fourth: Pingfeng Pavilion, or Screen Pavilion, main deity the Jade Emperor; east of Mahavira Hall was Guanyin Pavilion. The eight branches monk quarters surrounded the halls, forming a bagua, or eight trigrams, pattern in plan. In the second year of Xuantong (1910), responding to criticism of humans and gods sharing a temple, monks moved the Jade Emperor to the western Ksitigarbha Hall and enshrined Hu Ze statue alone in Pingfeng Pavilion, as the vegetarian Seated Lord Hu Ze. Though Hu Gong was moved out of Mahavira Hall, Yunming notes that even today small statues of Tathagata, Manjusri, and Samantabhadra remain behind Hu Gong image.

Tragically, on the fourth day of the ninth month, Daoguang 29 (1849), a fire destroyed the entire shrine-temple and monk quarters. Though rebuilt over a year later, this marked the first step toward decline. According to Yunming, in the late Qing local people lived in extreme poverty; opium addiction led some to steal temple objects or dismantle halls for timber. Monks dwindled from eight branches to four. The Fangyan Guide notes during Guangxu (1875 to 1908), monks left one after another. In Republic years 20 and 21, a Daoist from Houqian Village seized Guangci, forcing monks to flee to branch temples.

Among subsequent decades of man-made disasters, the Cultural Revolution dealt the heaviest blow. Nearly all Guangci buildings were demolished in late August 1966; only Pingfeng Pavilion and one monk quarter survived. Pingfeng Pavilion was slated for demolition by Fangyan Commune, but out of nearly a millennium of respect for Hu Ze, old street residents refused. It was retained and used successively as brigade meeting hall, forestry warehouse, post-and-telecom school, and Factory 602. Moreover, ten shoulder-poles of calligraphy, paintings, and books were carried down the mountain and burned for a full day. Although the Guangci Temple Chronicle survived under Yunming protection, it was inexplicably lost among Yongkang Religious Affairs Bureau files.

Two years after the Cultural Revolution, in the early morning of the first day of the eighth lunar month, 1978, initiated by the mother of villager Huang Pulin, she and three other women carried a newly cast small Hu Gong statue up the mountain. The first post-Cultural Revolution abbot, Yunming Chan Master, ascended on the third day of the tenth lunar month, 1979, and from memory recast Hu Ze statue and other Pingfeng Pavilion deities, Wenqu Star, God of Wealth, Dragon King, Guan Yu. Because Pingfeng Pavilion survived, pilgrims came to worship Hu Ze. Using donated incense money, from 1980 to 1984 Yunming purchased four ancestral halls to restore Guangci buildings: first, in 1980, a hall from Guzhufan Village for over 3,000 yuan, to rebuild Mahavira Hall before Pingfeng Pavilion; then, cheaply, the Zhou Clan Hall of Hanxia Zhou Village, demolished for Yanghan Reservoir; then, for over 4,000 yuan, the Ox King Hall of Shanjia Village, Longyou County, to restore the Heavenly Kings Hall, Buyun Pavilion, rear mountain pavilions, and repairs; finally, the Xu Clan Hall in Yongkang city, rebuilt as Guangci Guanyin Hall on the former Guanyin Pavilion site.

广慈寺与胡公庙现状
Current State of Guangci Temple and Hu Gong Temple
三、广慈寺和胡公庙现状 | Current State of Guangci Temple and Hu Gong Temple

要点 Highlights

  • 1980年代中叶胡公庙从广慈寺分离独立，占据广慈寺原有中轴线（西侧）
  • 广慈寺被迫向东平移，以重建观音殿为中轴线，后补建大雄宝殿
  • 广慈寺三进殿堂：前厅（1984年复建）、观音殿（1984年复建）、大雄宝殿（寺庙分离后补建）
  • 前厅神龛供弥勒佛，神龛正上方祠堂戏台顶精致藻井（螺旋形斗拱层叠上升）
  • 前厅东西两间九宫天花绘西游记经典场面，黄绿色底线条精美退晕渲染讲究
  • 观音殿和两侧围廊大木构件用神牛寿桃喜鹊雕饰主题，月梁蟮梁狮子舞球
  • 中国建筑构造特点：一幢建筑能拆了异地重建，还能把所有构建重新排列组合
  • 僧房：清道光二十九年1849年大火后重建，高三层坡屋顶平面异形，中间四方小院放生池（防火）
  • 僧房外立面受西方古典主义影响：通高壁柱、三角形山墙、山墙中圆形装饰母题
  • 胡公庙前殿：三开间一层木建筑挂胡公祠横匾，利用拆除大雄宝殿和民居构建重新组装
  • 胡公庙前殿中榀檐柱牛腿雕狮子戏球，叠拱插叶卷草图案和世远公祠相似
  • 胡公庙前殿中榀两金柱间天花八面锥体藻井，正中盘龙，八面绘如来弥勒观音太上老君西游记四主角
  • 胡公庙前殿垂脊末端小雕像：前哪吒托塔李天王，后两名天兵天将
  • 胡公庙大殿：钢筋混凝土支撑两层高建筑，半米多高基础，官式建筑气派
  • 胡公庙大殿正中两米多高玉皇大帝扮相胡公正襟端坐一米多高须弥座上
  • 胡公吃荤：三牲供品摆放须弥座前香案上
  • 胡公头顶半球形藻井绘龙凤，硕大殿堂平时空荡荡庙会期间挤得水泄不通
  • 屏风阁：清道光二十九年1849年大火后重建，主要构件改用石材防火，宣统年间1909至1911年改回木构（狮子吃人传说）
  • 屏风阁利用天然岩洞建造，建筑空间延伸到石洞外，利用石洞上巨石作为楼板加建二层
  • 屏风阁二层以前供玉皇大帝，宣统二年1910年移到地藏殿，一二层无垂直联系
  • 佛家圣地正殿高高供奉道教众神之皇玉皇大帝，中国式宗教无与伦比的博大和宽容

在二十世纪八十年代中叶，以发展旅游业为目的，胡公庙从广慈寺中被分离出来，作为一个独立的寺院，再次和广慈寺肩并肩地坐落在方岩山顶上。胡公庙在西侧，占据了广慈寺原有中轴线的位置，即以屏风阁为最后一进大殿。在屏风阁前刚刚建好没几年的大雄宝殿再次被拆除，取而代之的是两幢新建的胡公庙前殿和大殿。广慈寺则被迫向东平移，改以重建的观音殿为中轴线，并在后面补建了大雄宝殿。

这里的老人们都说，广慈寺以前的各个殿堂之间也都是有围廊相连的，下雨都不会湿鞋。不仅如此，允明禅师讲，以前从山脚到山顶都是有长廊的，是太平村的一个村民每年来修，用了很多年才建成。现任住持释明修也说，在他去应聘广慈寺住持的时候，也曾经见过一张广慈寺的图，上面画着从山下到寺院都有走廊相连。

如今的广慈寺有三进殿堂。一进前厅和二进观音殿为允明禅师于1984年复建；第三进殿堂是在寺、庙分离之后补建的大雄宝殿。观音殿与前厅有侧廊相连，中间围合成一个小的院落。由于是利用祠堂建筑的木料重建，所以无论从大木构件或小木雕饰中都可以看到世俗建筑的影子。一进前厅会让人不禁想起祠堂里的戏台。前厅正中间的神龛里目前供奉着弥勒佛，神龛本身看上去很像戏台上使用的建筑道具。神龛正上方是一个通常只用于祠堂戏台顶上的精致藻井，藻井里的斗拱呈螺旋形层叠上升。边上两间各有一组九宫天花，四周还有小斗拱做支撑，天花上绘制的都是《西游记》中的经典场面。壁画颜色以黄、绿色为底，线条精美，退晕渲染也十分讲究。观音殿和两侧围廊中祠堂建筑的痕迹就更加明显。这其中的大木构件不仅用神牛、寿桃、喜鹊做雕饰主题，还可以看到富有张力的月梁，极富装饰效果的蟮梁以及活灵活现的狮子舞球。广慈寺的这座建筑是中国建筑构造特点和设计理念的最好体现：一幢建筑不仅能拆了异地重建，而且还能把所有构建重新排列组合，建造成平面和功能都不尽相同的新建筑。

方岩山上另外一幢没有在文化大革命期间被拆毁的建筑就是位于现广慈寺东南角的僧房。这幢仅存的僧房是在清道光二十九年（1849年）的那场大火后重建的。僧房高三层，坡屋顶，平面为异形，中间却围合了一个四四方方的小院，院内正中是一个巨大的放生池，池上有小拱桥。说是放生池，其实其主要目的是预防火灾。最值得一提的是，僧房的外立面明显受到西方古典主义建筑的影响：通高的壁柱，三角形山墙，以及山墙中圆形的装饰母题。

胡公庙的第一进殿是一个三开间的一层木建筑，挂胡公祠横匾。虽然我们无法区分建筑的哪些构建来自于拆掉的大雄宝殿，哪些来自于其他建筑，而哪些又是后来新做的，更不用说被允明禅师重建的大雄宝殿本身就是利用拆除后的一幢民居为建筑原料，但是这些都并不重要。乡土建筑的一大魅力就在于它创作中的自由性和追求物尽其用的特点，这种重新组装的做法不但延续了原有建筑物的生命，而且这种再创造本身就具有非常的意义。

前殿大木雕饰所采用的题材，都明显带有当地祠堂建筑的痕迹。例如，两个中榀檐柱的牛腿上就雕有当地祠堂建筑中通常采用的狮子戏球；牛腿上向各个方向飞扬伸展的叠拱和插叶所采用的卷草图案也和世远公祠里的非常相似。不过，前殿里最有特点的部分还要算天花。这里建筑的梁架通常是露明的，但是前殿的中榀的两个金柱中间却有天花，天花中间还形成一个八面锥体的藻井。藻井正中绘有盘龙，八个面上不但绘有如来佛、弥勒佛、观音、太上老君，还有《西游记》里的四个主要人物；四周的天花上则画满《西游记》中的一些主要故事情节。可以说，在这几米见方的二维画板上，已经完全看不到佛教、道教与世俗尘世之间的任何界限。前殿的屋面应该是新的，但在这里人、神之间的界限也是模糊的。垂脊的末端各有一尊小的雕像，前面分别是哪吒、托塔李天王，后面则是两名天兵天将。

胡公庙大殿是一个用钢筋混凝土做支撑结构的两层高建筑，坐落在一个半米多高的基础上，俨然一副官式建筑的气派。大殿正中有一个两米多高、玉皇大帝扮相的胡公正襟端坐在一个一米多高的须弥座上，他略微低头，审视着跪倒在他面前的虔诚香客。依照惯例，这尊胡公是吃荤的，也就是说，三牲之类的供品都会摆放在须弥座前面的香案上。胡公的头顶上也有一个半球形的藻井，上面绘着龙和凤。硕大的一个殿堂，只有一尊塑像在中间，平时显得空荡荡的，但是在庙会期间，据说就会挤得水泄不通。

最后一进殿是屏风阁，现存建筑基本上是清道光二十九年（1849年）那场大火之后重建的。允明禅师说，重建的时候，一些主要构件改用石材以防火灾。可是到清朝末年，寺院变得十分不景气，原本的八房僧人也只剩下了四房，每房的人数也少了许多。于是就有人说是因为狮子，石子的谐音，吃人，所以在宣统年间（1909至1911年），屏风阁又改回了木构。

屏风阁也是利用天然岩洞建造的，但是它和罗汉洞或五峰书院有着根本的不同。屏风阁的建筑空间不但延伸到了石洞以外，而且还利用石洞上的巨石作为楼板，在上面加建了二层。允明禅师说，二层以前供的是玉皇大帝，直到宣统二年（1910年），才把玉皇大帝移到了现在的地藏殿。屏风阁一、二层之间没有任何垂直的联系，像搬佛像这样大的变故，可能只有在重建或大修的时候才可以进行。把这种可望而不可即的位置留给玉皇大帝本是无可厚非，但是在佛家圣地的正殿里高高供奉道教的众神之皇，这不得不归功于中国式宗教无与伦比的博大和宽容。

— —

In the mid-1980s, for tourism development, Hu Gong Temple was separated from Guangci Temple as an independent monastery, once again standing side by side with Guangci on Fangyan summit. Hu Gong Temple occupies the west side, taking Guangci original central axis, with Pingfeng Pavilion as its final hall. The Mahavira Hall, only recently rebuilt before Pingfeng Pavilion, was demolished again and replaced by two new buildings: Hu Gong Temple front hall and main hall. Guangci Temple was forced to shift eastward, adopting the rebuilt Guanyin Hall as its central axis, with a Mahavira Hall added behind.

Elders say Guangci former halls were all connected by surrounding corridors, one never got wet shoes in rain. Moreover, Yunming recounts that covered corridors once ran from the mountain foot to the summit, built over many years by a villager from Taiping Village who came annually to repair them. Current abbot Shi Mingxiu also recalls seeing a Guangci plan showing corridors connecting the mountain foot to the temple when he applied for the abbotship.

Today Guangci has three halls. The first, front hall, and second, Guanyin Hall, were rebuilt by Yunming in 1984; the third, Mahavira Hall, was added after the temple-shrine separation. Guanyin Hall and the front hall connect via side corridors, enclosing a small courtyard. Because rebuilt from ancestral hall timbers, both large timber members and small wood carvings bear secular architectural traces. The front hall evokes an ancestral hall stage. Its central shrine currently enshrines Maitreya Buddha; the shrine itself resembles stage props. Directly above it is an exquisite caisson typically found only on ancestral hall stage roofs, with bracket sets spiraling upward in layers. The two side bays each have a nine-grid ceiling supported by small brackets, painted with classic scenes from Journey to the West. Murals use yellow-green grounds with refined lines and sophisticated shading. Guanyin Hall and surrounding corridors show even stronger ancestral hall traces: large timber members feature divine oxen, longevity peaches, and magpies as carving themes, alongside dynamic crescent beams, highly decorative shan beams, and vivid lions playing with balls. This building exemplifies Chinese architectural construction and design philosophy: a building can not only be dismantled and rebuilt elsewhere, but all its components can be rearranged into new buildings with different plans and functions.

Another building surviving the Cultural Revolution is the monk quarter at Guangci southeast corner. This sole remaining monk quarter was rebuilt after the Daoguang 29 (1849) fire. It is three stories high with a pitched roof, irregular in plan, enclosing a square courtyard with a giant mercy-release pond in the center and a small arch bridge over it. Though called a mercy-release pond, its main purpose was fire prevention. Most notably, its facade clearly shows Western classical influence: full-height pilasters, triangular gables, and circular decorative motifs in the gables.

Hu Gong Temple first hall is a three-bay single-story wooden building with a Hu Gong Shrine horizontal plaque. We cannot distinguish which components came from the demolished Mahavira Hall, which from other buildings, and which are new, not to mention that Yunming rebuilt Mahavira Hall itself used a demolished residence as material, but none of this matters. A great charm of vernacular architecture lies in its creative freedom and pursuit of making full use of materials; this reassembly not only extends the original building life but the re-creation itself is profoundly meaningful.

The front hall large-timber carving themes clearly bear local ancestral hall traces. For example, the corbels on the two central truss eaves pillars feature the lion-playing-with-ball motif common in local ancestral halls; the scrolling foliage patterns on the bracket sets and leaf inserts extending in all directions closely resemble those in Shiyuan Gong Hall. The most distinctive feature is the ceiling. Here the roof framing is usually exposed, but between the two gold pillars of the central truss there is a ceiling with an eight-sided pyramidal caisson. The caisson center depicts a coiling dragon; its eight faces show Tathagata Buddha, Maitreya, Guanyin, Laozi, or Supreme Lord, and the four main characters of Journey to the West; the surrounding ceiling is filled with major story scenes from Journey to the West. On this few-meter-square two-dimensional panel, all boundaries between Buddhism, Daoism, and the secular world vanish. The front hall roof is presumably new, but here the boundary between humans and gods is also blurred. At the ends of the vertical ridges stand small statues: in front, Nezha and Li Jing, or Pagoda-Bearing Heavenly King; behind, two heavenly soldiers.

Hu Gong Temple main hall is a two-story reinforced-concrete structure on a foundation over half a meter high, imposing official-style architecture. In its center, a two-meter-plus Hu Ze in the guise of the Jade Emperor sits solemnly on a sumeru pedestal over one meter high, head slightly bowed, surveying the devout pilgrims kneeling before him. By custom, this Hu Gong eats meat, that is, animal offerings, or three sacrificial animals, are placed on the incense table before the pedestal. Above Hu Gong head is a hemispherical caisson painted with dragons and phoenixes. The vast hall, with only one statue at center, feels empty on ordinary days but is reportedly packed solid during temple fairs.

The final hall is Pingfeng Pavilion, or Screen Pavilion; the existing building was essentially rebuilt after the Daoguang 29 (1849) fire. Yunming says that during reconstruction, major components were changed to stone for fire prevention. But by the late Qing, the temple was in decline, from eight branches of monks to four, with fewer in each. Some claimed this was because lions, homophone for stone pillars, eat people, so during the Xuantong period (1909 to 1911), Pingfeng Pavilion was converted back to timber construction.

Pingfeng Pavilion is also built using a natural rock cave, but fundamentally differs from Luohan Cave or Five Peaks Academy. Its architectural space extends beyond the cave, and the giant rock above the cave serves as a floor slab, with a second story added above it. Yunming says the second story formerly enshrined the Jade Emperor, until the second year of Xuantong (1910), when the Jade Emperor was moved to the current Ksitigarbha Hall. There is no vertical connection between Pingfeng Pavilion first and second stories; major changes like moving a Buddha statue could only happen during reconstruction or major repair. Reserving this inaccessible position for the Jade Emperor is understandable enough, but enshrining Daoism Emperor of All Gods high in the main hall of a Buddhist sanctuary, this must be attributed to the unparalleled breadth and tolerance of Chinese-style religion.

第六章 五峰坳里的书院
Chapter 6: Academy in the Wufeng Valley
寿山往事——五峰地理与桃岩寺
Shoushan Past — Five Peaks Geography and Taoyan Temple
一、寿山往事 | Shoushan Past

要点 Highlights

  • 书院作为文教建筑是科举制度产物：隋大业元年605年至清光绪三十一年1905年
  • 唐末至五代官学衰败，读书人避居山林模仿佛教禅林讲经制度创立书院
  • 浙江唐代书院五处：象山蓬莱、诸暨溪山、龙游九峰、建德青山、绍兴丽正
  • 宋代书院林立，浙江三十余家数量仅次于江西居全国第二
  • 清光绪二十七年1901年诏令各省书院改大学堂、府厅直隶州改中学堂、州县改小学堂
  • 寿山在方岩北三里，五峰皆石壁平地拔起周围如城郭：固厚、瀑布、桃花、覆釜、鸡鸣
  • 固厚峰为主峰，悬崖千仞峭壁如削，其下大石洞高敞轩豁可容千人
  • 瀑布峰上有龙湫水四时不竭直下数十丈如练；覆釜峰巅广而圆如大釜覆其上
  • 桃花峰岩色赤白相间状类桃花；鸡鸣峰午风微过嘐然一声彼唱此应
  • 天蜜悬崖直下天风偶吹大者如珠细者如雾其味甘美故名
  • 梁普通元年520年和尚利用固厚峰下两个大石洞创建永康第一座寺院桃岩寺后改名寿山寺
  • 固厚峰下西洞曾有罗汉堂，明朝建造五峰书院时撤掉罗汉；外洞之前有台三层甚壮伟
  • 寿山寺自明正德以前无论已，三层高台在明万历间始圮

书院作为文教建筑的一种，是中国实行科举制度后的产物。科举制度开始于隋大业元年（605年），到清朝光绪三十一年（1905年）举行最后一科进士考试为止，是中国历代皇朝采用的取士制度。尽管科举制度有许多弊端和局限性，但是其最大意义就在于它给广大平民百姓提供了改变自己以至整个家族社会地位的唯一途径。于是乎，在科举制度实行的一千三百年里，中华大地上就出现了无以计数的学子，不计十年寒窗，孜孜苦读。相伴而来的，就是私塾、书院、书斋、文馆、文庙、文昌阁等文教建筑的广泛普及。

唐末至五代期间，战乱频繁导致官学衰败，所以许多读书人就避居山林，以模仿佛教、禅林的讲经制度创立书院。浙江境内始建于唐朝的书院，现知有五处，分别是象山蓬莱书院、诸暨溪山书院、龙游九峰书院、建德青山书院和绍兴丽正书院。这些书院通常是住宅的一部分，建筑形式也十分简朴。

宋代是书院林立的时代。在北宋期间书院多以讲学为主，而随着理学的发展，南宋的书院逐渐成为各学派活动的场所。书院作为一种正式的教育制度，几乎取代官学而成为当时主要的教育机关。浙江地区当时有书院三十余家，数量仅次于江西，居全国第二。书院制度以及书院建筑在明、清两代又有了进一步的发展，但是已经逐渐从学者云集、自由讲学的私立书院，发展到受统治阶级控制的官学化书院。直至光绪二十七年（1901年），皇帝诏令各省的书院改为大学堂，各府、厅、直隶州的书院改为中学堂，各州县的书院改为小学堂，书院这种教育制度便退出了历史舞台。

在和尚、陈亮、县令、胡氏后人、国民党政府、还有我们这一行人进入寿山坑之前，坑里这五座山峰就已经屹立在那里了。有了这五座山峰，才有了美丽的传说和故事；再然后，才有了接下来所有的事情。

寿山在方岩北三里，有五峰皆石壁平地拔起，周围如城郭，曰固厚、曰瀑布、曰桃花、曰覆釜、曰鸡鸣。固厚之下有大石洞，高敞轩豁，可容千人。瀑布之上有龙湫，水四时不竭，直下数十丈如练，及霁久水杀，飘飘飏之，溅洒四出若雾雨然，可望而不可即，亦奇观也。固厚峰，主峰也。悬崖千仞，峭壁如削，较诸峰为淳固深厚，故其名。其下石洞在焉。瀑布峰，在固厚之右，上有飞泉下注。覆釜峰，在瀑布之下，巅广而圆，如大釜覆其上。桃花峰，与覆釜峰相连，专专耸妙，其岩色赤白相间，状类桃花。鸡鸣峰，午风微过，嘐然一声，彼唱此应，遂神其说耳。天蜜，悬崖直下，天风偶吹，大者如珠，细者如雾，其味甘美故名。

位于五峰之间、石洞之中、瀑布之下的这块世外桃源，终于在南北朝时期的梁普通元年（520年）迎来了它的第一位主人，即吃素念经的和尚们。固厚峰下有两个大石洞，上文中提到的是西边这个，靠东还有一个，叫外洞。这些出家人利用这两个天然的石洞创建了永康县境内的第一座寺院——桃岩寺，并于日后改名为寿山寺。关于寿山寺的具体情况已经无从查证，只知道固厚峰下的这个山洞里曾经有过一个罗汉堂，里面的罗汉还是在明朝为建造五峰书院时撤掉的；外洞之前有台三层，甚壮伟，猜想或许是大雄宝殿的位置。据《库川胡氏宗谱》中关于一场官司的记录，寿山寺自正德以前无论已，那三层的高台也在明万历间始圮。

— —

Academies, as a type of educational architecture, were a product of China imperial examination system. The examination system began in the first year of Daye of Sui (605) and lasted until the final jinshi examination in the 31st year of Guangxu of Qing (1905), serving as the recruitment system for successive dynasties. Despite its many flaws and limitations, its greatest significance was providing the broad common people with the sole path to change their own and their entire family social status. Thus, over the 1,300 years of the examination system, countless scholars studied diligently through ten years of cold-window hardship. Accompanying this was the widespread proliferation of educational architecture: private schools, academies, studies, literary halls, Confucian temples, and Wenchang pavilions.

From late Tang through Five Dynasties, frequent warfare caused official schools to decline, so many scholars retreated to mountains and forests, establishing academies modeled on Buddhist and Chan lecture systems. In Zhejiang, five academies founded in Tang are known: Xiangshan Penglai, Zhuji Xishan, Longyou Jiufeng, Jiande Qingshan, and Shaoxing Lizheng. These academies were usually part of residences with very simple architectural forms.

The Song was an era of flourishing academies. In Northern Song, academies focused on lectures; with the development of Neo-Confucianism, Southern Song academies gradually became venues for various schools of thought. As a formal educational institution, the academy nearly replaced official schools as the primary educational organ. Zhejiang had over thirty academies at the time, second only to Jiangxi nationwide. Academy institutions and architecture further developed in Ming and Qing, but gradually evolved from scholar-gathered free-lecturing private academies into state-controlled official academies. In the 27th year of Guangxu (1901), the emperor ordered provincial academies converted to university halls, prefectural and sub-prefectural academies to middle schools, and county academies to primary schools, ending the academy institution.

Before monks, Chen Liang, magistrates, Hu descendants, the Nationalist government, and our team entered Shoushan Hollow, the five peaks had stood there. With these five peaks came beautiful legends and stories; then came everything that followed.

Shoushan lies three li north of Fangyan, with five peaks of stone cliffs rising sheer from the plain, surrounding like city walls: Guhou, Pubu, Taohua, Fufu, and Jiming. Below Guhou is a great stone cave, high and spacious, capable of holding a thousand people. Above Pubu Peak is Longqiu Pool, its water never failing through four seasons, plunging dozens of zhang like white silk; when the water recedes after long fair weather, it drifts and sprinkles like mist and rain, visible but unreachable, a wondrous sight. Guhou Peak is the main peak, with thousand-ren cliffs sheared like knife cuts, more solid and profound than the other peaks, hence its name; the stone cave lies below it. Pubu Peak, to the right of Guhou, has a flying spring pouring down. Fufu Peak, below Pubu, has a broad round summit like an inverted cauldron. Taohua Peak, connected to Fufu, rises gracefully with red-and-white rock resembling peach blossoms. Jiming Peak, when a midday wind passes, emits a crowing sound, one calling and another answering, hence its mythic reputation. Tianmi, a spring plunging down the cliff, blown by occasional heavenly wind, large drops like pearls, fine ones like mist, sweet in taste, hence its name.

This paradise among five peaks, within stone caves, beneath waterfalls, finally welcomed its first masters in the first year of Putong of Liang (520): vegetarian, scripture-chanting monks. Below Guhou Peak are two great stone caves; the western one mentioned above, and the eastern one called Outer Cave. These monks used the two natural caves to found Yongkang first monastery, Taoyan Temple, later renamed Shoushan Temple. Details of Shoushan Temple are lost; only known is that the western cave once held an Arhat Hall whose arhats were removed when Wufeng Academy was built in Ming; before the Outer Cave stood a three-tier platform, very imposing, conjectured to be the Mahavira Hall site. According to a lawsuit record in the Kuchuan Hu Genealogy, Shoushan Temple was already gone before the Zhengde period, and the three-tier platform began to collapse in the Wanli period.

五峰书院——陈亮讲学与明代书院
Wufeng Academy — Chen Liang Lectures and Ming Dynasty Academy
二、五峰书院 | Wufeng Academy

要点 Highlights

  • 南宋乾道八年1172年永康人陈亮1143至1195年在寿山坑设帐授学，宣讲商籍农而立农赖商而行义利双行各务其实
  • 陈亮挚友吕祖谦1137至1181年（金华学派创始人）也曾来此讲学
  • 宋淳熙九年1182年秋浙东提举朱熹1130至1200年回访陈亮于五峰，陈亮请晦翁主讲席从游者数百人
  • 固厚峰下外洞岩壁上兜率台三个朱砂大字相传为朱熹题写，兜率为梵语音译佛教第四层天
  • 宋绍熙四年1193年陈亮51岁中状元，一年多后死在赴任途中
  • 明正德间应典（字天彝号石门，永康芝英人，正德九年1514年进士）建丽泽祠祀朱熹吕祖谦张栻陆九渊陈亮
  • 丽泽祠名取自金华明招山丽泽书院（吕祖谦宋乾道初年创建），《易·兑》丽泽兑君子以朋友讲习
  • 嘉靖十五年1536年桃岩丽泽精舍建成改名五峰书院，太守姚公命耆老吕瑗建，以撤去阿罗汉象为代价
  • 明末永康学者周佑德在丽泽祠西侧建学易斋（三间两层），纪念婺州学者何基王柏金履祥许谦章懋
  • 明最后一百年著名学者讲学：应典、周桐、卢可久、周莹、应玠、程文德、程正谊、程梓
  • 讲学内容以王阳明致良知学说为主，兼正心诚意和程朱理学，培养三十多位进士
  • 五峰书院三幢建筑位于固厚峰下大石洞内面南自东向西一字排开，均三开间两层楼宇
  • 建筑最大特点即洞支木为之不施椽瓦而雨雪霜露自然莫及，应典名山洞为朝阳洞
  • 五峰书院平面接近方形一层有廊二层有栏，梁架穿斗式横梁不加装饰梁下丁头拱覆盆鼓形柱础
  • 丽泽祠和学易斋规模相当进深较短长方形平面，门窗学易斋和五峰书院为步步锦丽泽祠为满天星
  • 清康熙二十二年1683年知县姬肇燕赐额阐明理学；乾隆四十五年1780年重修彭元瑞赐匾丽泽如新
  • 清代中后期春秋两会成最隆重事，秋会阴历九月初九祀朱吕陈三子次日祀阳明王子三日祀何王金许诸儒
  • 会田由应典程梓卢可久三人创置，清康熙二十年1681年取消春会秋会改九月十二十三日

到了南宋乾道八年（1172年），永康人陈亮（1143—1195年）开始在寿山坑里设帐授学，宣讲他的商籍农而立，农赖商而行、义利双行和各务其实等有悖于传统儒学和经学的新思想。在接下去的两三年中，陈亮的挚友吕祖谦（1137—1181年），即事功学派下金华学派的创始人，也曾来这里讲学。宋淳熙九年（1182年）秋，刚刚上任一年的浙东提举朱熹（1130—1200年）回访陈亮于五峰。这是朱熹与陈亮的第二次会面，第一次是在这年正月，朱熹来婺州赈荒，陈亮赶去相见，并与朱熹畅谈十日。第二次会面的盛况在《五峰书院志》中略有记载，陈亮请晦翁主讲席，从游者数百人。相传固厚峰下外洞岩壁上的兜率台三个朱砂大字就是朱熹当时题写的。这里的兜率二字同兜率天，为梵语音译，代表佛教中的第四层天，这层天的内院是弥勒菩萨的净土，外院是天上众生所居之处；台应该指的是当时在这里的三层高台。宋绍熙四年（1193年），年已五十一岁的陈亮金榜题名中了状元，却不幸在一年多后死在赴任的途中。在这以后，我们无从查证寿山坑里是否依然回荡有士大夫高亢的辩论；或是仅留下与晨钟暮鼓相伴的木鱼声；还是一切都静了下来，只听得见水激顽石和风吹树叶的声音。

再次找到有关于寿山坑的文字记载，已经到明正德年间（1506—1521年）。尚宝丞应典，周视壁上有龙川书，志晦翁、东莱行迹，乃即堂东偏之隙建祠，以祀朱、吕、及张南轩、陆象山，而龙川配焉，曰丽泽祠。丽泽祠，正德间应石门先生建，太守张公钺题此额，嗣守姚公文炤记。应典字天彝，号石门，浙江永康芝英人，明正德九年（1514年）进士，曾任丽泽书院和白鹿书院山长。这也就是说丽泽祠应该建于1514年到1521年之间。这里的朱、吕、张指的是朱熹、吕祖谦和张栻，在宋乾道、淳熙时期（1165—1189年），他们三人被并称为东南三贤。除了这三个人之外，还以陆九渊和陈亮配祭。

丽泽这个名字，可能取自于位于金华明招山的丽泽书院。丽泽书院原名丽泽堂，为吕祖谦在宋乾道初年（约1165—1166年）创建。书院前有两个湖泊，而丽泽的原意为两个沼泽相连，所以恰到好处。更重要的是，《易·兑》中写道，丽泽，兑。君子以朋友讲习。由此可见，丽泽之意深远，朱熹还曾经做过如下的评价，两泽相丽，互相滋益，朋友讲习，其象如此。

十几年之后，丽泽祠东侧又建造了一幢规模稍大的建筑，取名为桃岩丽泽精舍。当时的永康知县洪坦写道，嘉靖癸巳秋，余偕虚谷姚公访石门子于岩，览遗迹名胜，作而叹曰：朱子欲屋之而未成，盖有所待也，乃命耆老吕瑗购材兴工，因其中岩下为楼数间，以广来学者，犹欲知先人丽泽之义，即名桃岩丽泽精舍。嘉靖十五年（1536年），桃岩丽泽精舍建成，并改名为五峰书院。五峰书院，嘉靖丙申太守姚公命耆老吕瑗建，原名桃岩丽泽精舍。邑侯洪坦记，嗣守陈公京书。相比丽泽祠要盖在堂东偏之隙，桃岩丽泽精舍的建造则是以撤去阿罗汉象为代价的。明末，永康学者周佑德在丽泽祠西侧又建造了一幢与丽泽祠规模相当，也是三间两层的建筑，取名为学易斋。

在明朝最后的一百年里，有许多著名的学者都曾在五峰书院讲学，其中包括尚宝丞应典、江西抚州教授周桐、著名学者卢可久、周莹、应玠等人。这其中还有与岩下程氏同祖的三位程氏后人，他们是吏部左侍郎程文德（1497—1559年）、四川左布政程正谊（1531—1610年）和他的父亲程梓（1498—1585年）。来讲学的人大都是王阳明的弟子，讲习内容以倡导致良知学说为主，同时也包括正心诚意和程朱理学的部分内容。五峰书院在这段时间培养了许多优秀的人才，其中包括三十多位进士。

五峰书院除了广来学者和提供讲学场所之外，祭祀活动也是很重要的一个功能。丽泽祠创建之初共祀五人，但是程文德、应延育、周桐以陆九渊和张栻从未到过五峰为缘由，改仅祀朱熹、吕祖谦和陈亮三人。五峰书院则主祀阳明王文成公；配祀石门应先生，方峰程先生。学易斋的建造原本就是以纪念婺州学者何基、王柏、金履祥、许谦和章懋为初衷的。除了这五人之外，学易斋里还先后共祀二十三人，其中不仅包括曾来五峰书院讲学的周桐、周莹等著名学者，还有程鋐等当地乡绅。

随着五峰书院的名气越来越大，来赐额题联的名人也越来越多。清康熙二十二年（1683年），永康知县姬肇燕赐额阐明理学。乾隆四十五年（1780年），永康太平吕大宗祠和程、应、卢三姓后裔共同出资，重修五峰书院的三幢建筑，并请提督学使彭元瑞赐匾。次年一月彭元瑞亲自书写丽泽如新匾额。文楼村的程氏进士们也先后多次题写对联，例如五峰书院这幢建筑中就挂有程兆选题写的学则数言矩矱遥承鹿洞；心传一脉渊源近溯姚江，还有《五峰书院志》的作者程尚斐题写的学术启良知，恍示鸢飞鱼跃；讲堂开胜地，何殊鹿洞鹅湖。

进入清代中后期，讲学的气氛日渐衰退，每年的春秋两会便成了五峰书院最隆重的事情。会期各三天，届时前来讲学、奉祀的地方官绅多达数百人，其盛况不亚于朱熹来这里讲学的日子。关于春会的时间，《五峰书院志》里并没有提到，依惯例或许是在清明；秋天则在阴历九月初九，每岁重阳日，祀朱、吕、陈三子，次日祀阳明王子，三日祀何、王、金、许诸儒。关于祭祀的费用，则来自于应典、程梓和卢可久三人创置的会田，并由应、程、卢的后人负责料理会田和组织祭祀活动。会田创立之后，陈亮、程文德、王柏和吕祖谦的后人也相继捐出了部分田地。但是由于会田的收入还要支付日常开销，以及建筑修缮的费用，所以依旧入不敷出。清康熙二十年（1681年），五峰书院不得不取消春会，并把秋会祭祀的时间改为阴历九月十二、十三两日。五峰书院秋会不仅包括启龛、迎神、读祝文、献礼、献饭等通常意义上的祭祀活动，更是各路学者交流和学习的绝好机会。为此会规里有明确的规定，十二日早膳后各出质言以相参证，其式正楷大书，登名于尾。凡与会者各带纸笔，午后互相抄录完时，实粘壁上。十三日早膳后，老成者各拈书一章，性理一章。三日内余功，或质疑问难、或阐发格言，言具登载于簿。五峰书院秋会基本每年举行，一直由应、程、卢的后人负责，直到民国时期停办。

五峰书院的三幢建筑均位于固厚峰下能容千人的大石洞内，面南自东向西一字排开，均为三开间两层楼宇。其建筑的最大特点就是即洞支木为之，不施椽瓦而雨雪霜露自然莫及。丽泽祠的建造者应典还给这个山洞起名为朝阳洞，因为朝日初出，苍苍凉凉，光射户牖。最东边的是五峰书院，其平面接近方形，一层有廊，二层有栏以供凭眺。虽然屡经修缮，但是梁架结构并没有改变，所以仍然可以看到明代建筑的特点，例如梁架采用穿斗式，横梁不加装饰，梁下采用丁头拱，使用有覆盆的鼓形柱础等。建筑东墙外有一间小室，曾作为厨房。丽泽祠和学易斋规模相当，都是进深较短的长方形平面，只是丽泽祠一层有廊。在门窗的处理上，学易斋和五峰书院较为类似，都是步步锦，丽泽祠则采用较为简单的满天星样式。

— —

In the eighth year of Qiandao of Southern Song (1172), Yongkang native Chen Liang (1143-1195) began teaching in Shoushan Hollow, propagating his new ideas contrary to traditional Confucianism and classical studies: merchants rely on agriculture to stand, agriculture relies on merchants to prosper, righteousness and profit both pursued, and each attends to its reality. Over the next two or three years, his close friend Lü Zuqian (1137-1181), founder of the Jinhua school under the utilitarian school, also lectured here. In the autumn of the ninth year of Chunxi (1182), Zhu Xi (1130-1200), newly appointed as Eastern Zhejiang Commissioner, visited Chen Liang at Wufeng. This was their second meeting; the first was that January when Zhu Xi came to Wuzhou for famine relief and Chen Liang rushed to meet him, talking for ten days. The grandeur of the second meeting is briefly recorded in the Wufeng Academy Chronicle: Chen Liang invited Master Huiweng to the main lecture seat, with several hundred followers. Legend says the three cinnabar characters Doushuai Terrace on the Outer Cave rock wall below Guhou Peak were written by Zhu Xi at that time. Doushuai, Sanskrit transliteration, refers to the fourth Buddhist heaven; its inner court is Maitreya pure land, outer court dwelling of heavenly beings; Terrace refers to the three-tier platform then standing there. In the fourth year of Shaoxi (1193), Chen Liang, aged 51, passed the palace examination as zhuangyuan, but unfortunately died en route to his post over a year later. Thereafter, we cannot verify whether Shoushan Hollow still echoed with lofty scholar debates; or only wooden fish sounds accompanying morning bells and evening drums; or whether all fell silent, with only water striking rocks and wind in leaves.

The next written record of Shoushan Hollow appears in the Zhengde period (1506-1521). Shangbao Cheng Ying Dian, observing Longchuan writings on the walls, recording Huiweng and Donglai traces, built a shrine in the space east of the hall to sacrifice to Zhu, Lü, Zhang Nanxuan, and Lu Xiangshan, with Longchuan as associate, called Lize Shrine. Lize Shrine was built by Master Ying Shimen in Zhengde, with the plaque inscribed by Governor Zhang Yue and record by successor Governor Yao Wenzhao. Ying Dian, courtesy name Tianyi, hao Shimen, native of Zhiying, Yongkang, jinshi in the ninth year of Zhengde (1514), served as head of Lize Academy and White Deer Academy. Thus Lize Shrine was built between 1514 and 1521. Zhu, Lü, Zhang refer to Zhu Xi, Lü Zuqian, and Zhang Shi, collectively called the Three Worthies of the Southeast in the Qiandao-Chunxi period (1165-1189). Besides these three, Lu Jiuyuan and Chen Liang were sacrificed as associates.

The name Lize may derive from Lize Academy at Mingzhao Mountain in Jinhua. Lize Academy, originally Lize Hall, was founded by Lü Zuqian in the early Qiandao period (c. 1165-1166). Two lakes lay before the academy, and Lize originally means two connected marshes, aptly fitting. More importantly, the Book of Changes, Hexagram Dui states: Lize, Dui. The gentleman uses friends for discussion and practice. Thus Lize carries profound meaning; Zhu Xi evaluated: Two marshes join, mutually nourishing, friends discussing and practicing, such is its image.

Over a decade later, a larger building was built east of Lize Shrine, named Taoyan Lize Jingshe. Yongkang Magistrate Hong Tan wrote: In the autumn of Jiajing Guisi, I visited Master Shimen at the rock with Yao Xugu, viewing relics and scenic spots, sighing: Master Zhu wished to build here but did not complete it, awaiting the right time; so I ordered elder Lü Yuan to purchase materials and begin construction, building several rooms beneath the central rock to welcome scholars, preserving the ancestral Lize meaning, named Taoyan Lize Jingshe. In the 15th year of Jiajing (1536), Taoyan Lize Jingshe was completed and renamed Wufeng Academy. Wufeng Academy, built by elder Lü Yuan under Governor Yao in Jiajing Bingshen, originally named Taoyan Lize Jingshe. Record by Magistrate Hong Tan, calligraphy by successor Governor Chen Jing. Unlike Lize Shrine built in the east gap of the hall, Taoyan Lize Jingshe was built at the cost of removing the arhat statues. In late Ming, Yongkang scholar Zhou Youde built Xueyi Studio west of Lize Shrine, similar in scale, also three bays two stories.

In the last century of Ming, many famous scholars lectured at Wufeng Academy, including Shangbao Cheng Ying Dian, Jiangxi Fuzhou Professor Zhou Tong, renowned scholars Lu Kejiu, Zhou Ying, Ying Jie, and others. Among them were three Cheng descendants sharing ancestry with Yanxia Cheng: Left Vice Minister of Personnel Cheng Wende (1497-1559), Sichuan Left Administrative Commissioner Cheng Zhengyi (1531-1610), and his father Cheng Zi (1498-1585). Lecturers were mostly Wang Yangming disciples, focusing on the Extension of Innate Knowledge doctrine, plus Making the Heart Sincere and Intent Upright and parts of Cheng-Zhu Neo-Confucianism. Wufeng Academy cultivated many outstanding talents in this period, including over thirty jinshi.

Besides welcoming scholars and providing lecture space, sacrificial activities were an important function. Lize Shrine originally sacrificed to five, but Cheng Wende, Ying Yanyu, and Zhou Tong, citing that Lu Jiuyuan and Zhang Shi never visited Wufeng, changed to sacrifice only Zhu Xi, Lü Zuqian, and Chen Liang. Wufeng Academy mainly sacrificed to Wang Yangming, Duke Wencheng; associates Master Ying Shimen and Master Cheng Fangfeng. Xueyi Studio was originally built to commemorate Wuzhou scholars He Ji, Wang Bai, Jin Lüxiang, Xu Qian, and Zhang Mao. Besides these five, Xueyi Studio successively sacrificed to twenty-three people, including famous scholars Zhou Tong and Zhou Ying who lectured at Wufeng, and local gentry such as Cheng Hong.

As Wufeng Academy fame grew, more celebrities bestowed plaques and couplets. In the 22nd year of Kangxi (1683), Yongkang Magistrate Ji Zhaoyan bestowed the plaque Elucidating Neo-Confucianism. In the 45th year of Qianlong (1780), the Yongkang Taiping Lü Great Ancestral Hall and Cheng, Ying, Lu descendants jointly funded the renovation of the three buildings, requesting Education Commissioner Peng Yuanrui to inscribe a plaque. The following January, Peng Yuanrui personally wrote Lize Renewed. Wulou Village Cheng jinshi also wrote many couplets, such as Cheng Zhaoxuan Learning follows several words, standards remotely inherit Ludong; mind transmits one lineage, origin traces near to Yaojiang, and Wufeng Academy Chronicle author Cheng Shangfei Learning awakens innate knowledge, seemingly showing kites flying and fish leaping; lecture hall opens at scenic spot, no different from Ludong and Ehu.

In mid-to-late Qing, lecture atmosphere declined, and the annual spring and autumn assemblies became Wufeng Academy most solemn events. Each lasted three days, with several hundred local officials and gentry coming to lecture and sacrifice, grandeur no less than Zhu Xi visit. Spring assembly time is not mentioned in the Chronicle, perhaps Qingming by custom; autumn on the ninth day of ninth lunar month: every year on Double Ninth, sacrifice to Zhu, Lü, Chen; next day to Wang Yangming; third day to He, Wang, Jin, Xu scholars. Sacrifice expenses came from collective land established by Ying Dian, Cheng Zi, and Lu Kejiu, managed by their descendants. After its establishment, descendants of Chen Liang, Cheng Wende, Wang Bai, and Lü Zuqian also donated land. But because collective land income also covered daily expenses and building repairs, it remained insufficient. In the 20th year of Kangxi (1681), Wufeng Academy cancelled the spring assembly and changed autumn sacrifice to the 12th and 13th of ninth lunar month. The autumn assembly included not only usual rituals, opening shrine, welcoming deities, reading prayer, presenting gifts and meals, but also excellent opportunities for scholar exchange. Regulations specified: on the 12th after breakfast, each presents substantive remarks for mutual verification, written in large regular script with name at end; all bring paper and pen, after noon mutual copying completed, paste on wall. On the 13th after breakfast, elders each select a book chapter and a nature-principle chapter. Over three days, remaining time for questioning, debate, or elucidating maxims, all recorded in register. The autumn assembly was held nearly annually, managed by Ying, Cheng, Lu descendants until the Republic period.

The three Wufeng Academy buildings all lie within the great stone cave below Guhou Peak capable of holding a thousand people, facing south in a row east to west, all three-bay two-story buildings. Their greatest architectural feature is: timber erected within the cave, no rafters or tiles, yet rain, snow, frost, and dew naturally cannot reach. Ying Dian named the cave Chaoyang Cave, because as the morning sun first emerges, cool and clear, light shines through windows and doors. The easternmost is Wufeng Academy, nearly square in plan, with a corridor on first floor and railing on second for viewing. Though repeatedly repaired, the timber frame unchanged, still showing Ming features: column-and-tie construction, undecorated crossbeams, dingtou brackets under beams, and drum-shaped column bases with inverted-basin molding. A small room outside the east wall served as kitchen. Lize Shrine and Xueyi Studio are similar in scale, both shallow rectangular plans, only Lize Shrine has a first-floor corridor. For doors and windows, Xueyi Studio and Wufeng Academy use bubujin lattice, while Lize Shrine uses simpler mantianxing pattern.

胡公家庙——寿山坑的胡氏家庙与重楼
Hu Gong Family Shrine — Hu Family Shrine and Chonglou in Shoushan Hollow
三、胡公家庙 | Hu Gong Family Shrine

要点 Highlights

  • 五峰书院东边外洞为寿山寺旧刹，明正德以前名存实亡，盖桃岩丽泽精舍时移走罗汉像
  • 嘉靖三十七年1558年奉例清查寿山废寺产业买价入官，徐一勤用五十一两白银买五十七亩田二十一亩地十八亩山林近九亩塘
  • 胡氏后人以寿山为祖墓所在、寿山寺产业系伊故祖胡士胜舍助却被异姓官买为由，打十八年官司
  • 田地山塘从徐家转程家再转吕家，胡氏万历八年1580年十一月以一百九十两赎回
  • 万历三十六年1608年库川胡氏决定利用洞口原有三层高台基础建造胡公家庙
  • 广慈寺僧人如昌把胡氏告上公堂，程氏代管山林为一审幕后支持，二审保住田地山塘但建庙维持原判拆毁
  • 判决理由：方岩有像四方香火供奉，寿山去彼一里许又安得重建祠宇，惟将寿山新建未成者拆毁
  • 清顺治十一年1654年胡氏资助广慈寺修理前厅；康熙二十二年1683年胡公家庙建成
  • 康熙四十一年1702年胡氏捐田助寺，双方协定方岩祭礼约，友好关系延续至1840年
  • 1840年程氏介入双方再次对簿公堂，胡氏胜诉但广慈寺道光二十九年1849年九月初四大火
  • 胡氏认为天意，决定从此放弃方岩大殿仅去寿山祭祀
  • 民国23年1934年胡氏在永康罗骏超建议下筹划重建，1935年始1937年完工，即今固厚峰下重楼
  • 重楼坐落在石材垒砌高大基座上，东西两侧地面高差一层多高，一层五间二层三层原本各三间
  • 三层利用岩壁围合空间，岩壁上兜率台三个大字清晰可见，二层空间高且进深为前后二进一直深入洞中
  • 重楼砖木混合结构，外立面砖墙竖向承重辅以木梁木质门窗，内部传统木构为主
  • 建筑式样受新时代影响：东侧入口砖砌发券西洋式小门楼，二层三层栏杆机器加工产品
  • 民国27年1938年浙江省政府迁方岩临时办公，财政厅进驻胡公家庙二层三层
  • 因房间不够二层两侧各增加一间，为采光所有门窗装玻璃，得匾春帆楼
  • 省政府主席黄绍竑题联：庙貌壮灵岩历宋元明清史册相传会栋宇更新欣兹后起多才争辉谱谍；官廨邻胜境仰聪明正直风微若接待湖山重整好与斯邦父母共荐馨香

五峰书院东边不远就是外洞，此寿山寺旧刹也，今为胡公庙。据《库川胡氏宗谱》记载，寿山寺在明正德（1506—1521年）以前就已经名存实亡，十多年后为了盖桃岩丽泽精舍又移走了洞里的罗汉像。

到了嘉靖三十七年（1558年），奉例清查寿山废寺产业，买价入官，一个叫徐一勤的人用五十一两白银买下了寿山坑里的五十七亩田、二十一亩多的地、十八亩多的山林和近九亩的塘。四年之后，胡氏的后人以寿山为祖墓所在，并且寿山寺虽久废，所存田地山场等业系伊故祖胡士胜舍助，却被异姓官买为由，开始了一场长达十八年之久的官司。这块田地山塘在这期间又被转手多次，从徐家转到程家，再从程家转到吕家，最终胡氏在万历八年（1580年）十一月，以一百九十两赎回。

万历三十六年（1608年），库川胡氏后人聚集在寿山坑里，决定利用洞口原有三层高台的基础建造一个胡公家庙。或许是广慈寺害怕这个正宗的胡公会抢了自己的香火，或许老街上的程氏也担心这个半路杀出来的胡公家庙会让老街从此变得萧条，于是一个叫如昌的僧人便把胡氏告上了公堂。从以程氏代管山林的一审结果来看，程氏应该也是这场官司的幕后支持或是怂恿者。胡氏当然不服，把官司告上了金华府，二审结果虽然保住了胡氏的田地山塘，但是建胡公家庙这件事，还是维持原判。原因是方岩有像，四方香火供奉……寿山去彼一里许，又安得重建祠宇耶……惟将寿山新建未成者拆毁。

经过这次教训之后，远在胡库的胡氏似乎明白了要想在寿山坑站稳脚跟，就要讨好邻山广慈寺的道理，于是在清顺治十一年（1654年），胡氏慷慨资助广慈寺修理前厅。随后，在康熙二十二年（1683年），寿山坑里的胡公家庙就建成了。康熙四十一年（1702年），胡氏又捐田助寺。在这之后的一百多年里，胡氏不仅多次资助广慈寺翻修大殿，双方还协定方岩祭礼约。这种友好的关系一直延续到1840年，在程氏的介入下双方再次对簿公堂。虽然胡氏胜诉，但是广慈寺于道光二十九年（1849年）九月初四合庙并僧房尽皆同禄，胡氏认为这是天意，决定从此放弃方岩大殿，仅去寿山祭祀。

民国23年（1934年），胡氏在当时永康罗骏超的建议下开始筹划重建胡公家庙。重建工程自1935年始，至1937年完工，于是就有了今天矗立在固厚峰下的重楼。

新建成的胡公家庙坐落在一个用石材垒砌的高大基座上，基座东西两侧的地面高差有一层多高。它的一层有五间，二层、三层原本各三间。三层利用岩壁围合空间，岩壁上兜率台三个大字清晰可见。建筑一层和三层进深较浅，二层不但空间高，而且进深为前后二进，一直深入洞中。重楼在结构上采用砖木混合形式，外立面用砖墙做竖向承重结构，辅以木梁和木质门窗，内部做法比较传统，以木构为主。建筑式样上则明显受到新时代的影响，东侧入口处为砖砌发券，西洋式小门楼，二层三层栏杆的做法也显然是机器加工的产品。

民国27年（1938年），浙江省政府迁至方岩临时办公，其中财政厅进驻胡公家庙的二层和三层。例如因房间不够，二层两侧便被各增加一间；为解决采光的问题，所有的门窗还被装上了玻璃。国民政府进驻期间，胡公家庙还得匾春帆楼，省政府主席黄绍竑还亲自题联庙貌壮灵岩，历宋元明清，史册相传，会栋宇更新，欣兹后起多才争辉谱谍；官廨邻胜境，仰聪明正直，风微若接，待湖山重整，好与斯邦父母共荐馨香。

— —

Not far east of Wufeng Academy is the Outer Cave, the former site of Shoushan Temple, now Hu Gong Temple. According to the Kuchuan Hu Genealogy, Shoushan Temple existed in name only before the Zhengde period (1506-1521); over a decade later, the arhat statues in the cave were removed to build Taoyan Lize Jingshe.

In the 37th year of Jiajing (1558), following regulations to清查 abandoned temple property and sell it to the government, a man named Xu Yiqin bought 57 mu of farmland, over 21 mu of dry land, over 18 mu of forest, and nearly 9 mu of ponds in Shoushan Hollow for 51 taels of silver. Four years later, Hu descendants, citing Shoushan as their ancestral tomb site and that Shoushan Temple remaining lands were donated by their ancestor Hu Shisheng but illegally purchased by outsiders, began an 18-year lawsuit. The lands changed hands multiple times, from Xu to Cheng to Lü, until the Hu family redeemed them in the 11th month of Wanli 8 (1580) for 190 taels.

In the 36th year of Wanli (1608), Kuchuan Hu descendants gathered in Shoushan Hollow and decided to build a Hu Gong Family Shrine on the foundation of the original three-tier platform at the cave mouth. Perhaps Guangci Temple feared this authentic Hu Gong would steal its incense, or perhaps Yanxia Cheng worried this sudden Hu Gong Family Shrine would make the old street decline, a monk named Ruchang sued the Hu family. From the first-instance result with Cheng managing the forest, Cheng was likely the behind-the-scenes supporter or instigator. The Hu family appealed to Jinhua Prefecture; the second instance preserved their lands but upheld the original verdict against building the shrine, reasoning: Fangyan has the statue, receiving incense from all directions; Shoushan is but a li away, how could another shrine be built? The unfinished new building at Shoushan shall be demolished.

After this lesson, the Hu family, far away in Huku, seemed to understand that to gain a foothold in Shoushan Hollow they must please neighboring Guangci Temple. In the 11th year of Shunzhi (1654), the Hu family generously funded Guangci Temple front hall repair. Then, in the 22nd year of Kangxi (1683), the Hu Gong Family Shrine in Shoushan Hollow was completed. In the 41st year of Kangxi (1702), the Hu family donated land to support the temple. Over the following century, the Hu family not only repeatedly funded Guangci Temple main hall renovations but both sides agreed on the Fangyan Sacrifice Ritual Pact. This friendly relationship lasted until 1840, when Cheng intervention brought them to court again. Though the Hu family won, Guangci Temple burned entirely along with monk quarters on the fourth day of ninth month, Daoguang 29 (1849). The Hu family regarded this as heaven will and decided to abandon Fangyan main hall forever, sacrificing only at Shoushan.

In the 23rd year of the Republic (1934), the Hu family, on the suggestion of Yongkang Luo Junchao, began planning to rebuild the Hu Gong Family Shrine. Reconstruction started in 1935 and finished in 1937, resulting in today Chonglou, or Multi-Story Building, standing below Guhou Peak.

The newly built Hu Gong Family Shrine sits on a tall stone-masonry base, with over one-story height difference between east and west ground levels. It has five bays on the first floor, originally three bays each on second and third. The third floor uses the rock wall to enclose space, with the three large characters Doushuai Terrace clearly visible on the cliff. First and third floors are shallow in depth; the second floor is not only high-ceilinged but has a two-bay front-to-back depth extending deep into the cave. Chonglou uses a brick-wood mixed structure: exterior walls are brick for vertical load-bearing, supplemented by timber beams and wooden doors/windows; interior is more traditional, mainly timber construction. The architectural style clearly shows New Era influence: the east entrance has brick arch vaults and a Western-style small gatehouse; second and third floor railings are evidently machine-made products.

In the 27th year of the Republic (1938), the Zhejiang provincial government moved to Fangyan for temporary office, with the Finance Department occupying the second and third floors of Hu Gong Family Shrine. Due to insufficient rooms, one bay was added to each side of the second floor; to solve lighting, all doors and windows were fitted with glass. During the Nationalist government occupation, the shrine received the plaque Chunfan Lou, and Provincial Government Chairman Huang Shaohong personally wrote the couplet: Shrine majesty strengthens Lingyan, through Song Yuan Ming Qing, history transmitted, halls renewed, rejoicing that later talents shine in genealogy; government office neighbors scenic spot, revering wisdom and uprightness, faint influence continuing, awaiting lakes and mountains restored, to join this land parents in offering fragrance.

没有了进士的五峰书院——从校舍到展览馆
Wufeng Academy Without Jinshi — From School Building to Exhibition Hall
四、没有了进士的五峰书院 | Wufeng Academy Without Jinshi

要点 Highlights

  • 没有了科举考试没有了进士，书院各种活动在民国时期全部停止
  • 五峰书院与胡公家庙在民国27年1938年到31年1942年作为省政府临时办公场所
  • 政府工作人员走后第一年，五峰书院作为永康师范学校校舍一直到1962年
  • 如今五峰书院变成展览馆，设有书院历史、书院制度、书院功能、书院与文房四宝、书院与科举制度、五峰书院与永康学派等专题展览
  • 瀑布下、山洞里、水塘边设有茶室颇具意境
  • 没有了进士和学者穿梭其间，没有了晦翁主讲席从游者数百人的热闹场面
  • 没有了或质疑问难或阐发格言的高声论辩，五峰书院寂静得可怕
  • 静得连偶尔来参观的游客都不愿意在这本应该诗意盎然的茶室里小坐，便毅然转身离去

没有了科举考试，没有了进士，书院曾经举办的各种活动在民国时期就全部停止了。与胡公家庙一起，五峰书院在民国27年（1938年）到31年（1942年），也曾作为省政府的临时办公场所。或许是因为近千年来，曾经在这里徘徊的进士和学者们都久久不愿离去，所以政府工作人员走后的第一年，五峰书院就作为永康师范学校的校舍，一直到1962年。

如今，学生们也走了。空荡荡的五峰书院变成了展览馆，设有书院历史、书院制度、书院功能、书院与文房四宝、书院与科举制度、五峰书院与永康学派等专题展览，甚至还有祭堂。瀑布下、山洞里、水塘边还设有茶室，倒也颇具些意境。但是不知怎的，没有了进士和学者们穿梭其间，没有了晦翁主讲席，从游者数百人的热闹场面，没有了或质疑问难、或阐发格言的高声论辩，五峰书院便寂静的可怕。静得连偶尔来参观的游客，都不愿意在这本应该是诗意盎然的茶室里小坐，便毅然转身离去，只留下他们匆匆的脚步声回荡在寿山坑里。

— —

Without the imperial examination and without jinshi, all activities once held by the academy ceased entirely in the Republican period. Together with Hu Gong Family Shrine, Wufeng Academy served as temporary provincial government office from the 27th to 31st year of the Republic (1938-1942). Perhaps because for nearly a thousand years, the jinshi and scholars who once wandered here were reluctant to leave, in the first year after government staff departed, Wufeng Academy became the school building of Yongkang Normal School, remaining so until 1962.

Today, the students are also gone. The empty Wufeng Academy has become an exhibition hall, with thematic displays on academy history, academy institutions, academy functions, academy and the four treasures of the study, academy and the imperial examination system, Wufeng Academy and the Yongkang School, and even a sacrificial hall. Tea rooms are set beneath waterfalls, within caves, and by ponds, quite evocative in atmosphere. Yet somehow, without jinshi and scholars moving among them, without the bustling scene of Master Huiweng at the lecture seat with hundreds of followers, without the loud debates of questioning and challenging or elucidating maxims, Wufeng Academy has become terrifyingly silent. So silent that even occasional visiting tourists are unwilling to sit a moment in what should be a poetically evocative tea room, but turn and leave resolutely, leaving only their hurried footsteps echoing in Shoushan Hollow.

第七章 建筑的雕饰与装修
Chapter 7: Architectural Decoration and Finishing
大木作雕饰
Major Carpentry Ornamentation
建筑的雕饰与装修 | Architectural Ornamentation and Decoration

要点 Highlights

  • 老街建筑装饰以木雕为主，石雕砖雕不普遍，彩画保留少
  • 马腿（牛腿）位于檐柱上方向天井垂直出挑，承托金枋，通体透雕
  • 梭梁（月梁）气堂凹槽加阳刻卷草纹，不同时期风格演变明显
  • 蟮梁（转梁）仅祠堂正堂使用，级别高，与三抽互斥
  • 雀替、叠拱、插叶、梁斗、梁斗垫、柱斗等构件各有雕饰

老街建筑的装饰以木雕为主，石雕和砖雕并不普遍，彩画保留下来的也很少。过去匠人多从东阳请来，现在只有程高明（1942年生，第22代圣字辈）一位大木匠仍在从事这项工作。

马腿，即牛腿，位于檐柱上方向天井垂直出挑，承托金枋。马腿为倒楔形体，通体透雕，三面都有视觉效果。简单题材为三狮戏球，复杂题材则有人物故事，如王母骑凤、玉皇大帝乘龙等。程昭夫祠堂的马腿最为精美。马腿垫上常雕吉祥字或四字成语。胡公庙还有盘龙马腿。

梭梁，即月梁，中部有气堂凹槽，凹槽内阳刻卷草纹。不同时期的梭梁风格有明显演变：作勳公祠和世远公祠的梭梁较浅自然；程昭夫祠堂的梭梁较深生硬；义丰雅记五架梁尽端妖娆；程立重宅院气堂内侧卷草长达半米；檐下梭梁中部雕凤凰、蝙蝠、画卷、扇面等。

梁下巴，即梁托，形制较为程式化。义丰南院正房檐廊下有四组以城市建筑为主题的三维立体雕饰，三四厘米高的房子门窗比例正确，可见瓦线和雀替，轮廓线脚变为花瓣，曲形底面深雕菊花。

蟮梁，即转梁，位于梁架靠上部分，一端固定在柱子上，牵引支撑桁条叠拱。蟮梁仅在祠堂正堂使用，级别较高。绍常祠堂的蟮梁有盘龙吐水五彩水花彩绘；程昭夫祠堂的蟮梁为抽象龙眼；周氏宗祠的蟮梁为椭圆抽象卷纹。三抽即三架梁，与蟮梁互斥：绍常中榀抬梁用三抽，边榀穿斗用蟮梁。

米梁、桁条、挑檐枋等承重结构一般不做雕饰，但祠堂露明梁架的底面成为雕饰载体。周氏宗祠米梁浅刻抽象几何纹；程昭夫祠堂金桁枋、挑檐枋浮雕花草、凤凰、仙鹤、飞鸟、鱼纹、狮子戏球等；绍常祠堂上金桁枋彩色勾连花卉，前下金桁枋、挑檐枋透雕花卉，檐枋透雕鱼纹花鸟，极为精美。桁条有时改为方形，并加两三层木板做透雕。

其他雕饰构件包括雀替、叠拱、插叶、梁斗、梁斗垫、柱斗等，题材多为卷草、花卉、回纹、吉祥字。世远公祠有卷草纹叠拱插叶；绍常祠堂有花形梁斗附书卷状梁斗垫、盆形梁斗附荷花状梁斗垫、雕飞鸟叠拱彩绘梁斗刻寿字梁斗垫；义丰雅记有回纹雀替；绍常祠堂后堂檐廊有花木鸟兽雀替、正堂有亭台楼阁雀替；程立重宅院有卷草纹雀替。

— —

Architectural decoration in the old street is dominated by wood carving; stone and brick carving are uncommon, and few painted decorations survive. In the past, craftsmen were hired from Dongyang; today only Cheng Gaoming (born 1942, 22nd generation Sheng character) remains as a master carpenter.

The ma tui (ox leg), or corbel, projects vertically from the top of the eave column toward the courtyard and supports the jin fang (gold tie beam). It is an inverted wedge shape, entirely openwork carved, with visual effect on three sides. Simple motifs include three lions playing with a ball; complex motifs depict narrative scenes such as the Queen Mother riding a phoenix or the Jade Emperor riding a dragon. The corbels at Cheng Zhaofu Ancestral Hall are the finest. The corbel pad is often carved with auspicious characters or four-character idioms. Hu Gong Temple features dragon-coiled corbels.

The suo liang (shuttle beam), or moon beam, has a concave qi tang (air hall) in the center with positive-relief scrolling grass patterns. Styles evolved markedly across periods: those at Zuoxun Gongci and Shiyuan Gongci are shallow and natural; those at Cheng Zhaofu Ancestral Hall are deeper and more rigid; the five-frame beams at Yifeng Yaji have elegant ends; the scrolling grass inside the qi tang at Cheng Lizhong's residence extends half a meter; the middle of eave shuttle beams is carved with phoenixes, bats, painted scrolls, and fan faces.

The liang xia ba (beam chin), or beam bracket, is fairly stylized. Under the eave gallery of the main hall at Yifeng South Courtyard, four groups of three-dimensional carvings depict urban architecture—houses three or four centimeters high with correctly proportioned doors and windows, visible tile lines and spandrels, petal-shaped profile moldings, and deeply carved chrysanthemums on curved undersides.

The shan liang (eel beam), or transfer beam, sits in the upper part of the beam frame, with one end fixed to the column and pulling and supporting the purlin brackets. It is used only in the main hall of ancestral halls, indicating high status. At Shaochang Ancestral Hall it features a dragon spitting water with five-color splash painting; at Cheng Zhaofu Ancestral Hall it is an abstract dragon eye; at Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall it is an oval abstract scroll pattern. The san chou (three-pull), or three-frame beam, is mutually exclusive with the eel beam: Shaochang's central bay uses抬梁 (beam-lifting) with three-pull, while the side bays use穿斗 (column-and-tie) with eel beams.

Load-bearing members such as mi liang (rice beam), purlins, and eave projection beams are generally not carved, but the undersides of exposed beam frames in ancestral halls become carving surfaces. Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall's rice beam has shallow abstract geometric carving; Cheng Zhaofu Ancestral Hall's gold purlin tie and eave projection beam feature relief flowers, phoenixes, cranes, birds, fish patterns, and lions playing with balls; Shaochang Ancestral Hall's upper gold purlin tie has colored interlocking flowers, its front lower gold purlin tie and eave projection beam have openwork flowers, and its eave tie has exquisite openwork fish and bird-and-flower patterns. Purlins are sometimes made square with two or three layers of boards for openwork carving.

Other carved components include spandrels (que ti), stacked brackets (die gong), inserted leaves (cha ye), beam blocks (liang dou), beam block pads (liang dou dian), and column blocks (zhu dou), with motifs of scrolling grass, flowers, meander patterns, and auspicious characters. Shiyuan Gongci has scrolling-grass stacked brackets with inserted leaves; Shaochang Ancestral Hall has flower-shaped beam blocks with scroll-shaped pads, basin-shaped beam blocks with lotus-shaped pads, bird-carved stacked brackets with painted beam blocks and longevity-character pads; Yifeng Yaji has meander-pattern spandrels; Shaochang Ancestral Hall's rear hall eave gallery has flower-bird-animal spandrels and its main hall has pavilion-and-terrace spandrels; Cheng Lizhong's residence has scrolling-grass spandrels.

小木作装修
Minor Carpentry and Joinery
建筑的雕饰与装修 | Architectural Ornamentation and Decoration

要点 Highlights

  • 住宅正房明间六扇通高可摘除门扇，每开间分三部分
  • 早期窗子上下两屉，下屉固定窗视线高度，上屉两扇平推窗
  • 19世纪中期以后窗饰演变，题材从抽象几何变为吉祥寓意具象图案
  • 早期二层栏板不做雕饰，清末民初机械螺纹栏杆成为时尚
  • 新仁昌旅店和程铿别墅有精美的手工雕花栏板

住宅正房明间通常设六扇通高可摘除的门扇。每开间分为三部分：中间主窗，两侧高窗。

义丰雅记等早期建筑的窗子分为上下两屉。下屉为固定窗，位于视线高度；上屉为两扇平推窗，沿水平、垂直、水平依次分格，形成小、大、小的节奏。早期窗花以抽象几何回纹为主。

19世纪中期以后建造的住宅，上屉平推窗的尺寸在两个方向上都有所减小，三边余下的部分和下屉窗一起组成一个大的窗套。平推窗本身只沿水平方向做一次划分，窗套的上半部分会再做两次到三次格，成为固定的雕花小窗。从雕饰题材来看，含有吉祥寓意的鸟兽、花草、字画，甚至亭台楼阁等具象图案成为雕刻的主题和重点。这些图案通常位于每个分格的中间部位，四周衬以抽象纹饰。下屉固定窗面积较大，通常还会嵌上三组这样的图案。用做背景的回纹也变得更为精致、细腻、富有层次，但同时却少了过去的那种朴素之美，略显矫揉造作。

老街上的早期建筑，可能由于二层原本并不住人，所以二层栏板不做任何雕饰，只是把十多厘米宽的木板竖向排列在栏板的框架之间。清末民初，由于车床的引进，带螺纹的木质栏杆成为一种时尚。对于大户有钱人家，这种千篇一律的机械式加工显然有失水准，所以便出现了手工雕刻的栏板。

老街上有两幢中西合璧式木构单体别墅——新仁昌旅店和程铿别墅，每一幢都有精美的雕花栏板。这一方面可能是受到了西方建筑装饰的影响，更重要的是这两幢建筑的居住空间都不仅仅局限在一层，所以高层空间也就自然而然地产生了装饰装修的需求。

新仁昌旅店原为四层高单体建筑，现仅剩两层；建筑三开间，雕花栏板位于二层前廊。明间栏板分成三段，每段内有雕花栏板三块，每块约二十五厘米宽、七十厘米高，两块之间的间隔比栏板宽度略小；次间有同样大小的雕花栏板各十块，均匀排列。雕刻主题大部分以吉祥鸟兽为主，并配以不同的花草，例如狮子、凤凰、仙鹤、鸳鸯、喜鹊、雄鸡等。其中明间正中的一组分别是凤凰、狮子、凤凰，并且两凤凰面面相对。明间栏板的边框处理还和次间略有不同，两侧做竹节状线脚，并且相互间高低错落有致。这二十九块雕花栏板之中，有三块边框的处理手法不同，而且雕的都是姿态相同的白菜，推断是上层的栏板拆下来补缺的。余下的二十六块虽然有的采用相同的主题，但是个个造型独特。

程铿别墅高两层，宽三开间，进深两开间，二层四面有廊。每开间栏板分三组，每组三块；走廊进深这一架也有栏板三块。所有栏板都以回纹为底，镂空雕饰，栏板的宽度根据其所在的位置不同稍有变化，宽度或位置一样的栏板采用相同的回纹样式。稍宽的栏板在回纹中间嵌雕有宝瓶、仙鹤、月季等图案。

— —

The central bay of a residence's main hall typically has six full-height removable door leaves. Each bay is divided into three parts: a central main window and high windows on both sides.

In early buildings such as Yifeng Yaji, windows are divided into upper and lower tiers. The lower tier is a fixed window at eye level; the upper tier consists of two sliding casement windows, divided horizontally, vertically, and horizontally in sequence, creating a small-large-small rhythm. Early window lattice patterns are dominated by abstract geometric meander patterns.

In residences built after the mid-19th century, the upper sliding casement windows were reduced in both dimensions, and the remaining area on three sides, together with the lower tier window, forms a large window surround. The sliding casement itself is divided only once horizontally; the upper half of the window surround is further divided two or three times into fixed carved small windows. In terms of carving motifs, auspicious birds and animals, flowers and plants, calligraphy and painting, and even pavilions and terraces became the dominant themes. These motifs usually occupy the center of each division, surrounded by abstract ornamentation. The lower fixed window is larger and typically embeds three groups of such patterns. The meander pattern used as background became more refined, delicate, and layered, but at the same time lost some of its former simplicity, appearing somewhat contrived.

In early buildings on the old street, possibly because the second floor was not originally used for habitation, second-floor railings had no carving—only vertical boards ten or more centimeters wide arranged within the railing frame. In the late Qing and early Republican period, with the introduction of lathes, threaded wooden railings became fashionable. For wealthy families, this monotonous mechanical processing was clearly substandard, so hand-carved railings emerged.

There are two Western-Chinese hybrid wooden standalone villas on the old street—Xinrenchang Inn and Cheng Keng Villa—each with exquisite carved railings. This may partly reflect the influence of Western architectural decoration, but more importantly, the living space in both buildings extends beyond the ground floor, naturally creating a demand for decoration on upper levels.

Xinrenchang Inn was originally a four-story standalone building, now only two stories remain. It is three bays wide, with carved railings on the second-floor front gallery. The central bay railing is divided into three sections, each containing three carved railing panels about 25 cm wide and 70 cm high, with gaps slightly narrower than the panels; each side bay has ten equally sized carved railing panels evenly arranged. Most carving themes feature auspicious birds and animals paired with various flowers and plants, such as lions, phoenixes, cranes, mandarin ducks, magpies, and roosters. The central group in the middle bay depicts phoenix, lion, phoenix, with the two phoenixes facing each other. The frame treatment of central bay railings differs slightly from side bays, with bamboo-joint moldings on both sides and staggered heights. Among these 29 carved railing panels, three have different frame treatments and all depict cabbage in the same posture, presumably taken from upper floors to fill gaps. The remaining 26 panels, though some share themes, are each uniquely shaped.

Cheng Keng Villa is two stories high, three bays wide, and two bays deep, with galleries on all four sides of the second floor. Each bay's railing is divided into three groups of three panels; the corridor depth bay also has three railing panels. All railings use meander pattern as background with openwork carving; panel widths vary slightly by position, and panels of the same width or position use identical meander patterns. Wider panels embed carved treasure vases, cranes, and Chinese roses within the meander pattern.

砖石雕
Brick and Stone Carving
建筑的雕饰与装修 | Architectural Ornamentation and Decoration

要点 Highlights

  • 石雕很少，主要用在柱础上，除祠堂正堂中间覆盆外皆为鼓形
  • 庭院角落石板雕阴阳相间铜钱纹样，引导雨水流入方岩溪
  • 砖雕主要应用在马头墙、旁门和小窗眉檐上
  • 眉檐做法：砖牙子、仿木椽头、挑砖放瓦，高级的可多层
  • 得耕居是老街唯一立面上饰有大量砖雕石雕的建筑

老街上建筑中的石雕很少，主要用在柱础上。这里的柱础形制比较简单，除了祠堂正堂中间的几个柱础有覆盆之外，其余的皆为鼓形柱础。柱础一般不做雕饰，最多只在上下各做一道线脚。

另一处用石雕的地方则在庭院的角落处。以程成昌旅店为例，明堂四角各有一个一尺见方的石板，中间雕成阴阳相间的铜钱纹样，这样雨水就可以从这里流到院落下面的雨水道，再汇入老街两侧的雨水沟，最终流入方岩溪。

砖雕主要应用在马头墙上，还有建筑正立面旁门上，或是小窗上的眉檐。眉檐的做法通常是下面有几排砖牙子，然后是仿木构的椽头，上面再加一两皮挑砖，砖上放瓦。高级一点的眉檐可以多砌几排砖牙子，也可以做两层椽头相叠。程振兴四合院入口上的眉檐，不仅挑了六层砖牙子，而且中间还外加收分，整个眉檐还用红、白、黑三色相间描画。

老街上唯一一幢立面上饰有大量砖雕或石雕的建筑是得耕居。得耕居的建造年代较早，采用牌楼式门楼。中间的重檐牌楼为砖砌仿木结构，上面不但用砖雕砌出梁、柱、枋、柱头、柱础、椽头等构件，而且还用砖雕模拟雀替和柱头上的雕饰，甚至是梁枋上的彩画，靠近檐口的部分砖雕还残留有彩绘的印记。砖雕的主题既有一些抽象的花纹，又包括一些精美的鱼纹和鸟兽。最精美的雕饰要属门额上方的双狮抢球石雕，门额正中砖刻得耕居三字，并在两侧辅以石雕麒麟，下方衬以砖雕花鸟。得耕居立面檐口下还有砖雕鱼跃龙门。

— —

Stone carving is rare in old street buildings, used mainly on column bases. The column bases here are relatively simple in form: except for several inverted-bowl (fu pen) bases in the center of ancestral hall main halls, all are drum-shaped. Column bases are generally not carved, with at most one molding line at top and bottom.

Another location for stone carving is the corners of courtyards. At Cheng Chengchang Inn, for example, each of the four corners of the ming tang (central courtyard) has a one-chi-square stone slab carved with an interlocking yin-yang copper coin pattern, allowing rainwater to flow into the underground drainage channel beneath the courtyard, then into the rain gutters on both sides of the old street, and finally into Fangyan Stream.

Brick carving is mainly applied to horse-head walls, side doors on building front facades, and the eyebrow eaves above small windows. The typical eyebrow eave construction has several rows of brick dentils below, followed by imitation wooden rafter heads, then one or two courses of projecting bricks with tiles on top. More elaborate eyebrow eaves can have more rows of brick dentils or two layers of overlapping rafter heads. The eyebrow eave above the entrance of Cheng Zhenxing's courtyard house projects six rows of brick dentils, has an additional taper in the middle, and is painted in alternating red, white, and black.

The only building on the old street with extensive brick or stone carving on its facade is Degeng Ju. Built early, it uses a pailou-style gatehouse. The central double-eave pailou is a brick imitation of wooden structure, with brick carving not only forming beams, columns, tie beams, column caps, column bases, and rafter heads, but also simulating spandrels and column cap carvings, even beam paintings; brick carving near the eave still retains traces of polychrome. Brick carving themes include both abstract patterns and exquisite fish and bird-animal motifs. The finest carving is the twin-lions-playing-with-a-ball stone carving above the lintel; the center of the lintel is brick-carved with the three characters Degeng Ju, flanked by stone qilin, with brick-carved flowers and birds below. Under the eave of Degeng Ju's facade is also a brick-carved carp-leaping-over-the-dragon-gate motif.

彩画
Polychrome Painting
建筑的雕饰与装修 | Architectural Ornamentation and Decoration

要点 Highlights

  • 彩画通常只位于砖墙上端靠近檐口的位置，形成一尺左右装饰带
  • 程振昌旅店照壁上的挂落式彩画是老街保留最完整的
  • 彩画分三开间，中间绘垂莲柱、雀替等建筑构件
  • 主题包括田园茅舍、鸟兽花草等
  • 色彩以黑白为主，辅以少量靛青和赭石

彩画通常只位于砖墙上端靠近檐口的位置，并随檐口高低起伏，在青砖墙上形成了一个一尺左右的装饰带。由于风雨的侵蚀，能完整保留至今的彩画很少。

老街上保留最完整的彩画要属程振昌旅店里照壁上的挂落式彩画。这套彩画分三开间，中间绘有垂莲柱、雀替等建筑构件，每一开间分成有韵律的一些片段，每段独自为题。彩画的主题包括田园茅舍、鸟兽花草等。色彩以黑白为主，辅以少量的靛青和赭石。

程振兴外墙檐口下也有黑白卷草纹饰。得耕居立面上靠近檐口的部分砖雕还残留有彩绘的印记。

— —

Polychrome painting is usually located only at the top of brick walls near the eave, following the eave's undulations and forming a decorative band about one chi wide on the gray brick wall. Due to weathering, few paintings survive intact.

The best-preserved polychrome painting on the old street is the hanging-valance style painting on the screen wall inside Cheng Zhenchang Inn. This painting is divided into three bays, with architectural elements such as hanging lotus columns and spandrels painted in the center; each bay is divided into rhythmic segments, each with its own theme. Painting themes include rural thatched cottages, birds, animals, flowers, and plants. Colors are mainly black and white, supplemented by small amounts of indigo and ochre.

Black-and-white scrolling grass patterns also exist under the eave of Cheng Zhenxing's exterior wall. Brick carving near the eave on Degeng Ju's facade still retains traces of polychrome painting.

第八章 文化和传统
Chapter 8: Culture and Traditions
胡公庙会
Hu Gong Temple Fair
文化和传统 | Culture and Traditions

要点 Highlights

  • 阴历八九月农闲季节，八月十三胡公生日，庙会长达一个多月
  • 重头戏是打罗汉，也称迎案或胜案，以村为单位组织罗汉班
  • 打罗汉来历可追溯至正德禅师习武，与武举制度、戚继光鸳鸯阵相关
  • 庙会集宗教、文化、娱乐、祭祖为一体，影响范围扩大到相邻郡县
  • 库川胡氏家族介入，签订《方岩祭礼约》，进一步扩大庙会规模

阴历八九月，刚好是永康一带的农民忙完夏收夏种，而秋收冬种还未开始的农闲季节。恰巧八月十三又是方岩山顶广慈寺护法神胡则的生日。虽然广慈寺里的和尚，还有老街上的许多人都认为胡则的生日是阴历二月二十五，而八月十三这一天仅是胡公检阅罗汉班，抑或是被神化的日子，但是这个小小的分歧并不影响在八九月间方岩山上长达一个多月的胡公庙会。

胡公庙会的重头戏就是打罗汉，也称迎案或者胜案。关于迎案和胜案的区别，程崇舜老人解释，迎案是指具体的行动，胜案则是对整个过程的统称。打罗汉现在演化成以村为单位组织起来的罗汉班，以模仿古代军队出征的形式，按旗、棍、刀、叉等顺序排阵游行，并表演各种武术。

关于胡公庙会以及打罗汉的来历，允明禅师说，开山祖师正德禅师在的时候就有和尚习武练功，这便是打罗汉的由来。胡则在方岩的时候也提倡百姓练武强身。不仅如此，广慈寺最初还出资赞助村里的打罗汉活动。当地人则说村里打罗汉是从明朝末年开始的。武举制度经唐朝的初创时期，到宋朝便日趋完善。南宋时期，武举制度更是演变成与进士科举制度相当的一种全国性的系统考试。婺州地区历史上先后出过两位武状元，其中之一就是在宋光宗绍熙元年（1190年）武科状元东阳人厉仲方。到了明末，浙江地区经常受到倭寇骚扰，习武健身便更具有保家卫国的切身目的。明末抗倭名将戚继光于嘉靖三十八年（1559年）在义乌成立了以农民和矿工为主的戚家军。戚继光独创的鸳鸯阵和打罗汉还有异曲同工的意味，鸳鸯阵是以十二人为一队，并配以盾、枪、叉、钯、棍、刀等长短兵器，因敌因地变换队形，灵活作战。

到了清朝，从县志中可以看出胡公庙会已经颇具规模。康熙三十七年（1698年）《永康县志》记载：八月十三日，佑顺侯胡公则生辰，各分村落为会，挂大帛为旗，长二三丈，导以鼓乐，从以伞盖，或以纸为马，登方岩。赛神而还。盖一郡香火之盛。未有若此比者。

胡公庙会在当时就已经超越了以简单的祭祀或是习武健身为目的的一种形式，而是成为一个集宗教、文化和娱乐为一体的赛神会。庙会的影响范围不仅扩大到了相邻的郡县，其过程更是累月不休。

作为建筑和空间的载体，广慈寺并赫灵庙一方面可以依靠与日俱增的香火钱来更好地维护并扩大其自身的建筑和领地；另一方面，修缮一新的大殿和金光灿灿的胡公大帝也能吸引更多远道而来的朝圣者。据《库川胡氏家谱》记载，从顺治十一年（1654年）到道光二十七年（1847年）的近二百年里，奉胡则为伯祖的库川胡氏后人，先后多次出资帮助广慈寺修理或重建前厅和大殿，有记载的就有七次。在嘉庆十二年（1807年）八月，库川胡氏又与广慈寺签订了《方岩祭礼约》，规定了每年正月、八月在方岩祭祀时的一些细节。胡氏家族的介入在客观上进一步扩大了胡公庙会的影响和规模，同时使庙会的性质，在兼有习武健身、宗教、文化和娱乐等功能之后，又加上了祭祖的目的。

胡公庙会的准备工作一般从阴历七月就开始了，有的村庄甚至过了正月十五就开始商议筹备。参加筹备的人多是村里德高望重的老人，过去以族长或是祠堂常会为主。资金的筹集总是第一步。有了钱，就可以请拳师来训练罗汉班。罗汉班通常是一直练到上方岩的前一两天。最后就是准备包括头旗、锣鼓、刀棍、铲子、宝剑、高跷等各式行头道具。由于筹备庙会耗时耗资，所以许多人少力薄的村落并不会年年迎案。但是在迎案的时间上却有一条不成文的规矩，那就是一旦你选择迎案，就要连续迎三年或是三年的倍数。

庙会期间，除了迎案之外的另一个重要的内容就是换香火。参加庙会的村落，都会有一个用精良木材雕刻的胡公神龛、牌位或龙庭，平时供奉在祠堂或本保庙里。到了约定上方岩的日期，迎案的队伍就要背着胡公的牌位上方岩，从胡公殿前的香炉里，掏一把滚烫的香灰放进背着的胡公牌位前的小香炉里，这就叫换香火。

胡公庙会正式开始是在八月初一，以胡则出生地胡库村打罗汉上方岩作为开端，其他村会在接下来的一个半月里，按照事先约定好的日期陆陆续续上方岩。从迎案的时间安排就可以看出，胡公庙会到八月十三日会有一个高潮，而重阳初八夜则又是一个小高潮。当地有八月十三，七十二个胡公上方岩的说法。

具体到迎案当天的一些细节，村村有异，但大体程序基本相同。在约定好上方岩的前一天晚上，所有参与的人员要沐浴洁身。到了第二天凌晨，要在练武场上向供在这里的小胡公神位举行祭叉仪式。以后塘巷村为例，迎案的队伍从前至后依次是举鹅毛旗四人、令字旗一人、抬香案两人、持锣四人、举执事牌四人、挑花烛四人、挑香十人、持开道刀铲两人、举万岁牌一人、背胡公神座一人、掌扇一人、举伞一人及降神童一人，随后才是罗汉班和吹唱班。罗汉班的人数少则四五十人，多则近百。这一队人马浩浩荡荡地来到了方岩山顶上的胡公大殿前。在三响铁铳之后，从村里背上来的胡公神座被放在了大殿前，罗汉们于是在喧闹的锣鼓声中绕大殿顺向跳三圈、再逆向跳三圈，这叫跳罗汉。与此同时，降神童会在大殿前的香炉里捧一把香灰放进背来胡公神座前的小香炉里，完成换香火的仪式。这些都进行完毕，迎案的队伍便原路返回本村，这叫打回头案。

— —

The lunar eighth and ninth months fall in the agricultural slack season after summer harvest and planting and before autumn harvest and winter planting. Coincidentally, the 13th day of the eighth month is the birthday of Hu Ze, the protective deity of Guangci Temple on Fangyan Mountain. Although the monks of Guangci Temple and many on the old street believe Hu Ze's birthday is actually the 25th day of the second lunar month, and that the 13th of the eighth month is merely the day Hu Gong reviews the arhat troupes or is deified, this minor disagreement does not affect the month-long Hu Gong Temple Fair on Fangyan Mountain during the eighth and ninth months.

The centerpiece of the fair is da luohan (beating the arhat), also called ying an (welcoming the case) or sheng an (victorious case). According to Elder Cheng Chongshun, ying an refers to the specific action, while sheng an is the general term for the whole process. Da luohan has evolved into village-based arhat troupes that parade in formation imitating ancient armies, in the order of banners, staffs, swords, and forks, performing various martial arts.

Regarding the origins, Monk Yunming says that when the founding monk Zhengde was present, monks already practiced martial arts, which is the origin of da luohan. Hu Ze also advocated martial training for the people when he was at Fangyan. Guangci Temple initially sponsored village da luohan activities. Locals say village da luohan began in the late Ming dynasty. The military examination system, founded in the Tang, became increasingly sophisticated in the Song; in the Southern Song it evolved into a nationwide system equivalent to the civil service examination. Wuzhou produced two military zhuangyuan, one being Li Zhongfang of Dongyang in 1190. In the late Ming, Zhejiang suffered frequent wokou raids, making martial training more directly tied to defending home and country. Qi Jiguang founded the Qi Family Army in Yiwu in 1559, and his unique yuanyang zhen (mandarin duck formation) bears similarities to da luohan—teams of twelve with mixed long and short weapons, adapting formation flexibly.

By the Qing dynasty, the fair had grown to considerable scale. The 1698 Yongkang County Gazetteer records: On the 13th of the eighth month, the birthday of Lord You Shun Hu Ze, each village forms an association, hanging large silk banners two or three zhang long, led by drums and music, followed by umbrellas and canopies, sometimes using paper horses, climbing Fangyan Mountain. They return after racing the deity. The prosperity of incense in the entire prefecture has no equal.

The fair had already transcended simple sacrifice or martial training to become a gathering integrating religion, culture, and entertainment. Its influence extended to neighboring counties, and the process lasted for months without cease.

As architectural and spatial carriers, Guangci Temple and Heling Temple could rely on growing incense money to maintain and expand their buildings and territory; conversely, renovated halls and the gilded Hu Gong Dadi attracted more pilgrims. According to the Kuchuan Hu Family Genealogy, from 1654 to 1847, the Kuchuan Hu descendants, who revere Hu Ze as their grand-uncle, funded repairs or reconstruction of Guangci Temple's front hall and main hall at least seven times. In 1807 they signed the Fangyan Sacrificial Ritual Agreement with the temple. The Hu family's involvement further expanded the fair's scale, adding ancestor worship to its functions of martial training, religion, culture, and entertainment.

Preparation typically begins in the seventh lunar month; some villages start discussions as early as after the 15th of the first month. Respected elders—formerly clan heads or ancestral hall council members—lead the process. Fundraising is the first step. Martial arts trainers are hired to train the arhat troupes, which practice until a day or two before climbing the mountain. Props include head banners, gongs and drums, swords and staffs, shovels, swords, and stilts. Because preparation is costly and time-consuming, small villages do not hold ying an every year. However, an unwritten rule holds that once a village chooses ying an, it must continue for three years or a multiple of three.

Another important activity is huan xiang huo (exchanging incense fire). Each participating village has a finely carved Hu Gong shrine, tablet, or dragon throne, normally enshrined in the ancestral hall or local protective temple. On the appointed day, the procession carries the tablet up the mountain, takes a handful of hot ash from the incense burner before Hu Gong Hall, and places it in the small burner before the carried tablet—this is exchanging incense fire.

The fair officially begins on the first day of the eighth month, with Hu Ze's birth village Huku leading the first da luohan up the mountain. Other villages follow on prearranged dates over the next month and a half. The 13th of the eighth month is a peak, and the eighth night of the ninth month (Chongyang) is a secondary peak. A local saying holds that on the 13th of the eighth month, seventy-two Hu Gongs climb Fangyan.

Details vary by village, but the general procedure is similar. The night before, all participants bathe and purify themselves. Before dawn, a cha (fork) sacrifice ceremony is held before the small Hu Gong tablet at the training ground. Taking Houtangxiang Village as an example, the procession order is: four goose-feather banner bearers, one command banner bearer, two carrying the incense table, four gong bearers, four ceremonial plaque bearers, four flower-candle carriers, ten incense carriers, two path-clearing sword-and-shovel bearers, one long-life banner bearer, one carrying the Hu Gong throne, one fan bearer, one umbrella bearer, one descending prodigy, followed by the arhat troupe and singing troupe. The arhat troupe numbers forty to nearly one hundred. The procession reaches Hu Gong Hall, and after three iron cannon salutes, the carried Hu Gong throne is placed before the hall. The arhats then dance three circles clockwise and three counterclockwise around the hall amid gongs and drums—this is tiao luohan (dancing the arhat). Meanwhile, the descending prodigy takes incense ash to complete the exchange. The procession then returns to the village—da hui tou an (beating the return case).

正月
The First Lunar Month
文化和传统 | Culture and Traditions

要点 Highlights

  • 过年以祭灶揭开序幕，二十四日送灶君，供黏糖
  • 腊月二十五年头禁，赤豆米煮蚕花粥，洒扫沐浴
  • 谢年以家庭为单位，猪头鹅鸡鱼豆腐酒饭等丰盛供品
  • 除夕坐岁，新年吃鸡蛋素面、羹、粽子（先耕后种）
  • 正月十四迎龙灯，板桥灯由龙头龙尾和几十桥灯组成

老街上的人家过年，是以祭灶揭开序幕的。在江浙地区，关于祭灶的时间有官三民四船家五的说法，这也就是为什么在《永康县志》风俗篇里的记载为十二月二十三日祭灶，而当地人却在二十四日送灶君。在这天傍晚，家家户户都会在灶台前祭拜灶君，并供上黏糖，以保证灶君在玉皇大帝面前只讲好话，不讲坏话，以便带来年的好运气。

送走了灶王爷，就到了年头，又叫年头禁。在腊月二十五这一天，是日不出财。以赤豆和米煮粥，曰蚕花粥云；不仅如此，还要洒扫沐浴。过了二十五以后，每家要选一个好日子来谢年，又叫送年。谢年是一个以家庭为单位的祭神活动，通常在自家的厅堂里举行，还要准备猪头、鹅、鸡、鱼、豆腐、酒、饭等丰盛的供品。谢年由全家的男子参加，在祭拜的同时，大家要相互说一些祝福的话，还要把几天前送走的灶君接回来，请他老人家开始新一年的工作。

除日，即除夕，这天白天要送年桃，符春帖，蒸饭足新年数日之食，晚上在团圆饭之后则要全家一起坐岁。十二点一过，家家户户都要抢着开门放炮，因为开门最先被视为大吉。新的一年到来之后，老街上的大部分人做的第一件事就是：连夜上方岩山拜胡公、求签。一方面是看看自己在这一年的运气；另一方面也希望无所不能的胡公大帝能保佑自己一家平安，生活美满。

新年这天早上，要吃鸡蛋素面，意味着健康长寿，全家团圆。早上还要有羹，即用青菜、豆腐和米粉等煮成汤；中午要吃粽子，意为先耕后种，讨年年有耕，年年有种的吉利。这小小的细节，再次体现了得耕这两个字对老街上程氏后人的深远影响。在早餐过后，就开始一系列的仪式性活动：先是要点香烛，望空而拜，接着就是到祠堂里去拜太公，最后是家里人以尊卑长幼为序互拜。自初二开始的四五天里，则是邻里、亲戚互相拜访。

正月十五是上元节，又称灯节，在这天前后，当地素有迎花烛、迎龙灯的习俗。这些活动一般以村为单位，临近的几个村则会把时间错开。老街所在的这个小山谷里，按规矩十三日迎花烛，十四日岩下村迎龙灯，十五日橙麓村迎龙灯。

关于迎龙灯的习俗，一些民俗学家把它一直追溯到古越人对水神的一种祭祀活动。他们认为龙灯是龙舟上岸后的一种新的形式，古人通过祭龙娱龙来祈求新一年风调雨顺，五谷丰登，人丁兴旺。对于老街上的人，当地话灯与丁同音，所以迎龙灯过程中的许多细节都和人丁兴旺、添丁的美好希冀密不可分。

老街上迎的龙灯属于板桥灯，这种灯的龙头、龙尾各为一节，龙身则是由几十桥，甚至一百多桥灯首尾相接而成，每桥有两盏纱灯。其中龙头和龙尾的做法类似，都是用一块长方形的木板作底，然后用竹篾编成骨架，外裱棉纸；随后上油彩，并辅以龙须、龙珠、龙爪等；最后涂上羊油，这样一方面能显得更为光泽，又能起到防水的作用。支撑龙头用的底板长三四米，宽不到半米，需要四个男青年协力共舞；龙尾则小一些，需要两个人抬。相比起来，龙身的制作则要简单很多。龙身的每一节称作一桥，由一个人迎举。所谓的桥就是一块一米多长的木板，板上两端各有一个篾扎纸糊，四方八角的彩灯，每盏彩灯上再插上一面写有诸如五谷丰登、风调雨顺等字样的三角纸糊彩旗。龙头、龙身、龙尾每一桥灯的底板两端都各有一孔，插上拴灯柱，就可以把上百桥灯接成长龙。

迎龙灯的准备活动通常从腊月就开始了。负责舞龙头和龙尾通常是一种荣耀，这项工作叫捻灯，会优先让给在这一年村里第一个生儿子的人家。由于龙头和龙尾的骨架是要被重复使用的，所以平日就存放在世远公祠厢房的二层。正月初六这天，负责舞龙头和龙尾的人家就要开始着手准备了。

正月十四日早上七点开始，负责捻灯的人家会找两个人，敲着大锣，走遍老街的每一个角落，这叫催灯。下午三点的时候，在得耕居内会有为晚上舞灯的人准备好的饭菜。晚饭后夜幕降临，各家各户开始放鞭炮，并举行点灯仪式。这里的规矩是红灯出，红灯入，家家户户点好的一桥桥的龙灯，从老街上的各个角落出发，纷纷向得耕居前的空场上聚集，开始接灯。得耕居前爆竹声震耳欲聋，陆陆续续到达的部分桥灯依次和龙头接好，在接灯的同时，龙灯已经在空场上转上了三小圈。随即龙灯在举迎牌的、吹喇叭的、敲锣的和领灯人的引导下，走向蜥蜴山前山头上的朱公位。朱公位后有一棵大柏树，龙灯会绕着朱公位和大柏树转几圈，并向朱公磕头。下山的时候还会在八世祖程濂，即松门公墓前拜一下。后两圈比较小，基本上就是绕着空场和澈月塘转。龙灯每次回到得耕居前，就会有更多的桥灯顺次接上，到第三圈结束的时候，龙尾才会最终出现。于是，接好的龙灯在众人的簇拥下和震耳的锣号声中开始游街。

龙灯首先沿老街向北行进，沿街的家家户户都会在门前挂纱灯，放鞭炮，摆好香烛、猪肉、鸡肉等供品迎接龙灯，龙头也会进到家里去磕头讨彩。讨来的钱会归总到一起，再统一发给参与迎龙灯的人家。当龙灯行进到位于下胡坑坑口的经堂前，不能进去打扰菩萨，但是会在外面跳灯，所谓跳灯就是龙头要往前走，龙尾却偏要往后拉，这样来来去去要反复几次，直到大家都精疲力竭了才罢休。随后龙灯会沿着老街一直走到橙麓村北，象鼻岗山脚的程氏总祠。这时龙头会被抬进祠堂摆放在大厅中间，由领香的人代为上香，祭拜程氏先祖。

迎龙灯的队伍在祠堂前稍事休息后会向西进入寿山坑，并沿方岩山北面的山路上山。上山的时候，已经是深夜了，几百桥龙灯蜿蜒近一里，纱灯里的烛火可以照亮整个方岩山。走到天门的时候，广慈寺的住持已经身着袈裟、手持法杖，带领众僧在这里迎候。龙灯被迎到寺院里，这里早就备好茶点、糖果以招待舞灯的人，寺里的和尚还会把没有烧完的蜡烛分给大家。休息片刻，龙灯便再次起程，从前山下山。下山的路十分难走，最陡峭的一段是飞桥，还不到一米宽。随后龙灯再次进村，并分别到世远公祠和绍常祠堂里拜祭，最后龙灯来到绍常祠堂前的空场上。这时天色已经微明，舞灯的人们在这里再做最后一次休息后，以备迎龙灯活动的高潮，即盘龙灯，也称团龙灯。盘龙灯一开始，龙头便以圆形路线向内盘旋，直到龙头被几圈桥灯团团围在中间。这时，龙头要设法逃出重围，而龙尾又要带领几百桥龙身尽力阻拦，这叫翻龙灯。翻龙灯会反反复复进行许多次，这期间舞灯者还会轮换休息，直到大家都筋疲力尽。这时绍常祠堂的管事会出来讲好话，在管事的好言劝说下，龙尾终于让龙头翻出重围。

当龙灯再次回到得耕居前的时候，天已经大亮了。龙灯被一桥桥的拆开，换上新的蜡烛之后，桥灯便在鞭炮声中被村民们纷纷迎回了家。最后，龙头上的珠灯要取下来，送给村里的新婚夫妇，或是想要儿子的人家，这叫送灯。由于送灯暗含着送丁的吉祥祝福，所以收到珠灯的人家要准备好红包。到此为止，长达十几个小时的迎龙灯活动在正月十五日的早上终于结束了。人们在吉祥和喜庆的氛围下，新一年的各项工作便陆陆续续开始了。正如老街上的俗话，灯过年过，牛归牛栏，马归马舍。

— —

New Year celebrations on the old street begin with the kitchen god sacrifice. In the Jiangzhe region, there is a saying officials on the 23rd, commoners on the 24th, boat people on the 25th regarding the timing, which is why the Yongkang County Gazetteer records the 23rd of the 12th month, while locals send off the kitchen god on the 24th. That evening, every household worships the kitchen god before the stove and offers sticky candy, ensuring he speaks only good words before the Jade Emperor.

After sending off the kitchen god comes nian tou (year beginning), also called nian tou jin (year beginning prohibition). On the 25th of the 12th month, no wealth is dispensed; red bean and rice porridge called canhua zhou (silkworm flower porridge) is cooked, and the house is swept and baths taken. After the 25th, each family chooses an auspicious day for xie nian (thanking the year), also called song nian (sending off the year). This family-based deity sacrifice is usually held in the main hall, with abundant offerings including pig head, goose, chicken, fish, tofu, wine, and rice. All men of the family participate, exchanging blessings while welcoming the kitchen god back.

On New Year's Eve, peach branches are sent, spring couplets posted, and enough rice steamed for several days. After the reunion dinner, the whole family sits together for sui (staying up). At midnight, households rush to open doors and set off firecrackers, as the first to open is considered most auspicious. The first thing most people do after the new year arrives is climb Fangyan Mountain overnight to worship Hu Gong and draw divination sticks.

On New Year's morning, egg plain noodles are eaten for health and family reunion; a soup of greens, tofu, and rice flour is also served. At noon, zongzi (sticky rice dumplings) are eaten, meaning first plow then plant, wishing for plowing and planting every year—reflecting the deep influence of the name Degeng (obtaining plowing). After breakfast, incense is lit and prayers offered to the sky, then ancestors are worshipped at the ancestral hall, and family members bow in order of seniority. From the second through the fifth or sixth day, neighbors and relatives visit each other.

The 15th of the first month is the Lantern Festival. Around this time, locals welcome flower candles and dragon lanterns. Activities are village-based, with neighboring villages staggering dates. In this small valley, the 13th is for flower candles, the 14th for Yanxia village dragon lanterns, and the 15th for Chenglu village dragon lanterns.

Folklorists trace dragon lantern customs back to ancient Yue people's water deity sacrifices, seeing dragon lanterns as a land version of dragon boats. Locally, the word for lantern (deng) is homophonous with male child (ding), so many details tie to wishes for prosperous descendants.

The old street's dragon lantern is a banqiao deng (plank bridge lantern): the dragon head and tail are each one section, while the body consists of dozens or even over a hundred qiao (bridge) lanterns connected end to end, each with two gauze lanterns. The head and tail use a rectangular wooden board base, bamboo skeleton, cotton paper covering, oil paint, and dragon whiskers, pearls, and claws; sheep oil is applied for luster and waterproofing. The head board is 3-4 meters long and under half a meter wide, requiring four young men; the tail is smaller, requiring two. Each body section (qiao) is a one-meter-plus board with two paper-covered octagonal lanterns at each end, each topped with a triangular paper flag bearing auspicious phrases. Holes at both ends of each board allow connecting pins to join hundreds of lanterns into a long dragon.

Preparation starts in the 12th month. Dancing the head and tail is an honor called nian deng (twisting the lantern),优先 given to the family that had the first son that year. The reusable head and tail skeletons are stored on the second floor of Shiyuan Gongci's wing. Preparation begins on the sixth of the first month.

At 7 AM on the 14th, two people beat a large gong through every corner of the old street—cui deng (urging the lanterns). At 3 PM, a meal is prepared at Degeng Ju for the evening's dancers. After dark, firecrackers are set off and the lighting ceremony begins. The rule is red lanterns out, red lanterns in. Lit lanterns gather at the open space before Degeng Ju for jie deng (connecting the lanterns). After three small circles, the dragon proceeds to Zhu Gong's position on Lizard Mountain, circling it and a large cypress, then kowtowing. On the way down, it pays respects at the tomb of the 8th ancestor Cheng Lian (Songmen Gong). After more circles, the tail finally appears, and the connected dragon parades through the street.

The dragon heads north along the old street; every household hangs gauze lanterns, sets off firecrackers, and offers incense, pork, and chicken. The dragon head enters homes to kowtow and collect donations. At the Jingtang (prayer hall) at the mouth of Xiahu Pit, the dragon cannot enter to disturb the deity but performs tiao deng (jumping lanterns) outside—the head pulls forward while the tail pulls back, repeated until exhaustion. The dragon then proceeds to the Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall at the foot of Elephant Trunk Hill, where the head is carried in and incense offered.

After resting, the procession enters Shoushan Pit and climbs the north side of Fangyan Mountain. By then it is deep night, with hundreds of lanterns winding nearly a li, their candles illuminating the mountain. At Tianmen (Heaven Gate), the abbot of Guangci Temple in kasaya with staff welcomes the dragon. Tea, snacks, and candies are served, and leftover candles distributed. The dragon descends via the front mountain—the steepest section, Feiqiao (Flying Bridge), is under a meter wide. It returns to the village, worships at Shiyuan Gongci and Shaochang Ancestral Hall, and reaches the open space before Shaochang Ancestral Hall at dawn. After a final rest, the climax begins: pan long deng (coiling the dragon lantern), also called tuan long deng. The head coils inward until surrounded by layers of body lanterns, then attempts to break free while the tail leads the body in resistance—fan long deng (overturning the dragon lantern). This repeats many times with rotating dancers until exhaustion. The Shaochang Ancestral Hall manager speaks kind words, persuading the tail to let the head escape.

When the dragon returns to Degeng Ju, it is broad daylight. The lanterns are disassembled, candles replaced, and each qiao welcomed home amid firecrackers. Finally, the pearl lanterns on the dragon head are removed and given to newlyweds or families wanting sons—song deng (sending the lantern), which implies sending a son. Recipients prepare red envelopes. The ten-plus-hour event ends on the morning of the 15th. As the local saying goes: when the lanterns pass, the year passes; cattle return to their sheds, horses to their stables.

祭祖
Ancestor Worship
文化和传统 | Culture and Traditions

要点 Highlights

  • 一年中三个阴间节日：清明、冬至、中元
  • 清明祭祖最隆重，前三天后四天到祖坟祭扫，添土标青
  • 清明果有红白青三种，用馃印压制，最具特色
  • 冬至以家庭为单位小型祭祖，祭拜前四代祖宗，做羹饭
  • 中元（鬼节）也祭祖，同时祭祀孤魂野鬼，有路头羹饭

对祖先的祭祀活动一直是中国民众社会生活的重要组成部分，同时也是一种最淳朴的宗教意识的体现。人们通过一系列的丧葬仪式来安抚死者的灵魂，使他们能够顺利地进入另一个世界，并在那里安逸舒适的生活。仅仅这样还不够，人们还要借助定期供奉食物、送钱、送衣物的形式和祖先保持联系，祈求祖先保佑整个家族人丁兴旺，财源茂盛，五谷丰登。在一年中众多的节日里，有三个节日被认为是阴间的节日。在这三天里，村里都有或大或小的祭祖活动。

清明：祭祖仪式最隆重的要属清明，即每年的四月初。在清明的前三天、后四天，村里的人要到各房的祖坟上去祭扫，添土和标青，标青即是在坟前挂白纸。祭祀用的食物通常包括猪头、鹅、豆腐、糕、大麦面、金团和清明果等，其中以清明果最有特色。清明果有红、白、青三种，它是用糯米做皮，红糖或白糖做馅的点心。磨好的糯米直接用的话就可以做出白色的清明果；如果加入野菜汁（野蒿），就可以做成青色的；红色的则是加入了食用颜料。和其他地方不同的是，当地的清明果是借用馃印压制成的。金团类似于清明果，只是用豆腐、粉丝、青菜和猪肉做馅。

祭祀的过程是由在场的辈分最大的人主持，主持人通过高喊通和引来指挥其余的人。通是让大家准备好一起做事，而引则是让某个人前去祭拜。整个祭祀的程序大致包括拈香、点烛、跪拜、奠酒、进汤、献饭、读祝文和焚祝文，其中在焚祝文的同时还会打锣、吹号和放鞭炮。祭祀结束之后所有的人会在一起席地围坐吃午餐，又名散清。

岩下老街的程姓人中，如今多数是十五世毅庵公的后人。据民国33年（1944年）《程氏天房谱》记载，毅庵葬四十二都小墟塘龟山墈前墈下，距岩下村35里，现属舟山镇。因为距离远，坐轿子单程要走三四个小时，再加上开销大（雇轿子的费用由个人出一半，祖上出一半），所以每年去的人不多，一般也只有四五十人左右。能去的人多来自地位较高和家境较好的人家，而且妇女和外姓人也可以去。已经年过古稀的程镇康老先生回忆说，他小时候随父亲去过舟山两次祭祖。让他印象最深的就是：经过的村庄都会觉得这四五十顶轿子组成的队伍很是威风。这在客观上也是对岩下程氏的一种宣传，使邻村的姑娘都愿意嫁到这样的望族。程毅庵的夫人被子孙们尊称为太婆。因为她葬在较近的金龙江，每年清明去的人就较多。

村里的一部分人还会到下胡坑里作勳公的坟地去拜天官。作勳公是岩下程氏第十三世上的人，岩上村的作勳公祠也是以他命名的。他之所以受到如此尊敬，是因为他性情敦朴，节俭持躬，铢积寸累以成家业。而资雄里闬，每遇岁歉必出粟赈饥，凡有求者缓急靡不相济。人皆德之。

在清明那天，村里的几个祠堂会打开祠堂门，挂起祖宗像，摆上猪头、鹅、豆腐等供品，等待子孙们的祭拜。比起那些有钱有地位的人，村里那些穷苦的程氏后人就只能在清明那天到祠堂里去拜拜祖宗，期待着可以分到的那点供品。现住在作勳公祠里，74岁的程茂发老人讲：他在十六岁以后就可以去祠堂祭祖，并且他有三个祠堂可以去。他可以从村里人人都有份的世远公祠和一部分人有份的作勳公祠里各领到一斤饭、两片肉和两片豆腐；他还能从本房的，一个在几家人中轮换管理的祠堂里领到一点钱。

1995年，永康程氏第一到第四世祖的墓被统一移到了离村子三里的北政岭。自此，遍布永康地区十七个村的程氏后人开始在清明期间进行联合祭祖。祭祖的活动由十七个村庄的程氏轮流主持，通常情况下是由村里老年协会的主席带头组织。

冬至：冬至是另外一个祭祀祖先的重要时间。不同于清明时期的宗族集体祭祖，冬至主要是以家庭为单位进行小型的祭祖活动，祭拜的对象通常也仅限于自家前四代的祖宗。在清光绪编《永康县志》中提道旧家大族四时祭于祖庙，而冬至祭最重。或许是因为冬至祭最重，所以住在岩下老街的程氏后人在四时之中只保留了冬至祭祖的习俗。当地人称冬至为下世人过年。在岩下老街和橙麓村里，关于冬至祭祖的时间有两种说法：一种说法是在冬至的当天祭祖；另一种说法则认为要在冬至的前三天或后四天中的一天中祭祖。但是他们祭祖的形式都是相似的。每一家都要做羹饭，在厅堂里摆供品以招待先祖。据程崇舜老人讲，冬至祭祖的羹饭要比其他日子里的多一些点心。

祭祀过程多在午饭之前，有的人家还会放鞭炮。岩下街79号的程镇康家每年都在冬至这天祭祖，2008年冬至这天他家的供桌上摆了猪肉、煎豆腐、鸡蛋、自家种的橘子和用糯米做皮，豆腐、粉丝、猪肉和青菜做馅的团子。这些供品放在桌子中间，旁边整整齐齐的摆放了九副碗筷和酒杯。在靠近明堂一侧的桌子上有两个烛台和两束各三支的香。在一切都准备好以后，程家的女主人，即程镇康的夫人，拿着一束香到院门口拜三拜请祖宗们回家。如果这时外面正好有人的话，大家都会自觉地让开门口。等祖宗们落座之后，程家的儿子要给每个祖宗敬三次酒，酒是用自家酿的上好黄酒。随后，家里的人开始在院子里一起给祖宗烧些纸钱和纸银锭。最后，程家的女主人要拿着另一束香，走到门口再拜三拜送祖宗们回去，然后关上院门。在整个祭祀过程结束之后，供桌上的部分美味就成了当日午餐的一部分。

中元：第三个祭祖的日子是在阴历七月十五的中元节，也称鬼节。在这一天里，家家户户也要做羹饭来盛筵祖先。但是不同于另外两个节日的是，在这一天，短命的和没有后代的孤魂野鬼也可以享受到祭祀。正如县志里说：中元，荐祖考、祀孤。所以这里有些善良的人家会在祭祖之后，用篮子带上一些供品，到村庄外的路口或者空地上进行野祭，俗称路头羹饭。更有富裕的人家会结队去寺院或是祠堂去拜星，以超度饿鬼和死囚。这一切都是为了能让自己的祖先安心地享受儿孙们孝敬的香烛、食物和钱财，而不会被那些受不到祭祀的孤魂野鬼骚扰。

— —
